  • P4-7One-command host integration (the Docker Desktop pattern): ship the kelyfos CLI for macOS/Windows/Linux, where on macOS/Windows it transparently provisions, starts, and stops its own Linux layer (Lima / WSL2) — kelyfos doctor creates it if missing, and users never touch the VM by hand. Same commands, same kelyfos.toml, same images everywhere. Backlog experiment behind it: a native macOS backend via libkrun on Apple's hypervisor, replacing the VM layer entirely. [PROMOTED 2026-08-24 → Phase 6, P6-12, scoped to macOS on the owner’s ruling; Windows/WSL2 and the libkrun experiment are post-1.0 by name (D35).] [AMENDED 2026-08-24, D35: “same commands, same kelyfos.toml, same images everywhere” is not what v1.0 delivers and is retired from the 1.0 copy. It needs every command proxied across limactl shell, and an interrupt does not cross it — which orphans a microVM and discards the workspace its teardown was syncing back. macOS means “KelyfOS manages the Linux layer”.]

Exit checkpoint: none — this phase is a direction, not a deadline. Dispositioned 2026-08-24 (D35) and parked: every row above now has a verdict, and none of them will ever be ticked here — P4-1 and P4-2 are the history of work done in Phase 5, P4-3 and P4-7 of work done in Phase 6, P4-6 is closed with its residual parked, and P4-4 and P4-5 are blocked on conditions this project cannot manufacture. The dashboard keeps counting all seven rather than excluding them, because a denominator adjusted to flatter the numerator is the same defect as a ticked box with unmet conditions. v1.0 does not mean 100%.

config KELYFOS_PHASE_5

Phase 5 — Hardening (v0.9 · "and now it is hardened")

DONE

Goal: make the README’s one remaining apology unnecessary. Every release so far has carried the sentence “not hardened yet”, and it has been true: KelyfOS relies on the hardware boundary Firecracker gives it and adds nothing of its own around the VMM process or around what a compromised agent may reach inside its guest. This phase adds both layers and then replaces that sentence with one that is also true — stating what is now enforced and what is still out of scope, with docs/threat-model.md saying the same thing in more words.
Promoted from the P4 backlog on the product owner’s ruling of 2026-08-23 (D27): exactly P4-1 and P4-2, no more. PLAN-FEATURES.html is complete and closed; this document governs from here.

  • P5-0The hardening spec, written before the code. docs/hardening.md: what a compromised agent can reach today, what each layer takes away, and — the longer half — what remains reachable afterwards. Names the threat each mechanism answers and the ones it does not, so the README sentence this phase replaces has something exact to be measured against rather than a feeling. Reviewed before P5-1 starts, the way docs/qol.md was.
  • P5-1Firecracker under the jailer (P4-1): chroot, its own cgroup, dropped uid/gid, and the device nodes it needs and nothing else. Every path that starts a VM goes through it — run, team up, fork, snapshot restore, serve-mcp, shim — or none does, because a hardening that half the entry points skip is a hardening nobody can reason about. The vsock socket, the images and the workspace device all move inside the chroot; the flight recorder and the named-session store stay outside it, because a record that the jailed process can reach is not a record. Existing cgroup limits (E1) keep working, on the jailer’s hierarchy rather than beside it.
  • P5-2The host-side syscall filter, stated rather than assumed. Firecracker ships its own seccomp filter; this task establishes which filter is in force in a KelyfOS run, proves it is on rather than trusting a default, and records what it permits. If a custom filter is warranted, it is built from Firecracker’s published one rather than written from scratch — a hand-rolled syscall allowlist for somebody else’s binary is a way to produce a crash that looks like a security feature.
  • P5-6The cgroup and the jail, composed rather than each assuming the other. [ADDED 2026-08-24, D30.] Found by P5-2 on the machine the quickstart describes: kelyfos run --cpu-quota is refused whenever the run is jailed on a host whose cgroup path resolves to the systemd user manager. The jailed path builds its command line through the jailer and never calls WrapArgv, so no scope is created; --parent-cgroup is passed only in direct mode, so nothing places the VMM either; Confirm then reads /proc and correctly refuses a quota that was never applied. Fail-closed rather than silent, which is the design working — but E1’s caps have been unusable by default since P5-1, and this phase’s own acceptance asks that the cgroup a VMM sits in be the one KelyfOS asked for. Lands before P5-4, with the check that proves it added to dev/accept-jail.sh rather than left as a claim.
  • P5-3Guest hardening: what the supervisor grants what it spawns (P4-2). Per-flavor restrictions on every process the supervisor starts — exec, plugins, the shell — so a compromised agent inside a guest is confined further than the VM boundary alone confines it. Landlock for the filesystem where the guest kernel supports the ABI version required, seccomp for the syscall surface, both declared per flavor and both refusing to start rather than silently degrading if the kernel cannot apply them. /work stays writable, the read-only root stays read-only, and the supervisor’s own vsock channels stay reachable: a profile that breaks the toolbox has hardened nothing.
  • P5-7A restored machine’s confinement, in the record and in the terminal. [ADDED 2026-08-24, D32.] A snapshot taken before v0.9 restores into the guest it captured, which has no profile — and the restore path never learns the posture, because a restore gets no ready frame. So the host asks the restored machine over the control channel and session.start carries the answer, on every entry point that opens a chain, so a transcript can never make an unconfined-guest run look confined. Restoring a pre-confinement snapshot warns in the terminal, every time, with the fix that actually fixes it: re-create the snapshot under v0.9. Refusal is deliberately not required — the host walls are unchanged and guest confinement is depth behind them — but the record and the warning are, and docs/threat-model.md and the release notes say it in those terms.
  • P5-9snapshot restore is broken for any machine that had a workspace. [ADDED 2026-08-24, found by P5-7; a task rather than a fold-in under D30’s rule.] Firecracker refuses the load with “Error manipulating the backing file: No such file or directory /workspace.ext4”. The cause is the jail: a snapshot records its drives by the path written in it, which since P5-1 is the chroot-relative /workspace.ext4, and Restore loads the snapshot before it copies the workspace and repoints the drive — an order that worked when the recorded path was an absolute host one that still existed. The same family as the rootfs-at-both-names fix P5-1 made for the three-agent recipe, and missed because no suite snapshots a machine with a workspace. Fix the staging order, and add the case to an acceptance so it cannot go missing again.
  • P5-8The sibling-ptrace refusal, made legible. [ADDED 2026-08-24, D33.] P5-3’s per-process Landlock domains refuse ptrace between siblings, so attaching a debugger to an already-running process in a guest fails even on dev, where the profile deliberately leaves ptrace out of the refusal list. Launching a program under a debugger still works, because the child inherits its parent’s domain. Shipping that is acceptable only if somebody who hits it can find out why, so this task names it where a person will look, states what still works, and proves both halves in the acceptance rather than asserting them. Whether dev should relax it is decided and logged in D33; base stays strict either way.
  • P5-4The bars, re-earned, and the sentence replaced. The jailer and a seccomp filter sit on the boot path, so cold boot-to-ready and snapshot restore are re-measured on the bare-KVM reference (D15) and the numbers reported are the ones measured — a bar is never assumed across a change of security posture. Then the README’s “not hardened yet” sentence is replaced by a true one naming what is enforced and what is not, and docs/threat-model.md is updated to match it rather than to soften it. Every acceptance suite in dev/ re-run green.
  • P5-5Launch assets, committed and posted nowhere. A ~20-second terminal recording — boot, a refused egress showing the printed fix line, a five-agent team up, a chain --verify — embedded at the top of the README; a first-time-visitor pass over the README’s opening screen; and docs/launch/hn-post.md refreshed to v0.8/v0.9 reality. John submits things; this task produces artifacts.

Acceptance test — recreate & verify

  1. Boot a sandbox. On the host, /proc/<pid>/mountinfo shows the VMM’s root is this sandbox’s chroot, its filesystem holds no /etc, /home, /usr or /var, and it is not running as root — read from the host, not claimed by the CLI. [AMENDED 2026-08-24, D29.] This item first said “not running as the invoking user” and named /proc/<pid>/root. Both were wrong: that link reports the path as the process sees it, which is / for a chrooted process and / for an unjailed one — the same answer for opposite facts — and the VMM drops to the invoking user rather than to a dedicated account, which D29 argues for and prices.
  2. That process’s /proc/<pid>/status reports a seccomp mode that is on, and the cgroup it sits in is the one KelyfOS asked for.
  3. From inside the guest, a command that tries to open a path the flavor’s profile does not grant is refused by the kernel — and the same command inside /work succeeds, because a profile that breaks the toolbox has hardened nothing.
  4. Every existing acceptance suite re-run green: dev/accept-e1.sh through accept-e5.sh, the per-feature ones, and all fourteen cookbook recipes.
  5. A snapshot taken before this phase still restores, or the incompatibility is stated in the release notes as a break rather than discovered by somebody.
  6. Cold boot-to-ready and snapshot restore re-measured on the bare-KVM reference, 10 runs each; the numbers in the README and in docs/ are the measured ones.
  7. The README contains no sentence claiming a protection this phase did not build, and docs/threat-model.md agrees with it line for line.
  8. [ADDED 2026-08-24 — the product owner, before the final publish is called done.] The quickstart is re-timed against the actual v0.9 release artifacts, so the number the README prints is a measurement of the release it ships in rather than of the one before it. P5-4 measured 149 s on macOS and about 10 s of it on a Linux/KVM box against the published v0.8, because that is what a visitor downloaded that day; this closes the gap that leaves. Either order is acceptable: publish a release candidate the way v0.3 did, measure, then finalise — or publish the release and re-time immediately after, which is simpler when nothing is expected to move. The figure is expected within noise of 149 s and ~10 s; if it drifts materially the measured number is what goes in, never the remembered one, and the provenance sentence beneath it is updated to say which release it was measured against.

Exit checkpoint: tag v0.9 — “the boundary is the hardware, and everything around it is locked too.”

config KELYFOS_PHASE_6

Phase 6 — v1.0, the promise

DONE

Goal: the number 1.0 is a promise made to a stranger — that the surfaces they build against will not move under them, that the bytes they download can be traced back to the source that made them, and that everything this repository says about itself is true. Phases 0–5 built the product. This phase makes it something somebody outside the project can rely on, which is a different kind of work: three of its five pillars are not features at all but the withdrawal of every claim the repository has not earned, the enumeration of what stabilises, and the mechanism that lets a stranger check the first two without asking us.
Opened by P6-0 on the product owner’s instruction of 2026-08-24, spec-first in the P5-0 pattern. The pillars are the owner’s; the sizing is this seat’s, and where a pillar was scoped down the case is in the decision log rather than in a quiet omission — D34 for the phase, D35 for the P4 disposition, D36 for the credential window, D37 for output scrubbing, D38 for reproducibility, D39 for the signing mechanism, D40 for the documentation rule this document never had, D41 for the clients connect supports.

The order is not arbitrary. P6-1 comes first because every later task would otherwise inherit a false sentence and republish it. P6-8 (the release, built by CI) precedes the artifacts it signs, because the tag cannot be cut by a mechanism that does not exist. P6-14 (the compatibility promise) comes after every task that changes a schema, because a promise drafted against a moving surface is wrong the week it lands. P6-18 through P6-20 are the exit and are budgeted as work, not as ceremony: thirteen acceptance suites, 239 checks, two manual workflow dispatches with 180-minute timeouts, and a machine with KVM in front of it.
  • P6-0The phase, drafted before it is built. Read the P4 backlog, PLAN-FEATURES.html §4’s parking lot and every carried debt, then write this section: a verdict for each P4 item, a task list sized against the code rather than against the backlog’s wording, an acceptance test that names the six criteria in §1 by number, and the decisions above. Includes the two mechanical fixes without which this document would misreport itself — tools/check-plan.py’s dangling-reference check matched only P[0-4]-\d+, so a Phase 6 task id could be cited and never defined, and the dashboard had hand-written rows for phases 0–5 only while the script counted a sixth phase’s boxes against the total.
  • P6-1The honesty sweep: every claim this repository has not earned, withdrawn before anything is built on top of it. A 1.0 cannot be assembled on sentences that are already false, and the audit that opened this phase found them in numbers. At least: the three places claiming the published artifacts are byte-identical to what make image produces (README.md, Makefile, dev/fetch-image.sh) — the shipped v0.9 kernels name two different build hosts, one of them a laptop; llms.txt’s “not hardened yet”, which is a generated string, so the drift gate has been keeping a retracted claim consistently wrong; the README’s provenance paragraph, which attributes “every figure above” to make bench and ten runs when the five-agent pair comes from one run of a different workflow; docs/protocol.md §7’s claim that the host refuses a protocol revision it does not implement, which no code does; the generated CLI reference’s silent loss of one-letter boolean flags, so kelyfos log -f exists and is undocumented and the gate structurally cannot see it; the supervisor’s Version = "0.2.0-p2" and both os-release files at 0.1.0-dev; tools/gendocs’s hardcoded MCP revision string beside the constant it should read; and the two client-configuration blocks this repository prints for a stranger to copy. docs/integrating.md says of its block “that is .mcp.json in this repository, verbatim” and it is not — the file has run a launcher script since F-D48 changed it; the README stops short of the word but makes the same claim in substance, printing that block under “this repository ships a .mcp.json that attaches Claude Code”. The block itself is the defect either way: it names the inward bridge, which is the P2-era mistake F-D48 found and fixed, relies on a bare name on a PATH a non-interactive limactl shell does not have, and omits the --policy whose absence F-D44 exists to prevent. Two of the highest-traffic pages in the repository, telling a stranger to configure a client in a way that would give it no ceiling at all. Each one is small. Together they are the difference between a 1.0 and a number. The sweep is one commit and it is exhaustive rather than illustrative — the list above is what one audit found, not a budget.
  • P6-2A security contact that exists, and a scanner on a schedule. Three documents tell a person who has found a vulnerability not to open a public issue and to contact the maintainer privately; none of them says how, and there is no address anywhere. That is the most user-visible dead end in the repository and it is on the security path. SECURITY.md with a real disclosure policy: the channel, the response commitment, the supported-versions statement (which the compatibility promise then has to agree with), and what a reporter should expect. And govulncheck promoted from a habit into CI — it has been run by hand at least ten times and once found a real advisory, so this is not new tooling, it is a discipline the project already practises being written down where it cannot be forgotten. The task states which workflow, whether a finding fails the build or opens an issue, and it does not pretend to have flipped a repository setting only the owner can flip.
  • P6-3Fuzz harnesses for every parser on a trust boundary — and the boundary named, because “every untrusted-input parser” has no completion criterion without one. The instruction named four targets and two of them are not where it thinks: internal/vsock parses no bytes at all (it is AF_VSOCK socket setup), and the MCP stdio line protocol is internal/proto’s newline-delimited JSON reader plus encoding/json, so a byte-level harness there measures the standard library. The real framing on both channels is internal/proto, and the audit found roughly eighteen further decode sites the list of four omits. So: declare the boundary — guest→host and network→host are hostile; on-disk state KelyfOS itself wrote is corruption-tolerance, not adversary — and fuzz everything on the hostile side of it. Corpora committed, a short run per push, a longer run on its own schedule rather than sharing the Monday run whose whole purpose is to fail loudly about caches. And an inventory guard test, because go test -fuzz takes one target in one package per invocation, so any CI list drifts silently — the same class of decay the drift gate already prevents for documentation and the schema test already prevents for the config parser.
  • P6-4The credential window, narrowed as far as the architecture allows — and the rest parked with its blocking questions intact (D36). Today a secret is bound to a domain for the whole life of the proxy and attached to any allowed request that reaches it; the parked idea asks for a handle that is call-bound, destination-locked, scope-limited and single-use. Three of those four are buildable now and one is not. Deliver: destination-locking — a secret binds to an endpoint, not to a domain and every subdomain under it; a receipt that says when a credential was not attached and why, which today is a silence. [AMENDED 2026-08-24, D44, before implementation.] Method scoping, the request path in the record, and call-binding are cut, each for a reason found by an adversarial review of the design and re-verified against the code: the policy file cannot carry a comma, a path is a credential on several real APIs, and call-binding needs a structured home the string grammar cannot give it. What is not delivered is a handle the guest presents, because the guest has no call identity on the exec path and there is no guest→host request channel for a non-team sandbox; and correlation on the flagship run + mcp path, because those are two processes sharing only a locked file. Both are stated in docs/networking.md as loudly as that document already states which traffic the proxy can read. Six secret-parsing sites and five proxy constructions, one of them shared by two entry points: all of them, or the feature is the jailed bug again.
  • P6-5Echo suppression: the bound secret scrubbed out of what the proxy can read — named for what it is rather than for what it sounds like (D37). KelyfOS keeps secret values out of the guest by construction, so there has never been anything to redact. The case construction does not reach is a legitimate, allowlisted, secret-bound call whose response carries a credential. Exact matching on the bound values catches the half of that case that is real and cheap — an API reflecting the header back, an error quoting the token, a redirect carrying basic credentials — with zero false positives. It does not catch a response that mints a new credential, because a new token equals no bound string, and the general recogniser that would catch it is a false-positive machine that would corrupt a tarball or a JSON document undiagnosably from inside the guest. So the feature ships as echo suppression, length-preserving so a keep-alive connection cannot desync, on the terminated and plain-HTTP paths only, with the tunnelled majority stated before anything else is stated, and the compressed-body case handled or declared rather than left to be discovered. The record gains a way to say the proxy altered bytes, because a chain that reports only how much the proxy could read understates a proxy that writes.
  • P6-6The session export made verifiable by its reader — kelyfos verify, and no cryptography at all. §1 has required a “standalone, verifiable HTML session report” since the day this plan was written, and the export is not one: it embeds no events, no per-event hash and no chain head, and it renders the exporter’s own verdict as a green badge. A reader is trusting a sentence the file wrote about itself. Embed the chain as an inert data island, print the chain head as text, and add kelyfos verify <report.html> that extracts it and re-runs the same verification the recorder already implements — offline, keyless, no trust root, no network. This is the unmet definition-of-done criterion, it is separable from signing, and it is worth more to a reader than a signature would be. It lands before P6-7 so that signing is a promotion of something that already works rather than a substitute for it. [EXTENDED 2026-08-24, D44.] And it fixes the hash preimage, which P6-4 measured and which has to be right before v1.0 freezes anything: the digest is computed over the marshalled Event struct, so a chain written by a build with one more field makes an older build report event N has been modified — tamper-detection firing on a legitimate record. Hashing a canonical form instead is what makes docs/events.md §3’s “adding a field is not breaking” true, which it is not today.
    • The audit, and the gate it puts in front of the tag. An independent external security audit of babec8f arrived on 2026-08-24: 23 findings, 1 critical, 6 high, 9 medium, 7 low. It is high quality and largely real. D45 records its receipt and this triage plan. v1.0 does not tag until the trust-boundary group is closed and proven — C-1, H-1 and H-2, which are one defect wearing three hats: the workspace block device is a guest→host surface the threat model does not list at all, and the product’s central claim fails on it. The seven tasks below are inserted here, before P6-7, because list position is work order and the owner’s ruling is that the blocking set precedes the tag while the long tail and connect continue after it. The order inside them is the owner’s too: M-3 first because it is six lines and a live data-loss bug in the default flow, then the fixtures before any boundary fix so that every finding is a failing test before it is a fixed one.
  • P6-21M-3: the window in which --review destroys an edit somebody made while they were reading the review. First of everything, on the owner’s ruling and on the arithmetic: it is about six lines and it is live data loss in the default flow. Stage() fingerprints the host directory, the review is then shown to a person and waits on them for as long as they take, and Commit() renames over the destination without looking again. Everything that person edited in that window is gone. Re-fingerprint immediately before the rename, divert on mismatch exactly as Stage already would, and keep .kelyfos-previous until the next successful run rather than deleting it on the line after the swap — a backup removed at the moment it becomes useful is not a backup.
  • P6-22Adversarial fixtures: a committed hostile corpus, and a CI job named for what it does. Before any finding is fixed, because a fixed finding with no fixture is a finding that grows back. testdata/hostile/ and a job that runs it, seeded with the nine KVM-free fixtures the report enumerates: a crafted-dirent ext4 image, an image whose modes are 0777, write_file with /dev/vdb, with /etc/passwd and with ../escape, a team frame carrying timeout_ms = 1<<40, a ten-thousand-unique-key store loop, edgeless refusal spam with record_payloads on, a hundred concurrent POST /sandboxes, a stub sandbox answering OK:false to OpTrust, and a guest stub streaming 1 MiB frames with no StreamExit. Each one is a failing test first and green only when its finding is fixed. This is the gap the report found in this project’s own hardening: nineteen fuzz targets, and until P6-6 every one of them fed a parser a host-authored string. None of them was a guest→host path, and the guest is the untrusted party.
  • P6-23Every finding triaged against HEAD, with a verdict, in the decision log. Not against babec8f, which is what the audit read: the tree has moved and H-6 is already partly fixed. Four verdicts and no fifth: CONFIRMED-against-current-code, ALREADY-FIXED-since-babec8f, STALE, WONTFIX with the reason written down. Nothing is fixed that was not first reproduced or read as present in current source — the rule that keeps a triage from turning into a rewrite of code somebody else described. [BLOCKED 2026-08-25 — needs John: the audit’s text for M-1, M-4, M-6, M-7, M-8, L-1 through L-7, and D-1.] Eleven of the twenty-three have verdicts (D46, D47) and every finding in the v1.0 gate and in the blocking set is among them. The remaining twelve are known here only as identifiers, and a finding that cannot be read cannot be triaged — inventing a plausible defect to match an identifier would break the one rule this task exists to enforce.
  • P6-24The trust boundary the threat model does not have: the workspace block device. C-1, H-1, H-2 — and this is the v1.0 gate. The guest writes an ext4 image the host then extracts with debugfs rdump, and a dirent the guest authored decides where the host writes. That is arbitrary host file write as the invoking user, from inside the sandbox, on the one surface §5’s table omits. Two layers, both of them, because either alone is one bug from failing: validate the image and refuse rather than sanitise any name carrying /, \0, . or ..; and extract beneath a pinned root with openat2(RESOLVE_BENEATH|RESOLVE_NO_SYMLINKS) semantics, so the second layer holds when the first is wrong. With them: H-1, where write_file and read_file validate no path at all and hand back /dev/vda and /dev/vdb — the block devices the Landlock profile deliberately withholds, reachable because PID 1 is not confined by it; and H-2, guest-authored modes surviving the write-back onto the host, including the workspace root’s own. The workspace device joins the trust-boundary table as a first-class guest→host path, and the boot and restore bars are re-earned if the extraction path moved the timing. [AMENDED 2026-08-25 — the product owner, three additions to the definition of done.] (1) The documentation corrections ride the same commit as the fix and not one earlier. The trust-boundary table gains the workspace block device there, and the README’s “Both layers exist now” becomes true in the commit that makes it true — a document claiming a fix that is not in yet is the defect P6-1 spent a commit removing, and doing it while nominally fixing security would reintroduce it at the worst possible place. (2) The boot and restore bars are re-measured on the bare-KVM reference if the extraction path moved, and a validation walk plus openat2 beneath a pinned root is new work on the sync-back path, so the assumption is that it did until measured. The bars have wide headroom; that is a reason to expect the numbers to hold, not a reason to leave the record stale. Measured figures are reported whether or not they moved. (3) The gate opens on the fixtures and the full acceptance suite, not on the fixtures alone. Green fixtures prove the three findings are closed; they do not prove the fix left everything else standing, and a boundary repair that quietly broke the feature it guards would pass a fixture-only gate.
  • P6-25The clamps a hostile agent can reach: H-3, H-4, H-5, H-6, M-9. Each is small, each is a denial of service on the host driven from the untrusted side, and together they are the rest of the v1.0-blocking set. A timeout_ms the guest chooses with no ceiling; a team store that accounts no keys and cannot delete one; a record that writes a body where a digest would do, and describes before it checks; a ceiling and a bearer token the report found missing, one of which has moved since babec8f and is re-read rather than re-fixed; and no total-output ceiling anywhere. Every one of them has a fixture in P6-22 before it has a fix.
  • P6-26M-2 and M-5, which are not trivia. fork applies no CPU quota, so a documented hard ceiling is bypassed by asking for it from the untrusted side — a cap that is not applied is worse than a cap that was never claimed. And an agent name reaches the kernel command line, where a second init= is a sentence somebody else gets to finish. Both before the long tail, because both are the shape this phase exists to remove: a claim the code does not keep.
  • P6-27The long tail, batched, and the documentation table corrected as each fix lands. M-1, M-4, M-6, M-7, M-8, every L-* and D-1: fixed in batches where they are cheap, parked with a logged reason where sizing says post-1.0. And the overstatements the report lists are corrected in P6-1’s spirit — including the README’s “Both layers exist now”, which is untrue of the host-side workspace path until C-1 is closed. Each correction lands with the fix underneath it and not before it, because a document that claims a fix which is not in yet is the exact defect P6-1 spent a commit removing. [BLOCKED 2026-08-25 — needs John: the same audit text P6-23 is waiting on.] The half that could be done is done, because it was tied to fixes that landed: every documentation correction rode the commit that made it true — the README’s “Both layers exist now” and the trust-boundary table with P6-24, docs/teams.md’s store bounds and docs/e2b-shim.md’s token and ceiling with P6-25, docs/resources.md on fork and agent names with P6-26. What remains is M-1, M-4, M-6, M-7, M-8, every L-* and D-1, which are known here only as identifiers, plus whatever overstatements the report’s own list names that these fixes did not already reach. Neither can be worked from an identifier, and guessing at a plausible defect to match one is the failure D47 refused.
  • P6-7Signed exports — with a key the reader can anchor, or the word withdrawn. P4-3 asks for kelyfos log --export --sign verified offline by kelyfos verify. The only shape that gives a reader something the hash chain has not already given them is a key the user holds: --sign-key, ed25519 from the standard library, no new dependency, and the verifier reports a vocabulary rather than a badge — chain intact or broken, crossed with signed-by-K or unsigned. A per-run ephemeral key is explicitly rejected: it proves one process made both halves, which the chain already proves, and a green “signed” beside a key nobody has ever seen invites the reader to stop asking. An unsigned report still verifies, so the signature is optional by construction and never becomes mandatory by accident. Decide and pin what is signed — the chain head plus a digest of the embedded events, so a re-export of the same session does not change the signature.
  • P6-8The release, built and published by CI rather than by a laptop. There is no release workflow. Every release this project has cut was assembled by hand, and the v0.9 artifacts prove it: the two architectures were built on two different machines, one of them the developer’s. Nothing above the weakest provenance claim is reachable until this changes, so it changes first. A tag-triggered workflow builds both architectures from source, cross-builds both CLIs, stages dist/, regenerates SHA256SUMS from scratch — both release targets currently append to it and nothing truncates it — and publishes. Two cheap fixes ride along because the workflow needs them: the CI cache key hardcodes the base flavor while the build directory is per-flavor, so every release-flavor build has been running cold at about forty minutes. This task is the prerequisite of P6-11 and of the exit itself.
  • P6-9Determinism configured, and reproducibility measured rather than claimed (D38). The Go binaries are already bit-reproducible — measured, cold cache, different source path, identical digest. The image layer is not, and the reasons are four knobs: BR2_REPRODUCIBLE, SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH, a fixed filesystem UUID and hash seed, and gzip -n. Turn them on. Then do not write the word down: add a repro-check workflow that rebuilds and diffs, and let its result — per artifact, pass or fail — be the statement the project makes. Whether a full Buildroot tree rebuilds byte-identically has never been measured here, upstream calls the feature experimental and requires an identical build path, and the compiler cache has been mixing cached and fresh objects in every build this project has ever done. The honest experiment is a matrix and it costs hours of runner time. A reproducible-builds statement that says “the CLI is, the rootfs is not, here is the run that shows it” is worth more than one that says “reproducible”, and it is the only one this project is entitled to.
  • P6-10An SBOM per release, covering the whole image rather than the convenient half. The pinned Buildroot already ships a CycloneDX generator, and the stripped CLI still carries complete Go module build information, so both halves have tooling. The gap is the seam: the guest supervisor is cross-compiled by KelyfOS’s own toolchain and arrives through the rootfs overlay rather than as a Buildroot package, so a Buildroot-only SBOM would omit the one component this project actually wrote. An SBOM that is confidently incomplete is the supply-chain form of an audit record that is confidently wrong. Merge the three sources per architecture, emit the component count from the build information rather than transcribing it, and publish it as a release asset covered by SHA256SUMS.
  • P6-11Attested release artifacts, and exactly one claim made about them (D39). With the build in a workflow, actions/attest takes the checksums file KelyfOS already produces, in the format it already produces it, and creates one provenance attestation covering every asset, plus a second attestation over the SBOM. That is SLSA v1.0 Build Level 2 — a hosted platform signing a statement about which workflow, from which commit, produced these bytes — and it verifies offline against a trusted root fetched once. It is not the same claim as GitHub’s immutable-release attestation, which carries no builder identity at all and asserts only that GitHub received these bytes under this tag; that one is a separate owner setting, worth having, and must never be described with the same sentence. The four places that currently say “not yet signed — that is P4-3” are retired atomically with the mechanism, and the README gains the one command a stranger runs to check it.
  • P6-12macOS: the CLI ports, and doctor owns the Linux layer (P4-7, scoped by D35). The owner’s ruling is that a macOS user never runs limactl, and that is exactly deliverable. Today the CLI does not compile for darwin at all — the whole compile-level delta is seven lines behind two build-tag file pairs — and the release ships Linux binaries only. So: port it; ship it; teach doctor to provision, start, stop and report on the Lima layer from an embedded copy of the configuration, including drift detection, because Lima freezes its template into an instance at creation and this machine already carries an instance whose configuration no longer matches the file; bootstrap the layer’s tools and print the in-VM doctor’s own output on the Mac; and refuse every other command on darwin with one sentence naming the way in, rather than letting a user who downloaded a macOS binary discover a missing /dev/kvm. What this task does not do, and what P4-7’s own wording must stop promising, is “same commands everywhere” — that needs a transport across limactl shell, and the audit measured that an interrupt does not cross it, which would orphan a microVM and silently discard the workspace it was syncing back. Windows and WSL2 are post-1.0 and the compatibility document says so by name.
  • P6-13kelyfos connect <client> — one-command client setup. [ADDED 2026-08-24 — the product owner, into this draft.] Attaching an agent is currently a paragraph of hand-edited JSON per client, and every one of the lessons this project paid for lives in the details of that paragraph. The command writes the client’s own configuration, in its own format and its own location, with the policy path absolute and explicit rather than discovered (the F-D44 rule) and the launch path whatever P6-12’s architecture makes correct, so the two features compose instead of duplicating. Idempotent: a second run updates the entry in place, --remove uninstalls it, and a client that cannot be found refuses with a fix line rather than a stack trace. The supported list is short and was researched live rather than remembered — six clients, named with their evidence and their rejections in D41 — with a generic mode that prints the correct snippet for everything else, and every supported entry in the generated documentation carrying the tool and version and date it was verified against. One template with a swapped key is impossible and D41 says why: the working-directory and expansion matrix is asymmetric across all six, so half of them would silently attach the wrong policy under a shared snippet — F-D44’s failure, once per client. --check spawns the server the configuration names and completes a real MCP handshake, because “configured” asserted without evidence is the thing this command exists to replace — and it reports what a handshake honestly proves (this server starts and speaks MCP, here, now) as distinct from what it cannot (that the client will find the file, or expand the same variables, or speak this era of the protocol). The client is written dual-era from the first line: probe first, fall back on any other answer, which costs one round trip against KelyfOS’s own servers and needs no rewrite when the protocol revision moves.
    Settled before the task starts, so it need not be rediscovered: the surface a fresh user’s client is pointed at is serve-mcp, not mcp — F-D48 already found and fixed that mistake once in this repository’s own .mcp.json; and dev/mcp-server.sh is the hand-written prototype of what this command generates, so it is the specification to read rather than a file to ignore. Sequenced after P6-12, because the macOS launch path is whatever that task’s architecture makes correct. Four rulings the task makes and records rather than assuming: whether “that client is not installed” is a catalog refusal or a plain failure — the catalog already holds environment conditions with typeable fixes, but D33 shows the owner enforcing that boundary strictly, so it is ruled on explicitly; whether the written path is absolute or the client’s own variable, which F-D44 and recipe 9 currently answer differently; what happens to a configuration file that already holds other servers, for which this repository has no precedent at all — nothing here has ever merged into a file the product did not create; and how the write is made testable, since every home-touching path in this codebase is redirectable by one environment variable and there is not a single t.Setenv("HOME") in the tree, so an override is a design requirement rather than a convenience.
  • P6-14The compatibility promise — the document that makes this a 1.0. The words “semver” and “deprecated” appear nowhere in this repository. What does exist is unusually good raw material: seven surfaces already have a machine-readable source of truth and a CI-enforced generated page, so the promise can cite rather than re-list and cannot go stale by the mechanism that already keeps the reference honest. The promise names what stabilises — the kelyfos.toml schema, the CLI surface, the host/guest protocol revision policy, the MCP tool surface, the flight-recorder record, exit codes, denial identifiers — and, just as explicitly, what does not: guest confinement profiles are a security surface that must be allowed to narrow, and the E2B subset has always described itself as best-effort. It states what a major, minor and patch bump mean here, and what they attach to, because three independent version constants exist with no stated relationship to the product number and every release so far has shipped the same two of them. It defines a deprecation mechanism, since none exists and two configuration spellings have been kept “for compatibility” since v0.4 with no removal path. It carries the clause D41 requires: client configuration formats are external surfaces, re-verified on their own cadence, explicitly outside the drift gate and outside the semver promise, because there is no universal standard coming and per-client writers are permanent infrastructure. And it settles three questions the audit raised: whether a new record type breaks a consumer, whether the hash chain’s dependence on Go struct field order is now frozen ABI (with a test that pins it, because none does), and what KelyfOS does about an MCP revision that has already moved on without it. Sequenced after every task that changes a schema, deliberately.
  • P6-15The E3-0 pattern, re-run whole. Parallel audits of every hand-written document against the code that implements it — not against the other documents, which is the version that reports a tidy directory. E3-0 read seven documents and found roughly forty drifts and four real code defects; the corpus has more than doubled since, and seven pages have never been through it at all. The generated set is named explicitly and excluded, because a hand edit there is reverted by the next regeneration and turns the pipeline red; findings against a generated page are routed to the generator instead. Findings first, corrections second, in that order and in that many commits, so the record of what was found survives the fixing of it.
  • P6-28The fifteen code defects the documentation audit found. Routed out of P6-15 by D49, because a documentation commit that also changes behaviour is a commit nobody can review. Ordered by what a user would notice: kelyfos shell reports exited 0 when the supervisor died mid-session, contradicting both docs/protocol.md §5.7 and proto.ShellExit’s own doc comment; a file.write event is recorded from the request, so a write the guest refused is in the chain as though it happened; a forked team member emits no resource.oom at all, because forkAgent installs no guest-event handler where bootAgent does; a paused sandbox that had egress can never be resumed, and pause prints the resume command anyway; scratch above mem is refused on two entry points and silently accepted on the other two, where the cap it creates is inert. Each one is a defect the documents were right about, which is the useful half of an audit that reads them against the code.
  • P6-29The DCO, gated where it can be true. D56. CONTRIBUTING.md has required a Signed-off-by since v0.1 and not one commit in this repository carries one, which makes the requirement a claim the project fails on every push. History cannot be rewritten and rewriting it would be worse than the gap. So: a CI check that requires the trailer on new commits, and one honest line in CONTRIBUTING.md saying pre-v1.0 history predates enforcement — the “false until true” convention this project already uses for a claim it intends to earn rather than one it quietly drops. The check has to pass on a merge commit and on this repository’s own history without failing every existing branch, which is the part worth designing rather than copying.
  • P6-30Dependabot security updates, and what a bot-authored pull request costs. D54. Enabled by the owner as its own consent rather than folded into P6-2, because it does not report — it opens pull requests, which is a different thing to agree to in a repository whose CI boots microVMs. The task is the part around the toggle: what a Dependabot PR is allowed to touch, whether it may run the KVM workflows (it must not — those are dispatch-only for a reason and a bot branch is not a reason to change that), and the line in SECURITY.md that stops being false once alerts are on.
  • P6-31The macOS binary a stranger downloads, and what Gatekeeper does to it. D55. v1.0 ships the darwin pair raw, unsigned and unnotarized, because there is no Apple identity today — so the honest thing is not to ship it quietly and let the first macOS user meet “cannot be opened because the developer cannot be verified” with no warning. The documentation states the quarantine plainly, gives the clearing step, and says why it is that way rather than implying the binary is broken. Signing and notarization are logged as post-1.0 with the condition that unblocks them, per D55.
  • P6-16The documents a 1.0 is expected to have and this one does not. A CHANGELOG.md aggregated from every release’s notes, v0.0 through v1.0 — today those notes exist only as GitHub release bodies, outside the tree, outside the drift gate, and three of them carry breaking-change statements the project treats as commitments. An upgrade guide whose centrepiece is the pre-v0.9 snapshot break, scoped to the breaks the project has actually shipped and can name from the code rather than to an archaeology of eleven tags. CONTRIBUTING.md refreshed against reality: it currently describes a pre-v0.1 project, calls the hardening that shipped in Phase 5 “phase 4 work”, points a security reporter at a contact that does not exist, and requires a sign-off that no commit in this repository carries. And issue and pull-request templates — where a template that restates the §2 non-goals does more work than a template that asks for a stack trace. The task decides, once, whether the changelog is the source the release notes are cut from or a mirror something regenerates; a second copy of the truth that nothing keeps honest is the exact failure the generated reference exists to prevent.
  • P6-17The generated set regenerated, and a token count measured by a command that is committed. Every task above changes a flag, a key, a tool or an event, so the reference, llms.txt and llms-full.txt are regenerated and the count is re-measured. The measurement command goes into the repository this time — the last count was taken with an invocation recorded only as a sentence in a progress row, which is the class of unrecorded provenance this project refuses everywhere else. A number that cannot be reproduced is an estimate wearing a measurement’s clothes.
  • P6-18The bars re-earned, every suite green, and the two criteria nobody owns, discharged. Cold boot-to-ready and snapshot restore re-measured on the bare-KVM reference (D15) across everything this phase put on the boot path; all thirteen acceptance suites and every cookbook recipe re-run; and the two §1 criteria that no pillar covers, faced rather than assumed — the verifiable export (P6-6 builds it; this proves it) and the E2B SDK smoke test, which was run once by hand in August and has no suite, no job and no HTTP test behind it. That one gets a decision: a fourteenth acceptance suite with a pinned SDK, or a re-worded criterion citing the evidence that exists. Deciding it is the point; letting it pass unexamined is what this task exists to prevent. Budgeted as work: two manual workflow dispatches, a Linux/KVM host, and hours rather than minutes.
  • P6-19The docs-only exam, re-run against the v1.0 artifacts. The E3-5 bar: a fresh session, the installed binaries and the documentation, no source tree, no plan files, no git history, no web — and a task it can only complete if the documentation is true. The environment is the thing that makes it evidence rather than theatre, so it is set up as E3-5 specifies and the transcript is committed whatever it says. The task is chosen against the surface this phase added, which is what E4 and E5 both did, rather than repeated for comparability.
  • P6-20The quickstart re-timed against the actual v1.0 release, the README’s first screen, and the post. P5’s eighth acceptance item is now doctrine: the number the README prints is measured against the release it ships in, not the one before it, and a release candidate is available if the ordering needs it. Then a first-time-visitor pass over the opening screen — a stranger’s ten seconds is the scarcest resource this project has — and docs/launch/hn-post.md brought to v1.0 reality: the new numbers, the supply-chain paragraph rewritten from a confession into a mechanism, and the honest limits kept rather than quietly dropped now that there are fewer of them. John submits things; this task produces artifacts.
Owner actions this phase cannot take for itself. Each is recorded as [BLOCKED — needs John] where it blocks a task, and carried in STATUS.md and the final report: (1) the DCO ruling — sign from here on, gate only new commits, or amend CONTRIBUTING.md to match a history that cannot be rewritten; (2) a security contact or the private-reporting channel for SECURITY.md; (3) whether immutable releases are enabled, which locks published assets and protects tags and is therefore a commitment rather than a toggle; (4) whether Dependabot security updates are enabled; (5) whether a macOS binary is distributed as a raw download, which Gatekeeper quarantines, or through a package manager that does not — and, if the former, whether a signing identity exists.

Acceptance test — v1.0 means this list, run

  1. The six criteria in §1, each answered by name and by evidence, not by assertion: the five-minute quickstart, the one-command run with egress refused and the secret outside the guest, the two boot bars and a four-way fork, the complete, tamper-evident, exportable and verifiable audit record, the E2B shim passing an SDK smoke test, and published-with-a-quickstart-and-a-demo plus the owner using it for his own runs. Two of the six were unmet when this phase opened and are named in P6-6 and P6-18; a 1.0 declared with them unexamined would be the same error P5’s eighth item caught and withdrew.
  2. Every acceptance suite in dev/ re-run green on the reference, plus every cookbook recipe and the Go suite — and the count of each stated, so “green” is a number.
  3. Cold boot-to-ready and snapshot restore re-measured on the bare-KVM reference, ten runs each, and the figures in the README and in docs/ are those measurements. Bars not met are reported as not met.
  4. A release built and published by CI, and a stranger’s verification command run against the published artifacts, with its output pasted. The reproducibility statement is whatever the repro-check run measured — per artifact, pass or fail — and never a word that outruns it.
  5. The compatibility promise exists, and every surface it names either has a generated page it cites or an explicit exclusion. No surface is silently in or silently out.
  6. The documentation audit’s findings are landed rather than filed; make docs is clean; the token count is re-measured by the committed command; and the docs-only exam has been re-run against the v1.0 artifacts with its transcript committed whatever it says.
  7. The quickstart is re-timed against the actual v1.0 release artifacts, and the README’s provenance sentence says which release each figure was measured against.
  8. kelyfos connect proved per supported client: the configuration asserted byte-for-byte into a sandboxed home, and the handshake check run live against what it wrote.
  9. No document in this repository contains a claim this project has not earned. That is the phase’s whole thesis, it is the hardest item to prove, and the way it is proved is that P6-1 ran first and P6-15 ran last.

Exit checkpoint: tag v1.0 — “the surfaces hold, the bytes are traceable, and everything it says about itself is true.”

config KELYFOS_PHASE_7

Phase 7 — The declared shape of a run (v1.1 · “what it was allowed to do”)

ACTIVE

Goal: make the record say what a run was permitted to do, not only what it did. Thirty-four settings decide what a machine can touch — eleven resource caps, an allowlist and the ports it covers, bound credentials, a workspace, a spawn budget, store ACLs and an edge list — and every one of them is enforced on each call and then thrown away when the run stops. So a finished session cannot show its own caps, its allowlist, its image digest or its topology, and the first question a reviewer asks — what was this allowed to reach? — has no answer in the file that answers everything else. That is the same gap mcp.host.result was written to close, in this document’s own words: a policy ceiling nobody can see being enforced is a ceiling nobody can audit.
This phase writes the declaration into the chain and then builds the views that read it — a run map, an agent sheet and a reach matrix, in the terminal and in the exported report — and closes three defects the survey turned up on the way. Promoted into this document on the product owner’s ruling of 2026-08-27 (D59): PLAN-FEATURES.html is complete and closed, and F-D56 already redirected the promotion path here.

  • P7-0The policy-record spec, written before the code. docs/policy-record.md: every field this phase adds, its JSON key, its Go type, its position in the frozen order, which of the eight doors that open a chain writes it, and — the longer half — what it deliberately omits and why. Reviewed before P7-2 starts, the way docs/hardening.md was at P5-0, because a schema argued about after it is hashed is a migration rather than an argument. D59 settles the one irreversible question so this task does not reopen it: typed fields on Event, not JSON inside a string, so tools/gendocs documents each one and TestSchemaFieldsExist covers it.
  • P7-1One fold, not three. internal/digest walks a chain once and returns the aggregate every view needs: per-agent counters, per-pair message counts, per-domain egress, store activity, the policy, the timeline. kelyfos watch and internal/report are rewritten onto it and lose their own copies. This is first, and not a refactor to do afterwards, because there are already two folds — host/watch.go’s absorb() and the three loops in internal/report — and the views below would add a third. This project has found that exact class of duplication twice: in the MCP argument summarisers (S19) and in the three copies of SafeText that existed by the end of P6-3.
  • P7-2session.policy, at every door that opens a chain. What the machine was permitted: all eleven [resources] caps, the egress allowlist and the ports it actually covers, bound credentials by name with their host and path scope, workspace, scratch, plugins, forwards, the image’s rootfs_sha256 and kernel_sha256, the tool surface, the parent session id when this machine came from another, and an inbound W3C traceparent when one is supplied. One constructor and one test that enumerates the doors and fails when a new one appears without it — the shape WithPosture exists to enforce, because P5-1 set jailed at one of eight doors and seven kinds of session then said nothing about a wall that was in fact around them. Never a secret value: names, hosts and path scopes only, which is what secret.use already writes. The parent field also closes the third defect — a restored or forked sandbox records its origin as prose (reason: "forked from nightly"), and a snapshot name is not a session id, so a run’s history cannot be followed across two hops.
  • P7-3team.topology, at team boot. The resolved agents with their own sandbox ids and their fork-template group, the declared edges, the store keys with their read and write lists, the collective slice, and whether payloads are being captured. team.spawn already records attach and detach, so the graph replays over time with nothing new. This closes two absences at once: the edge list exists only in run/team.json, which team down deletes, and a member’s own sandbox id — the handle kelyfos diff and kelyfos shell take — lives nowhere else either.
  • P7-4Two silences made loud. [[plugin]] and [[forward]] parse in a team policy, load, and do nothing: packPlugins is called from run and serve-mcp and never from the team path, and resolveForwards from run alone. Nothing refuses them, warns, or documents the limitation. Refused at plan time with a sentence saying why, or wired in — refusing is the smaller change and is strictly better than the current silence. And every sandbox in this product reaches ports 80 and 443 only, because egress.Policy.Ports has no caller and there is no ports key in the schema: either documented as a fixed property in docs/networking.md and printed in the views, or promoted to a configuration key. The default is right either way; the invisibility is not. Placed before the views so that what they show is true.
  • P7-5Retention, pruning and tombstones (D61). ~/.cache/kelyfos/sessions/ has never been pruned — the template cache has a bound and is swept oldest-first, and the records that outlive every sandbox grow without limit, with no setting, no kelyfos sessions prune and no warning. A retention floor, a prune command, a size warning, and an erasure path that writes a replacement record preserving chain integrity while keeping a content fingerprint — a digest proving what was there without keeping it. KelyfOS is unusually well placed for that pattern because file.write already records by digest rather than by content, so it is native rather than bolted on, and it is what lets a GDPR Article 17 erasure and an EU AI Act Article 12 record coexist instead of cancelling.
  • P7-6The layout, once. internal/graph: a pure function from agents, edges and the resources they touch to placed nodes and routed edges, plus the transitive closure the reach view needs — because a star topology in which every worker reaches every other in two hops through the hub is not the isolation it looks like, and OWASP names transitive privilege inheritance by name. Deterministic, golden-tested and fuzzed: a layout that moves between two runs of the same team is a diff nobody can read. One implementation, two backends — runes for the terminal, coordinates for the SVG.
  • P7-7The terminal views. kelyfos team graph renders the topology straight from kelyfos.toml with nothing booted: a pre-flight lint in the category of doctor rather than a monitor, and the place P7-4’s two silences become visible before they cost somebody an afternoon. kelyfos team ps --graph does the same for a running team, and watch gains the map and the agent sheet as panes with live counters. Every refusal carries the fix line the denial explainer already writes, so the view answers “now what” and not only “what”; every guest-influenced string goes through proto.SafeText first. The three modes get their names here and keep them everywhere: declared, aggregate, as-it-ran — three questions, and only the first is one anybody else can answer.
  • P7-8The report’s run section. The run map, the agent sheets, the reach matrix and the store panel, above the existing lane timeline, which does not change. Still one file, still no scripts, still no external request. Three fixes ride with it because this is the task that touches the template: a <meta http-equiv="Content-Security-Policy"> on a file whose whole purpose is to be forwarded and opened from file:// and which has none today; role="img" with <title> and <desc> on the drawing, and no distinction carried by colour alone; and a @media print block, because the report’s pre blocks are capped at 340px with overflow:auto and truncate on paper. Adversary strings reach element text content only — every SVG attribute is a number computed in Go, and there is no <foreignObject>, href or style attribute anywhere in the drawing.
  • P7-9Exporting a running session. --export against a live team, and --refresh, which rewrites the file atomically and emits a meta-refresh so a browser tab follows a running team with no socket anywhere in the path. This is the honest answer to “live” for anyone who does not want a listener, and it exists whether or not P7-12 does.
  • P7-10--json where it is missing. team ps, team graph and the digest, as a documented stable shape. Today only bench, log and verify can be piped, so somebody who wants a view this phase did not think of has to parse a table. This is the extensibility surface, and it is cheaper than any plugin system.
  • P7-11OTLP export. kelyfos log --export-otlp, mapping a session to OTLP-JSON spans: invoke_agent per agent, execute_tool per command, egress attempts and refusals as span events. Versioned apart from the chain and never an input to verification, because as of the v1.42 conventions every gen_ai.* attribute still carries the “Development” badge with no stabilisation timeline, and this record’s field order is hashed. Export to the standard; do not adopt it. A second mapping to the IETF agent-audit-trail draft rides on the same machinery and is worth having ready rather than shipping — it is an individual submission at -01 that expires in February 2027.
  • P7-12kelyfos view — the localhost read-only viewer (D60). The same page as P7-8, served live over server-sent events. These are conditions on the task, not suggestions, and every other process on the host is treated as hostile: binds 127.0.0.1 on a kernel-assigned port and refuses any non-loopback address, with no flag that relaxes it; never starts implicitly and never opens a browser; a 256-bit token from crypto/rand minted per process, printed once, required on every route including the stream, compared in constant time, and never passed as an argument; the Host header must match the address it bound; GET and HEAD only, so the read-only property is structural rather than a check; a strict CSP whose script and style hashes are pinned by a test that fails when the inline script changes; the live update carries data and never markup, with textContent and numeric SVG attributes only and innerHTML nowhere; the session file opened read-only, no path from the URL reaching the filesystem, and the process exiting when the session ends or after an idle timeout. The residual risk is stated rather than buried: on a shared host, loopback is reachable by every local user, so the token is what separates them and not the interface. A Unix socket at 0600 would be stronger and no browser can open one — that is the cost of the page being a page.
  • P7-13The phase read back against its own source. Before the exit checkpoint, an agent that wrote none of Phase 7 reviews the whole diff — every new event field, both renderers, the viewer, and the retention and tombstone path — against the four checklists in §8 rule 10 and against docs/threat-model.md, with make vuln and every fuzz target at a raised FUZZTIME. This is the P6 precedent rather than a new idea: an audit arrived mid-phase and what made it worth having was that nothing was fixed from a description. Findings are answered in the log one by one, including the ones rejected and the reason. A phase that adds a socket, twenty event fields and two renderers does not exit on its acceptance test alone.
  • P7-14A path-scoped credential can attach across a double-trailing-slash boundary. internal/egress/scope.go's covered() trims exactly one trailing slash off a bound Scope.Path before comparing (strings.TrimSuffix(prefix, "/")), so a scope configured with a doubled trailing slash (e.g. /repos//, a plausible config typo rather than a contrived input) approves a request to /repos/ that the fuzz property's own Tomcat/Jetty matrix-param-strip simulation resolves to /repos — not beneath the literal bound prefix. Found by FuzzScopeCovers during P7-4's re-verification (2026-08-27), confirmed independently by the orchestrator by tracing the actual property function against source, not taken on report. Same risk family as S3 (f6ac169, a path-scoped credential attached across a re-segmentation boundary) and the general path-scoping discipline in CHANGELOG.md's Unreleased section; this is a case that fix did not cover. Fix covered() to normalize away every trailing slash on both sides before comparing (not just one), commit the regression seed (prefix="/repos//", target="/repos/") alongside the fix per dev/fuzz.sh's own convention, and extend FuzzScopeCovers's seed corpus with it so the specific input is never rediscovered from scratch. Not gated by D62's barrier and not owned by any of the four lanes — internal/egress/scope.go is outside all four lanes' file boundaries; pick this up whenever convenient, reviewed as a PROXY surface per §8 rule 10 (credential-attachment logic).
  • P7-15FuzzAppendFieldValues OOM-kills its own worker once its corpus grows past roughly 260 entries. Reproduced three times during P7-3's review (twice on phase7/lane1-record, once independently on main with the same shared corpus directory): a fuzz run reliably dies around 12 seconds into “gathering baseline coverage,” with the kernel logging Out of memory: Killed process ... (recorder.test). Not a correctness defect in Append itself — every fixed-length, fresh-corpus run of the fuzz target passes clean (0 crashers) — but a real, reproducible problem in the fuzz target's own memory profile: whatever it generates and retains per corpus entry grows without an evident bound as the corpus regrows, and it is already regrowing past the size that triggered the first OOM. Left unfixed, this silently degrades make fuzz/dev/fuzz.sh for this target the same way P6-3's original fuzz failures degraded confidence in the suite as a whole — a target that kills its own process reads identically to one that was never run, unless someone happens to notice the exit code. Profile the target (start with what each corpus entry retains across iterations — a fuzz corpus directory that only grows, never prunes, is the first suspect) and either bound what it keeps or cap corpus growth, with the fix proven against a corpus grown back to the size that reproduced the original kill. Not gated by D62's barrier and not owned by any of the four lanes — internal/recorder/fuzz_test.go's harness, not any lane's feature code, is the file in question; pick this up whenever convenient.

Acceptance test — recreate & verify

  1. Every cap read back out of the record, field by field. A run with all eleven [resources] keys set to distinct values, then session.policy parsed out of the JSONL — not out of the CLI’s own output — and compared to the file that declared them. Eleven of eleven, named, or the task is not done.
  2. A secret bound and never used still appears, by name and domain, with no value anywhere in the chain — checked by grepping the raw file for the value and finding nothing.
  3. bash dev/demo-team.sh re-run green and unchanged, plus: the exported report’s run map renders six agents, five declared edges, the store with its two ACLs, the one allowed domain and the refusal that carries no edge — each counted out of the HTML rather than eyeballed.
  4. kelyfos team graph in the demo directory, with nothing booted, prints the same topology the running team shows; and a policy carrying [[plugin]] beside [team] is refused with the sentence P7-4 writes.
  5. kelyfos team ps --json and the digest’s JSON parse under a schema check, and kelyfos log --export-otlp validates against OTLP-JSON with its span names asserted.
  6. The viewer, attacked before it is accepted: fetched with no token (401), with a wrong Host (refused), with POST (405), bound to a non-loopback address (refused at startup), and with the token (200) — and the process confirmed to hold the session file read-only.
  7. Retention proves both halves. A pruned session leaves a chain that still verifies; an erased payload leaves a tombstone whose content fingerprint matches what was removed; kelyfos verify is green on both, offline and with no key.
  8. The hostile corpus extended and the ledger no larger. Rendering fixtures for markup in a path, an escape sequence in a message body, a store key that is a <script> tag, a 1 KiB key, a 64-character agent name and a 200 MB session — asserted on the rendered output: no <script, no on*=, no javascript:, no raw control byte. Each fixture fails before its fix.
  9. Every RECORD, PROXY, BROKER and RENDER task carries a review by an agent that did not write it (§8 rule 10), with its findings in the log — the rejected ones too. P7-13’s whole-phase read-back is complete and answered line by line. make vuln clean, and every fuzz target the phase touched run at a raised FUZZTIME with the corpus committed.
  10. The documentation obligations, per §8 rule 9: make docs clean, llms.txt and llms-full.txt regenerated and committed, the token count re-measured and logged, a cookbook recipe CI executes for every new command and flag, and the docs-only exam re-run — this phase adds commands, so the exam is not optional.
  11. The Go suite, every acceptance suite in dev/, and every cookbook recipe re-run green on the reference, with the count of each stated so “green” is a number.

Exit checkpoint: tag v1.1 — “the record says what the run was allowed to do, and three ways of reading it agree.”

section 7

Recreate & test from zero — quickstart

The canonical path a new machine (or a new contributor) follows. Every phase's acceptance test assumes this baseline.

# 1. Primary host — macOS 15+ on Apple Silicon (this project: MacBook Pro M5 Max, 64 GB).
#    Nested virtualization needs an M3 or newer chip.
brew install lima
limactl start --name kelyfos-dev dev/lima.yaml   # Ubuntu 24.04 arm64 · vmType: vz · nestedVirtualization: true
limactl shell kelyfos-dev
#    Alternate hosts: Windows 11 → WSL2 Ubuntu (nestedVirtualization=true under [wsl2]
#    in %UserProfile%\.wslconfig, then `wsl --update`); or any Linux/KVM box directly.

# 2. Inside the Linux VM — confirm KVM is exposed:
ls -l /dev/kvm
crw-rw-rw- 1 root kvm 10, 232 ... /dev/kvm

# 3. Clone first — every script below lives in the repository:
git clone <repo-url> kelyfos && cd kelyfos

# 4. Build tools (inside the VM):
bash dev/install-build-deps.sh   # apt build deps + the pinned Go toolchain (D11)
#    equivalently, by hand:
sudo apt update && sudo apt install -y build-essential git bc flex bison cmake \
     libssl-dev libelf-dev libncurses-dev unzip rsync file cpio wget curl \
     ca-certificates e2fsprogs
bash dev/install-go.sh              # Go from versions.mk; apt ships 1.22, too old

# 5. Build (ARCH defaults to the machine — aarch64 here):
make toolchain     # downloads pinned Buildroot (long, once)
make image         # kernel Image (aarch64) / vmlinux (x86_64) + rootfs.ext4
make cli           # the kelyfos binary into bin/ — image does not build it

# 6. Check, run, verify:
./bin/kelyfos doctor
./bin/kelyfos run --image base &
./bin/kelyfos exec "uname -a"
Linux kelyfos 6.x.x #1 SMP ... aarch64 GNU/Linux

section 8

Developer protocol — how Claude Code works on this project

This file (PLAN.html) is the single source of truth. Read it fully before any session. Then follow these rules, in order:

  • 1. Pick work top-down. Take the first unchecked task in the lowest-numbered phase that is not done. List position defines order; task IDs are stable labels and may be non-sequential. Do not skip ahead across phases without a decision-log entry.
  • 2. Small commits. Reference task IDs in commit messages: P1-6: exec over vsock.
  • 3. Record completion in this file. When a task is verified working: add the checked attribute to its checkbox in the markup, and append a row to the Progress Log (date, task ID, what changed, evidence: the exact command run and a trimmed output snippet). The dashboard bars compute from the markup, not from browser clicks.
  • 4. Close phases with the acceptance test. Run the phase's acceptance steps verbatim, paste evidence into the Progress Log, set the phase's data-status="done" and its badge text to DONE, set the next phase to active / ACTIVE, and update the "Current focus" line (#focus) and header status (#meta-status).
  • 5. Log deviations. Any change of approach, library, or scope gets a Decision Log row with rationale before implementation. Non-goals in section 2 are hard boundaries — do not cross them; propose a decision-log entry and stop if a task seems to require it.
  • 6. Append, never rewrite history. Log rows are immutable. Update #meta-updated with today's date on every edit of this file.
  • 7. Blocked? Mark the task with [BLOCKED: reason] appended to its text, log it, and move to the next task. Surface all blockers at the end of the session summary.
  • 8. Reconcile against CI, not only against the tree. Session start-up reads the latest CI run for origin/main alongside git status and git log. A red main is a blocker and is fixed before any new task is started — not noted, not carried, fixed. This rule exists because main was red from P5-1 until P5-2 found it three tasks later: the failure was in the job after the one that had always passed, every session read the tree and none read the pipeline, and nothing in these rules required anyone to look. A clean tree is not a green build, and P3-6 asked for a green pipeline on main, not for one that is usually green. (The product owner’s ruling of 2026-08-24.) [AMENDED 2026-08-24, D42.] “The latest CI run” means the latest run of every workflow that gates main, not only ci.yml. P6-2 added security.yml, whose whole purpose is to go red when nothing in this repository has changed — an advisory published against a dependency that has not moved. A rule that reads only the build would never see it.
  • 9. Documentation rides with the task that changed the surface. No task is ticked until make docs is clean (CI enforces the generated reference — F-D4); llms.txt and llms-full.txt are regenerated and committed with it; every new user-facing command, flag, kelyfos.toml key or MCP tool has a cookbook recipe that CI executes; the llms-full.txt token count is re-measured and logged whenever the generated set changed; and, when a phase added commands or MCP tools, the docs-only exam is re-run against that phase’s artifacts before its exit. This is F-D9, which governed PLAN-FEATURES.html through five epics and then evaporated when that file closed — this document’s protocol never carried it, and the consequence is that Phase 5 shipped v0.9 with no token re-measurement and no exam, not because anyone decided to skip them but because nothing here required them. A rule that governed the project through its most productive stretch does not stop being true when the file it lived in is finished. (D40.)
  • 10. Verified by somebody who did not write it. No task that touches the record, the proxy, the broker or a renderer is ticked until an agent that did not write the diff has reviewed it adversarially — trying to make the change wrong rather than confirming it is right — and against the source rather than against the description, which is the rule Phase 6’s audit set when it read twenty-four findings and fixed not one of them from its description alone. The reviewer reports; the author fixes; the reviewer does not push, because authorship and verification stop being separate the moment the same hand does both. Findings go in the Progress Log with the task, including the ones rejected and why — a review that records only what it changed is indistinguishable from one that found nothing.
    The four checklists. RECORD: a field appended and never inserted; every new field clippable, slices included; a schema.go row and a generated reference; the door-enumerating test; no secret value reachable by any path; and a real session’s chain still verifying end to end. PROXY: no new plaintext path; the value never inside the guest; the CA never persisted; every decision recorded with its reason. BROKER: no path that reaches a peer without an edge; every refusal recorded; ACLs evaluated before the effect and not after it; caps enforced rather than reported. RENDER: contextual escaping on every value; template.HTML nowhere but the chain blob; every SVG attribute a number computed in Go; proto.SafeText on every terminal path; output bounded, and saying so when it truncates.
    make test, make vuln and every fuzz target the task touched are part of the review, run by the reviewer, and not taken on the author’s word. (D63.)
