2026-08-22P1-0 docs/protocol.md written (400 lines), normative for v0.x. Covers all four required items: (a) the hybrid vsock model — host-side UDS, CONNECT <port>\n → OK <assigned_hostside_port>\n, guest-initiated connections landing on <uds_path>_<port>, and no host vhost module anywhere; (b) constants — guest_cid = 3, host CID 2, and a port map whose ranges encode direction (100xx guest listens, 101xx host listens): exec 10001, mcp 10002, control 10003, ready 10100, events 10101; (c) the newline-delimited JSON exec schema with base64 payloads, a 1 MiB line cap against cat /dev/urandom, 64 KiB write chunks, and exactly one terminating exit frame; (d) MCP framing as newline-delimited JSON-RPC, making kelyfos mcp a byte-level pass-through. Adds a conformance checklist mapping each requirement to the task that implements it. Two forward notes for P3 that the plan did not have: Firecracker's vsock_override for per-fork UDS paths, and the exact vsock reset semantics. Windfall: the newest MCP revision (2026-07-28) explicitly states the stdio wire format "works unchanged over Unix domain sockets, TCP connections, or any similar channel" — so KelyfOS is a conforming custom transport by the spec's own words, not a dialect. Verified against primary sources, not recall: Firecracker docs/vsock.md @ v1.16.1 (handshake, _<port> rule, guest_cid); resources/guest_configs/microvm-kernel-ci-aarch64-6.1.config @ v1.16.1 (CONFIG_VSOCKETS/VIRTIO_VSOCKETS + "# CONFIG_MODULES is not set"); MCP spec transports/stdio revisions 2025-06-18, 2025-11-25, 2026-07-28.
Mechanical: all 11 JSON examples parse; base64 sample decodes to 'Linux kelyfos 6.18.45\n'; no trailing whitespace.
Adversarial review — 4 independent lenses (firecracker / mcp / implementability / consistency), each finding re-checked by a refute-by-default verifier: 28 raised, 0 confirmed (32 agents, 1.64M tokens).
2026-08-22P1-0 Acted on a refuted finding anyway, because its underlying fact checked out against the primary source: §1.6 originally said "vsock state does not survive a snapshot" and "every channel must be re-established". Firecracker is more precise — open connections close but guest listen sockets survive and keep accepting, and the same reset also fires on the still-running source VM when a snapshot is taken. §1.6 now says exactly that, per direction: the supervisor must not re-bind 10001/10002/10003 (which is why the resync RPC can be the first message on a restored VM) but must itself re-dial 10100/10101. This is a precision fix to a note written today, not a change to any settled design. curl firecracker/docs/snapshotting/snapshot-support.md @ v1.16.1
"vsock connections that are open when the snapshot is taken are closed, but existing vsock listen sockets in the guest still remain active and can accept new connections after resume"
"Creating a snapshot has some minor effects on the currently running microVM: The vsock device is reset …"
2026-08-22P1-1 Buildroot br2-external tree under image/buildroot/ (external.desc name KELYFOS, external.mk, Config.in), parameterized by ARCH. Configuration is split into a shared kelyfos_common.fragment plus a one-line per-arch fragment, concatenated at build time — the two architectures cannot drift because there is only one copy of the shared half. Selections: musl toolchain, BusyBox, ext4 rootfs, hostname kelyfos, BR2_INIT_NONE (PID 1 arrives from the overlay — the init script at P1-3, the Go supervisor at P2-1), no getty (there is no console login in KelyfOS), no NLS, devtmpfs-only /dev. make toolchain now really works: image/fetch-buildroot.sh downloads the pinned release, verifies its sha256 against versions.mk and unpacks via a temp-then-rename so an interrupted extraction cannot masquerade as a good tree; the target then configures and builds the cross toolchain. ARCH defaults to the build host with arm64/amd64 normalised, and a linux-only guard tells a macOS user the limactl command to run instead of failing obscurely. Added image/check-config.sh: Kconfig silently drops a misspelled or unsatisfiable symbol, which for this project would mean a build that succeeds with "no modules" or "no getty" quietly gone — so every requested symbol is now asserted present in the resulting .config. limactl shell kelyfos-dev -- make toolchain

2026-08-22P1-2 Guest kernel configured and built per decision D12. Firecracker's own reference guest config is vendored byte-identically under image/buildroot/kernel/base-<arch>.config (header comment only; content sha256 matches upstream), with the KelyfOS delta as merged fragments. The shared fragment restates every guarantee rather than inheriting it — a promise that is only true by inheritance vanishes silently the day the base is re-vendored — covering # CONFIG_MODULES is not set, the virtio set, the vsock symbols required by docs/protocol.md §1.5, ext4/tmpfs/overlayfs/devtmpfs, 8250 console, and # CONFIG_BLK_DEV_INITRD is not set (KelyfOS never boots an initrd). Per-arch fragments carry the artifact choice and the boot arguments: BR2_LINUX_KERNEL_IMAGE on aarch64, BR2_LINUX_KERNEL_VMLINUX on x86_64 — never Image.gz, never bzImage. Verified against Firecracker source that the console is ttyS0 on both arches: aarch64's UART is advertised in the FDT as compatible = "ns16550a", so PL011 is genuinely unnecessary — the one real cmdline difference is the x86-only i8042.* knobs. New image/fetch-kernel.sh closes the integrity hole Buildroot leaves for custom versions, and image/check-kernel.sh asserts the built .config honours every fragment line and that the artifact is uncompressed. limactl shell kelyfos-dev -- make kernel

2026-08-22P1-2 First build attempt failed and the fix is worth recording, because it is a trap anyone bumping the kernel will hit: BR2_KERNEL_HEADERS_AS_KERNEL combined with a custom kernel version leaves Buildroot unable to infer its "headers are at least version X" symbol, and the build dies late with the memorable Incorrect selection of kernel headers: expected 2.6.x, got 6.18.x. The series selector BR2_PACKAGE_HOST_LINUX_HEADERS_CUSTOM_6_18 must be set too. Rather than hard-code it, the Makefile now derives the series from LINUX_VERSION (6.18.45 → 6_18) and templates it in, so the selector cannot drift from the pin; a kernel series this Buildroot does not list will fail loudly in check-config.sh rather than silently. before: >>> linux-headers 6.18.45 Installing to staging directory
  Incorrect selection of kernel headers: expected 2.6.x, got 6.18.x · EXIT=2
after: BR2_KERNEL_HEADERS_AS_KERNEL=y · BR2_PACKAGE_HOST_LINUX_HEADERS_CUSTOM_6_18=y · EXIT=0
2026-08-22P1-3 Guest boots and talks to the host. image/flavors/base/overlay/init is PID 1: it mounts /proc and /sys (the kernel already mounted devtmpfs on /dev, which is why init has a console at all), then puts a tmpfs-backed overlayfs over the read-only root and pivot_roots into it, carrying /dev, /proc and /sys across first. Two rules shape it: PID 1 never exits — no set -e, every step checked, and an idle loop as the last resort, because a dead init is a kernel panic that turns a small mistake into an unreadable failure — and it degrades loudly, falling back to a read-only root with tmpfs on /tmp and /run and reporting overlay:false to the host rather than failing mysteriously later. The vsock listener stub is the beginnings of the real supervisor (supervisor/main.go, static Go, cross-compiled into a generated rootfs overlay): it binds the exec port before announcing readiness, dials the host's ready channel with retry, heartbeats every 5 s, and answers exec with a well-formed "not implemented" exit frame — a host that receives a valid frame saying so can tell that from a crashed supervisor; a dropped connection cannot. Shared wire format in internal/proto, vsock in internal/vsock (decision D13). Also added: /etc/os-release identifying KelyfOS, and image/check-image.sh, which refuses to ship a rootfs missing /init or the supervisor. Booted under Firecracker v1.16.1 in the Lima VM, both directions of docs/protocol.md exercised.
guest → host (console + the host socket at <uds>_10100):

2026-08-22P1-4 make image is real: it cross-compiles the supervisor into a generated rootfs overlay, runs the full Buildroot build, verifies the kernel, and copies both artifacts out. Artifacts land in ~/.cache/kelyfos/out/<arch>/ on the Linux layer's own disk rather than in the repo — Firecracker reads the rootfs as a block device on every single boot, and P1-7 measures that boot in milliseconds, so putting it on the macOS virtiofs mount would be measuring the mount. The rootfs is deliberately sized at 64 MiB rather than left to Buildroot's default: the file is sparse, so it is a ceiling on what the guest may write, not a cost. image/check-image.sh enforces the contract on every build — both artifacts present, rootfs under the 200 MB budget, and /init plus /sbin/kelyfos-supervisor actually inside the filesystem, so a rootfs that would panic three minutes into a boot test fails at build time instead. limactl shell kelyfos-dev -- make image

==> rootfs contains /init and /sbin/kelyfos-supervisor

2026-08-22P1-5 kelyfos run exists. host/ holds the CLI, internal/sandbox the host side of a microVM: it resolves the image artifacts, creates a per-sandbox run directory, binds the host-side ready socket before Firecracker starts — not tidiness but correctness, since the guest dials out the moment the supervisor is up and a missing socket resets that connect — writes the machine config, launches firecracker --no-api --config-file, and waits for the ready frame. The boot time is measured on the host and never taken from the guest's clock. Two things learned from Firecracker's source are encoded in the boot arguments: root=/dev/vda and ro are omitted because Firecracker inserts them from the drive flags, and 8250.nr_uarts=0 from Firecracker's own default cmdline is dropped because it disables the serial port and the console is the only way to see why a guest died before the supervisor came up. Teardown runs from a defer on every exit path including the signal path: SIGTERM, SIGKILL after a grace period, then the run directory goes. A sandbox.json in the run directory lets a second process find the channels, which is what P1-6 needs. Firecracker runs in its own process group so a Ctrl-C to the foreground group cannot race the orderly shutdown. Makefile gained real cli, run, test and clean targets. limactl shell kelyfos-dev -- kelyfos run

2026-08-22P1-6 kelyfos exec works end to end, implementing docs/protocol.md §5.2 on both sides. Guest: one command per connection, its own process group so a timeout kills the whole tree rather than just the shell that spawned it; stdout and stderr streamed as base64 chunks through a serialising writer (interleaving between streams is allowed, a torn JSON line is not); both pumps drained to EOF before reaping, since reaping first would race them and truncate a command's last output; exactly one exit frame on every path, including every error path. Environment defaults to a fixed set rather than the supervisor's own — a sandbox that hands its process environment to guest commands is one leak away from handing over whatever the host put there. Host: the CONNECT handshake reads the acknowledgement one byte at a time, because a buffered reader would pull the following 4 KiB into its own buffer and swallow the start of channel data — a bug that only appears under timing you cannot reproduce. A closed connection with no exit frame is reported as a supervisor crash rather than papered over with an invented exit code. Shell wrapping happens on the host, in the open, so the audit log will show that a shell was involved. Protocol errors map to conventional exit statuses (timeout 124 as timeout(1), denied 126, not found 127) instead of a private numbering. make test is now real: 11 tests over the framing rules and the boot arguments, including one asserting that a 64 KiB chunk still fits a 1 MiB frame after base64 expansion. limactl shell kelyfos-dev -- kelyfos run & then kelyfos exec …
sandbox 09e770d7 ready in 982 ms
$ kelyfos exec "uname -a"

$ kelyfos exec "cat /etc/os-release" → NAME="KelyfOS" · ID=kelyfos · PRETTY_NAME="KelyfOS (base)" · exit=0
exit-code passthrough: exit 42 → 42 · stderr routed to the host's stderr, stdout to stdout
argv form: exec -- /bin/busybox echo "argv works" → argv works
piped stdin: printf … | kelyfos exec "cat" → hello from the host

The ≤ 500 ms phase target is not met on this machine. Machine shape is worth about 100 ms: 1 vCPU beats 2, and 128 MiB beats 512 MiB, but not enough to change the conclusion, and 128 MiB is too small to be a sensible default for real agent work. 10-run sets, best/median/worst:

2026-08-22P1-7 Why the target is missed, with evidence rather than a guess. Measured inside the guest against the Lima VM that hosts it: user-mode CPU is at parity (64 MiB md5 in 86 ms in the guest vs 121 ms in the VM), pure syscalls are effectively free, but virtio block I/O runs at about 77 MB/s (32 MiB read in 413 ms). So compute is native and the cost is concentrated in the virtualised device and MMIO path — which is exactly where nested virtualisation is expensive, since every L2 exit is serviced by Lima's hypervisor. The kernel's own boot log agrees: the 465 ms to reach userspace is spread thinly across many initcalls with no single hotspot above 70 ms, which is what uniformly-expensive traps look like rather than one slow driver. This is the situation the plan's own risk table anticipates — "nested-virt overhead means Mac-local timings are informational; Linux CI publishes the reference numbers" — so the reference measurement for the ≤ 500 ms claim belongs to make bench on bare Linux at P3-6, and these numbers stand as the dev-machine baseline. Surfacing it rather than burying it: the phase target is unmet on this platform and no v0.1 claim should quote 500 ms. guest vs its own host (L1), same workloads, host-side timing minus a 61 ms exec baseline:
CPU-bound 64 MiB md5 guest=86 ms L1=121 ms → parity
syscall-bound 400k ops guest≈0 ms L1=57 ms → no penalty
virtio-blk 32 MiB read guest=413 ms (~77 MB/s) → the bottleneck
dmesg gap analysis: largest single gap 0.070 s ("applying system-wide alternatives"), remainder spread across ~100 initcalls

The artifact is the uncompressed ELF vmlinux, 26 194 408 bytes, which is what Firecracker boots on x86_64 — not a bzImage — and the per-arch selection is asserted by image/check-kernel.sh on every build rather than assumed. The guest supervisor is cross-compiled to amd64 into the x86 rootfs by the same target. The two architectures build into separate trees and the aarch64 artifacts are untouched afterwards. 2026-08-22P2-0 TLS design gate closed: decision D6 recorded — selective TLS termination. Terminate only domains that have a secret bound to them; tunnel every other allowlisted domain untouched. The riskiest part was prototyped first rather than argued about: a throwaway Go proxy that generates a per-install CA, mints leaf certificates on demand, terminates CONNECT for a secret-bound domain, injects the credential, and forwards over a properly validated upstream TLS connection. The proof is the difference between two real GitHub responses — with injection the API answers "Bad credentials", meaning it received and parsed an Authorization header that the client was never given; without the proxy the same request gets "Requires authentication". Meanwhile a non-secret domain was tunnelled with the client validating the real public chain and no KelyfOS CA in sight. P2-5 and P2-6 now implement against a mechanism that is known to work rather than assumed to. spike (scratch, not committed): generates CA, mints leaves, terminates selectively
A. curl --proxy … --cacert kelyfos-ca.pem https://api.github.com/user  (no credential given to curl)
   → {"message": "Bad credentials", …} — the real server parsed an injected header
B. curl https://api.github.com/user  (control: no proxy, no credential)
   → {"message": "Requires authentication", …} — different message proves A's header arrived
C. curl --proxy … https://example.com/  (no --cacert at all)
   → http_code=200 ssl_verify_result=0 — tunnelled, real chain validated, no MITM
proxy log: TERMINATE api.github.com:443 (secret bound) · injected Authorization for GET /user · TUNNEL example.com:443
2026-08-22P2-1 The supervisor is PID 1. The phase-1 /init shell script is deleted and the kernel command line now says init=/sbin/kelyfos-supervisor, so a single static Go binary is the whole of userspace management: mounts and the tmpfs overlay (ported from shell into supervisor/mount.go, keeping the degrade-loudly behaviour), orphan reaping, the exec channel, the control channel, and an orderly power-off. The reaping is the part worth explaining. The obvious Go implementation — a goroutine calling wait4(-1) — is wrong, because os/exec's Cmd.Wait waits on its own child too and whichever call wins steals the status from the other; the symptom is intermittent "waitid: no child processes" on commands that ran perfectly. So there is exactly one waiter in the process: supervisor/reaper.go owns every wait, callers register a pid and read its status from a channel, and exec.go therefore builds its own pipes rather than using StdoutPipe, which is wired to Cmd.Wait. Control channel (§5.4) answers ping and shutdown, and says plainly that resync waits for P3-1 rather than pretending. Shutdown is now a request, not a power cut: the CLI asks over the control channel, the guest terminates children, syncs and calls reboot(POWER_OFF), and only if that fails does the CLI fall back to signalling Firecracker — which matters because a sandbox that is always power-cut can never promise a workspace was written back cleanly (P3-10). Boot-to-ready improved to 794 ms from 885 ms: the shell init, its ten BusyBox forks and its console writes are simply gone. limactl shell kelyfos-dev — sandbox 921563ab ready in 794 ms (vmm 249 + guest 545), supervisor 0.2.0-p2
1) exec "cat /proc/1/comm" → kelyfos-supervi · /proc/1/exe → /.oldroot/sbin/kelyfos-supervisor
2) exec "ls /init" → No such file or directory — the script is gone
3) /proc/mounts → overlay / overlay rw,…,lowerdir=/,upperdir=/mnt/upper,workdir=/mnt/work · proc · sysfs · devpts
4) orphan a child, wait, then ps → zombie count 0
5) hostname & uname -n → kelyfos (set by the supervisor, no init system involved)
6) control channel over CONNECT 10003 → handshake OK 1073741824
   ping → {"v":1,"id":"ping","ok":true}
   resync → ok:false, "resync is not implemented until P3-1"
   bogus → ok:false, "unknown control op bogus"
7) shutdown → guest console: "kelyfos-supervisor: shutting down" then "[ 0.669214] reboot: Power down" — the guest powered itself off; firecracker processes 0, run dirs 0
2026-08-22P2-0 D6 endorsed by John, with two binding implementation requirements folded into the decision row rather than raised as a new one: the egress CA is generated per run and never persisted (only the trust anchor reaches the guest, so a restart invalidates it), and the flight recorder must distinguish terminated from tunnelled on every egress event so a user can prove which traffic the proxy could read. The certificate-pinning limitation for secret-bound domains is to be documented in docs/networking.md. These are conditions on P2-5 and P2-6. decision log D6, amended in place with the requirements as stated

2026-08-22P2-1 The concurrency regression test John asked for found two real bugs, both now fixed. (1) A fatal window between fork and registration. The reaper registered a child's waiter after cmd.Start() returned; a short-lived command could exit inside that window, be reaped by the SIGCHLD handler, find no waiter, and have its status dropped — leaving the caller blocked forever on a channel nobody would ever write to. It hung the very first run of the test for thirteen minutes while the guest sat there answering control pings perfectly, which is exactly the invisible-under-light-load, reliable-under-concurrency failure the test exists to expose. Fixed by making start-and-register a single operation under the reaper's lock, so a concurrent drain cannot run until the waiter exists. (2) An arbitrary vsock listen backlog. The guest listened with a backlog of 16; 24 simultaneous CONNECTs overflowed it, and Firecracker answers a connection it cannot complete by closing the socket — which the host reported as "is the supervisor listening?" when the supervisor was listening fine. Fixed on both sides: backlog raised to 128, and the host now retries a closed handshake within its own timeout, since that same signal also means "the guest is not up yet". docs/protocol.md §1.1 now states both causes and makes the retry a MUST. Kept as instructed: the single-waiter design and the hand-built pipes. make test-integration (24 concurrent execs, orphan reaping, exit codes and 512 KiB of output):
before fix 1: TestConcurrentExecs hung > 13 min with the guest still answering control pings
after fix 1, before fix 2: --- FAIL: TestConcurrentExecs (1.18s), 7 of 24 workers "no acknowledgement for CONNECT 10001"

2026-08-22P2-2 MCP server live on vsock port 10002 with all six tools: exec, read_file, write_file, list_dir, upload, download. Framing is the MCP stdio transport's framing unchanged, which is what makes the P2-3 bridge a pass-through. internal/mcp holds the JSON-RPC envelope, supervisor/mcp.go the session, supervisor/tools.go the tools. Streaming uses MCP's own mechanism rather than an invention: when a caller supplies a _meta.progressToken, every chunk of output becomes a notifications/progress, interleaved with responses under the session's write lock. exec takes either command (run through /bin/sh -c) or argv (no shell), and the shell choice is made in the open so the audit log will show it. The error split follows the spec's reasoning exactly: a command exiting non-zero is a tool error — isError: true with the status in structuredContent — because the spec reserves that for "actionable feedback that language models can use to self-correct", while a malformed request is a JSON-RPC error. Both raw exec and the MCP tool now share one runCommand, so they cannot drift on environments, timeouts or exit statuses. Per-call file size is capped at 8 MiB — not a security boundary, but a tool that reads a 10 GB file into a JSON string is a way to kill the guest by accident. Real session over CONNECT 10002 against a running guest (sandbox 36fce689, ready in 807 ms):
handshake: OK 1073741824
initialize → protocolVersion=2025-11-25 serverInfo={"name":"kelyfos","title":"KelyfOS sandbox","version":"0.2.0-p2"}
tools/list → exec, read_file, write_file, list_dir, upload, download
exec → "Linux kelyfos 6.18.45 #6 SMP … aarch64 GNU/Linux"
exec "exit 7" → isError=True structuredContent={"exit_code":7,…}
write_file + list_dir /work → "file 12 demo.txt" · read_file → "hello agent\n"
read_file /no/such/file → isError=True "stat /no/such/file: no such file or directory"
Integration tests: --- PASS: TestMCPSession (0.97s) — initialize, ping, the six tools, a binary upload/download round trip with non-UTF-8 bytes, and the tool-error vs protocol-error split; --- PASS: TestMCPExecStreams (1.58s) — output arrives as ≥2 progress notifications carrying the caller's token, proving it streams rather than returning only at the end
2026-08-22P2-3 kelyfos mcp bridges an MCP client's standard streams to a running sandbox's MCP channel. It is a byte copier and nothing more: both ends already speak newline-delimited JSON-RPC, so there is nothing to translate, and translating anyway would only create somewhere for messages to be reordered, re-chunked or dropped. Its single protocol responsibility is consuming Firecracker's OK <port> acknowledgement and never letting it reach stdout — the specification says a server "MUST NOT write anything to its stdout that is not a valid MCP message", and that handshake line is the one chance the bridge has to break it. Diagnostics go to stderr, which the spec reserves for logging and clients are required not to treat as failure. Closing stdin half-closes the vsock connection so the guest sees EOF and the bridge exits, rather than both sides waiting on a peer that will never speak again. Attaching to a sandbox needs no arguments: it finds the only running one, or names them if there are several. Driven as a subprocess exactly as an MCP client launches it ({"command":"kelyfos","args":["mcp"]}):
initialize -> 2025-11-25 kelyfos
tools/list -> 6 tools: exec, read_file, write_file, list_dir, upload, download
exec -> "bridged\naarch64"
write_file -> "wrote 14 bytes to /work/via-bridge.txt" · read_file -> "through stdio\n"
stdin closed -> bridge exit code 0 · stderr -> "kelyfos: attached to sandbox 5a0ee4ae"
--- PASS: TestMCPBridge (0.94s) — launches the real binary and parses every stdout line as JSON, so a leaked handshake acknowledgement fails the test instead of confusing a client later
2026-08-22P2-4 Flight recorder live. docs/events.md defines the versioned schema first, because the plan is right that the schema is the product and the viewers are replaceable — it pins the canonical field order the hash depends on, and states plainly what the chain does and does not buy. internal/recorder implements it: append-only JSONL at ~/.cache/kelyfos/sessions/<id>/events.jsonl, deliberately outside the run directory because that is deleted when the sandbox stops and the record must outlive the thing it describes. Every event carries the previous event's digest. Events are written by the host, never the guest — a guest that could write its own audit trail could write a flattering one. kelyfos run records the session lifecycle, kelyfos exec records every command and its output, and the MCP bridge records tool calls by tee: each direction is copied through byte-for-byte while a duplicate is parsed, so P2-3's pass-through guarantee survives. File writes record path, size and content digest — never the content, because a recorder that copies every byte an agent writes is a second copy of the workspace in a worse place. kelyfos log replays, --follow streams, --verify checks the chain, --list shows sessions and --json emits raw events for the P3-8 and P3-9 viewers. Full session replay (kelyfos log):
 session start image=base arch=aarch64 · ready 769 ms kernel=6.18.45 overlay=true
 $ /bin/sh -c echo recorded-via-exec | recorded-via-exec | exit 0 66 ms
 $ /bin/sh -c exit 3 → exit 3 · $ …echo bridged; uname -m (via the MCP bridge) → bridged / aarch64
 write /work/via-bridge.txt 14 bytes sha256=5a83f505977a via=write_file · session end interrupted after 995 ms
--verify → session ce55e7ca: chain intact, 12 events verified · exit=0
flip one byte (recorded-via-exec → recorded-via-EXEC) → FAILED after 2 events: "event 3 has been modified — its contents hash to 7634730205c0, but it carries bd15b1166b25" · exit=1
delete an event → FAILED: "line 5 has seq 6 — the chain has a gap or was reordered" · exit=1
restore → chain intact, 12 events verified · exit=0
--follow streamed events live as two commands ran · --list → ce55e7ca … 12 events interrupted
Unit tests: chain verifies; every single-event edit detected; deletion detected; reordering detected; reopening continues the chain; a secret value never appears in the file
2026-08-22P2-4 Two bugs found and fixed while building this, both of the kind that only appear in real use. (1) The audit log could never have verified. A session is written by three separate processes — kelyfos run holds the sandbox open while exec and mcp are separate invocations — and each held its own sequence number and previous hash, so their appends interleaved into a chain that broke on the first concurrent write. The first end-to-end run caught it: "line 12 has seq 3". An audit log that cannot verify is worse than none, because it looks like one. Fixed by making every append take an exclusive flock and re-read whatever other writers added since this process last looked, with a regression test that runs six concurrent writers appending 150 events and verifies the result. (2) kelyfos exec hung forever whenever stdin was an inherited pipe — which is every script, cron job and CI step. It read stdin whenever stdin was not a terminal; an inherited pipe is not a terminal and never closes. Stdin is now opt-in behind --stdin, the same way docker exec requires -i. before (1): kelyfos log --verify → FAILED after 11 events, "line 12 has seq 3 — the chain has a gap or was reordered"
after (1): chain intact, 12 events verified; TestConcurrentWritersKeepOneChain (6 writers × 25 events) → PASS, 150 events verified
before (2): wchan=pipe_read, /proc/<pid>/fd/0 → pipe:[717697], exec never returned
after (2): kelyfos exec "echo no-more-hang" → no-more-hang, exit=0 with a pipe on stdin; printf … | kelyfos exec --stdin "cat" → piped input, exit=0
2026-08-22P2-5 Egress implemented, off by default. A sandbox with no --allow has no network interface at all — not a firewalled one, not an empty allowlist — so there is no rule that has to hold for "no egress" to be true. --allow builds the path in the only safe order: TAP first, then the proxy bound on the host's TAP address, then the nftables table that makes the proxy the only reachable destination, and only then a machine that can send a packet. docs/networking.md carries the exact rule template and calls out the trap that would bite anyone writing it from scratch: the base chains use policy accept and drop by iifname, because a base chain on the input hook with a drop policy filters all traffic to the host, not just the sandbox's — which on a developer's machine means locking yourself out of your own box the first time you run kelyfos. The NIC is configured by the kernel from ip= (CONFIG_IP_PNP), so nothing in the guest has to be trusted to bring the network up, and the supervisor reads kelyfos.proxy= from the same command line and exports it in both letter cases — curl reads the lowercase form, Go and the JVM read the uppercase, and setting one gives you a sandbox where half the tools have egress. Every attempt is recorded with its verdict, reason and mode. Per decision D16 the guest gets no DNS at all: the proxy resolves, so DNS is not load-bearing for permitted traffic, and removing it closes DNS tunnelling — the one exfiltration channel a hostname allowlist structurally cannot see. A. no allowlist → "egress none (no network interface)"; guest reports 0 eth0 interfaces
B. --allow neverssl.com → "egress neverssl.com via proxy on 169.254.206.61:38965"
  host: kelyfos338f98ba UP 169.254.206.61/30; nft table exactly as documented
  guest: eth0 169.254.139.218/30, MAC 02:00:62:f6:ad:4a (derived from the sandbox id), configured by the kernel before userspace
  env: HTTP_PROXY, HTTPS_PROXY, http_proxy, https_proxy, NO_PROXY, no_proxy all set
  allowed: wget http://neverssl.com → <title>NeverSSL - Connecting …</title>
  blocked: wget http://example.com → wget: server returned error: HTTP/1.1 403 Forbidden
  wrong port: http://neverssl.com:22 → 403, recorded as port_not_allowed
  bypass attempt: wget -Y off http://1.1.1.1/ → "download timed out" — silently dropped, and the nft drop counter climbed to 10 packets / 1232 bytes
C. recorder: "egress allowed neverssl.com:80 mode=tunnelled" and "egress BLOCKED example.com:80 not_in_allowlist"; --verify → chain intact, 25 events
D. teardown: no kelyfos TAP left, no kelyfos nft table left
Unit tests cover the allowlist near-misses that matter: notgithub.com, github.com.evil.net and githubXcom are all refused where api.github.com is allowed; default ports are 80 and 443 only
2026-08-22P3-3 dev flavor built, pulled forward per decision D17 because Phase 2 cannot be closed without it. Build trees are now per architecture and flavor, and each flavor owns its rootfs size in image/flavors/<flavor>/buildroot.fragment. Contents: OpenSSL, a real CA bundle, git, curl, python3 — the same guarantees as base (read-only root, no login, MCP-only interface, deny-all egress) with the tools an agent needs to fetch a repository, edit it and run its tests. Nothing was added to base, which remains BusyBox and musl with no TLS client at all. Deliberately not added: bash, diffutils and patch — Buildroot hides packages BusyBox already provides, and a second copy of each would be weight for nothing. Sized at 128 MiB after measuring: the toolbox is 59 MB, the root is read-only and every write goes to the tmpfs overlay, so the image only has to hold content — my first attempt reserved 512 MiB for writes that can never land there. limactl shell kelyfos-dev -- make image FLAVOR=dev

2026-08-22P2-6 Secret injection working, implementing decision D6 as amended. --secret NAME@domain reads the value from the host environment; the proxy terminates TLS only for that domain, attaches Authorization, and forwards over a properly validated upstream connection. Every other allowlisted domain is tunnelled untouched, so the proxy sees plaintext for exactly the domains a credential was deliberately bound to. The CA is minted per run, lives only in memory, is never persisted, and only its public anchor crosses into the guest — over the new trust control op, because the CA is per-run and the rootfs is read-only. The guest points SSL_CERT_FILE, CURL_CA_BUNDLE, REQUESTS_CA_BUNDLE, NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS and GIT_SSL_CAINFO at it, which is load-bearing rather than thorough: Python's certifi and Node's bundled roots ignore the system trust store entirely. A Secret cannot render its value through any format verb, and the recorder writes the name and destination only — a hash of a short credential is a credential. kelyfos run --image dev --allow github.com --secret GITHUB_TOKEN@api.github.com
  secret GITHUB_TOKEN -> api.github.com (Bearer; the value stays on the host)
1. authenticated call: curl https://api.github.com/user →
  {"message": "Bad credentials", …, "status": "401"} in 0.65 s, rc=0
  GitHub parsed an Authorization header the guest was never given — without one it answers "Requires authentication" instead
2. the token is nowhere in the guest: env → 0 occurrences; grep -rl over /etc /root /work /tmp → nothing
3. non-secret domain: curl https://github.com/ → 200 with ssl_verify_result=0 — the real chain validated, no MITM, no KelyfOS CA involved
4. git clone over HTTPS: git clone --depth 1 https://github.com/octocat/Hello-World → README reads "Hello World!"
5. blocked host: curl https://example.com → curl: (7) CONNECT tunnel failed, response 403
6. recorder: secret GITHUB_TOKEN -> api.github.com · egress allowed api.github.com:443 mode=terminated · egress allowed github.com:443 mode=tunnelled · egress BLOCKED example.com:443 not_in_allowlist · --verify → chain intact
Hermetic tests: the upstream server receives the credential while the client is given none; a domain with no secret is tunnelled with its real certificate validated; a Secret never renders its value through %v, %s or %+v
2026-08-22P2-6 One bug found by testing and fixed before ticking. The first authenticated call returned the whole JSON body and then hung until curl's 20 s timeout. Cause: Go's default transport adds Accept-Encoding: gzip and transparently decompresses the reply — convenient for a client, wrong for a proxy, because it discards the original Content-Length and leaves a response that can only be framed by closing the connection, while the termination loop sat waiting for another request. Fixed by disabling compression on the upstream leg so bytes pass through framed as the server sent them, and by closing when a response's length really is indeterminate. Same request now completes in 0.65 s. before: {"message":"Bad credentials",…} then "curl: (28) Operation timed out after 20011 milliseconds with 112 bytes received"
after: same body, real 0m 0.65s, rc=0

2026-08-22P2-4 Defect the acceptance test exposed, fixed straight after. Command output was recorded one event per pipe read, and a pipe read returns whatever is available — for an unbuffered writer like curl's error output that is often a single byte. "curl: (7) CONNECT tunnel failed, response 403" became 28 events, one per character: a log that is unreadable, needlessly long, and whose hash chain is mostly noise. Output is now coalesced before it reaches the recorder, flushing on a stream switch, at 8 KiB, or at exit. Coalescing happens on the recording side rather than on the wire, so output still streams to the terminal immediately — a person watching a command wants it live, a person reading the record wants it whole. before: 28 command.output events for one 43-character stderr line, one per byte
after: the same command produces 1; a whole session with two commands → 2 command.output events, chain intact, 10 events verified
replay now reads: "! curl: (7) CONNECT tunnel failed, response 403" on one line
2026-08-22P3-1 kelyfos snapshot save|restore working, with the clock and entropy resync of decision D19. Firecracker is now always started with its API socket rather than --no-api: pause, create, load and resume all go through it, and a sandbox that turns out not to be snapshottable because of how it was started is a surprise nobody wants at the moment they need it. save pauses, writes state and memory, and resumes — the source sandbox keeps running, and a failed snapshot costs a snapshot rather than the session. restore loads with resume_vm: false and a per-restore vsock_override, binds the host channels while the guest is still frozen, then resumes and resyncs; resuming first would let the guest run for a moment believing a stale clock, which is exactly the window that produces duplicate "random" values across forks. Snapshots live outside the run directory, which is deleted on teardown. kelyfos snapshot save --name demo → saved from sandbox b2655118 in 210 ms
  state 10 971 bytes · memory 536 870 912 bytes · the sandbox is still running
kelyfos snapshot restore --name demo → sandbox 880ced86 restored, clock and entropy resynced
  prepared state survived: cat /work/prepared/marker.txt → prepared-before-snapshot
  clock: guest 12:23:44 == host 12:23:44 (the snapshot was minutes old)
  entropy: source VM 3d0232fc03b7197ee33977c32a5bec33 → restored f2a356f125cdcbc6ba762250b6610693 — diverged, which is the whole point for forks
restore, 10 runs, local: min 124 · median 130 · p95 135 · max 135 ms (aarch64, dev, 2 vcpu / 512 MiB)
2026-08-22P3-1 The first restore number I measured was 3 ms, and it was wrong. Timing stopped at resume() — a machine that is running but not yet known to answer. Measuring through the resync round trip, which is a real vsock handshake and RPC to the guest and therefore proof it is usable, gives 130 ms median locally. That makes "restore" mean the same thing "boot-to-ready" does. Recorded because the flattering number was available, plausible, and would have been quoted. Per decision D15 as extended, the binding measurement for the ≤ 100 ms target is the bare-KVM reference runner, not this machine; .github/workflows/bench.yml now measures restore alongside cold boot with the same n ≥ 10, median and p95. measured to resume(): 3 ms — machine running, guest not yet proven to answer
measured through resync: 130 ms median (n=10, min 124, p95 135) — guest answering
One earlier failure worth noting: the first restore attempt returned "guest refused resync: resync is not implemented until P3-1" — the snapshot had been taken from a guest running the pre-resync supervisor, which is a neat demonstration that a snapshot carries its userland with it

2026-08-22P3-2 kelyfos fork -n N restores one snapshot into N independent sandboxes, concurrently, each with its own run directory and vsock_override. The mechanism is exactly as the plan describes and now demonstrated rather than asserted: one rootfs image and one memory file serve every fork, divergent writes live in the in-guest tmpfs overlay which is part of guest memory, and each fork maps the memory snapshot privately so the kernel provides page-level copy-on-write for nothing. Every fork is resynced on resume, which is the load-bearing part — without it four forks of one snapshot would produce four identical streams of "random" bytes (decision D19). Teardown stops the whole fleet on any exit path, including the one taken when some forks failed: a half-forked fleet left running is worse than none. Forks are vsock-only as the plan specifies. Scope note, stated rather than glossed: the per-fork workspace copy and its free-disk pre-check are not implemented here, because there is no workspace disk to copy until P3-10 creates one. That half of P3-2 lands with P3-10. parent: sandbox dcad90b9, wrote /work/shared/base.txt, snapshot save --name fleet (198 ms)
kelyfos fork --name fleet -n 4 →
  fork 1/4 ebbd6ea9 in 502 ms · 2/4 132e6eb3 in 533 ms · 3/4 89a19c8d in 485 ms · 4/4 3c964832 in 519 ms
  4 forks live in 534 ms wall clock (concurrent, so the fleet costs about what one costs)
every fork inherited the parent state and then diverged:
  fork 1: inherited=from-the-parent own=fork-1-was-here entropy=32d08d71438e
  fork 2: inherited=from-the-parent own=fork-2-was-here entropy=0b6a0c0cd6a0
  fork 3: inherited=from-the-parent own=fork-3-was-here entropy=fe6b95e1dee8
  fork 4: inherited=from-the-parent own=fork-4-was-here entropy=6208625f4050
  re-reading, each fork sees only its own write — four different entropy streams, which is precisely what the resync exists to produce
parents unchanged, proved by checksum: snapshots/fleet/memory sha256 b5d4f95255e5… and state f2cd0e955c63… are byte-identical before and after four forks wrote to their filesystems
restoring the same snapshot afterwards: /work/shared/base.txt present, fork.txt present? no-clean — no fork's writes leaked back
host cost: 1 memory file (512 MiB), 1 state file (10 971 B), 4 firecracker processes, 36 KB of run directories in total — no per-fork copies anywhere
2026-08-22P3-4 E2B-compatible REST subset in shim/, served by kelyfos shim, documented as a subset in docs/e2b-shim.md. Implemented: sandbox create / list / kill, envd /health, and binary-safe file read and write — enough that E2B code which creates sandboxes and moves files works unchanged against a self-hosted box. Every Sandbox.create() boots a real Firecracker microVM with the same guarantees as any other KelyfOS sandbox. Deliberately not implemented: command execution. The current SDK does not use REST for it at all — commands.run() goes over Connect RPC with protobuf, including server streaming, which is a different protocol stack and would mean vendoring E2B's schema and tracking it forever. That is precisely the burden a subset exists to avoid, so it returns 501 with an explanation pointing at kelyfos mcp rather than a 404 that reads like a bug. The shim also refuses to adopt sandboxes it did not create: an SDK call ending someone's interactive session because the lifecycle said so is a surprise worth designing out. Smoke-tested with the real E2B Python SDK 2.45.1 pointed at the shim via E2B_API_URL and E2B_SANDBOX_URL:
  Sandbox.create() → sandbox id 993e3e26 (a real microVM)
  sbx.files.write / sbx.files.read → round-tripped 'hello from the E2B SDK\n'
  binary round trip → 0001fffe414280 survived exactly
  sbx.kill() → sandbox stopped
  OK — E2B SDK round trip against the KelyfOS shim
shim request log: POST /sandboxes · POST+GET /files (text) · POST+GET /files (binary) · DELETE /sandboxes/993e3e26
One SDK behaviour worth recording: it validates the shape of E2B_API_KEY client-side before sending anything, so a self-hosted shim still needs a key that looks like an E2B key even though nothing checks it
2026-08-22P3-10 kelyfos run --workspace ./dir packs a host directory into an ext4 image, attaches it as the second virtio-blk drive, and the supervisor mounts it at /work. The device is pinned to /dev/vdb on the kernel command line rather than discovered — the host attached the disks, so it should say which is which rather than leave the guest guessing. Sizing is 2× the directory with a 1 GiB floor: a workspace sized exactly to its contents leaves the guest no room to build anything in it. Neither packing nor unpacking needs privileges: mke2fs -d populates the image straight from the directory and debugfs rdump reads it back without mounting, so the local-files path never asks a developer for sudo. On clean shutdown the image is written back, and --no-sync-back opts out. If the host directory changed while the sandbox was running it is not overwritten — the results go to <dir>.kelyfos-out and the user is told — because silently discarding an edit someone made in their editor is the most destructive thing this tool could do. A. clean round trip — host dir with README.md, src/calc.py, src/obsolete.txt
  guest: /dev/vdb /work ext4 rw,nosuid,nodev — edited README, created src/mul.py, deleted obsolete.txt
  after shutdown: "workspace written back to …/ws-demo"
  README.md carries both lines · src/mul.py present with its content · obsolete.txt gone (correct) — the image is authoritative, so a deletion is a deletion
  no stray .kelyfos-* directories left behind
B. host directory edited during the run
  "the host directory changed while the sandbox was running, so it was NOT overwritten. results written to …/ws-demo.kelyfos-out instead"
  host README.md still reads "the user edited this in their editor", and their urgent.txt survives
  the sandbox's own README.md and new.txt are in .kelyfos-out
2026-08-22P3-10 A silent-corruption bug found by testing the round trip and worth recording, because the failure looked like success. The first sync-back wrote debugfs rdump straight into the host directory, and rdump refuses to descend into a subdirectory that already exists — it prints "File exists while making directory", carries on, and exits 0. The result: top-level files looked correctly updated while every nested file kept its pre-run contents, and a file the agent created inside src/ never arrived at all. Fixed by dumping into a fresh empty directory and swapping it into place, keeping the previous copy until the new one is in position. reproduced directly: rdump into an empty dir → all files correct; rdump into a non-empty dir →
  "rdump: File exists while making directory /tmp/out2//src", exit 0
  top.txt → "top" (updated) but src/nested.txt → "stale" (not updated)
symptom in the real run: README.md synced, src/mul.py missing entirely
after the fix: all three cases correct, including deletion
2026-08-22P3-2 The half of P3-2 deferred at the time — per-fork workspace copies and the free-disk pre-check — is now delivered, since P3-10 created the workspace disk it needed. A snapshot now carries a meta.json and a copy of the workspace image, taken while the VM is paused so it is consistent with the memory image. kelyfos fork refuses up front if N copies will not fit, then gives each fork its own copy with cp --reflink=auto --sparse=always and repoints its drive before the machine resumes. A Firecracker constraint shaped the design and is worth recording: a snapshot will not load at all unless every block device's backing file exists at the path stored inside it — PATCH /drives is post-boot only, so the drive can only be swapped after the load succeeds. The original path is therefore restaged from the snapshot's captured copy purely so the load has something to open, and each fork swaps in its own copy before resume, with no guest I/O in between. kelyfos snapshot save on a sandbox with --workspace →
  snapshot dir holds memory, state, meta.json, workspace.ext4
  meta.json: has_workspace true, workspace_path …/workspaces/b530da4e.ext4, workspace_size 1073741824
kelyfos fork --name wsfleet -n 3 →
  "each fork gets its own copy of a 1073741824 byte workspace disk"
  fork 1 751f461e 358 ms · fork 2 cd22dca1 340 ms · fork 3 b02d7aff 355 ms · 3 forks live in 359 ms
  fork 1: inherited=shared starting point own=fork-1-private
  fork 2: inherited=shared starting point own=fork-2-private
  fork 3: inherited=shared starting point own=fork-3-private
  three separate 1 GiB images on the host — nominal 3.1 GB, actually 20 MB on disk, because the copies are sparse
The failure that led here, before the restaging fix: "Load snapshot error: … Block: Virtio backend error: Error manipulating the backing file: No such file or directory … workspaces/a4c20e37.ext4" — the parent's image had been removed at shutdown, and Firecracker wants it back before it will load
2026-08-22P3-1 Provisional bare-KVM reference, both numbers: cold boot median 90 ms (p95 95), snapshot restore median 29 ms (p95 35), n=10 each, x86_64, base flavor, 2 vCPU / 512 MiB, measured by make bench on the P3-6 reference environment per decision D15 as extended. Both targets are met on reference hardware: the ≤ 100 ms Phase 3 restore target with room to spare, and the ≤ 300 ms v1.0 cold-boot claim by a factor of three. Cold boot also improved from the 103 ms measured earlier in the day — the kernel trim and moving PID 1 from a shell script to the supervisor both landed in between. The local-versus-reference gap is now measured on two independent numbers rather than one: boot 899 ms local against 90 ms bare-KVM, restore 130 ms local against 29 ms. A tenfold and a fourfold difference on the same binaries is what D15 exists to account for, and is why no target is judged on the dev machine. gh workflow run bench.yml → run 32572967222, conclusion success

2026-08-22P3-11 kelyfos doctor checks eight things and, for each failure, prints the exact command that fixes it — tailored to the host flavor it detected, because "install Lima" is useless on a bare server and wsl --update is nonsense on a Mac. It detects Lima (hostname prefix or the cidata mount), WSL2 (/proc/version), bare Linux and macOS. It builds and runs on macOS too, which matters because it is the first command a new user runs and on a Mac they have no Linux layer yet — refusing to start would be precisely the wrong response. The Firecracker check compares against the version pinned in versions.mk, stamped into the binary at build time so the number is not typed twice. KVM is checked by using it — opening the node proves permission, the KVM_GET_API_VERSION ioctl proves it is a hypervisor rather than just a device file. Exit status is non-zero on failure, so it is usable in a script. On the dev machine: all eight checks pass — platform Lima VM · /dev/kvm usable, KVM API version 12 · firecracker v1.16.1 (matches versions.mk) · /dev/net/tun · ip, nft and privileges · mke2fs, debugfs · guest image present · disk space
Negative test — firecracker moved aside, Image removed, PATH narrowed:
  FAIL firecracker "not on PATH" → bash dev/install-firecracker.sh
  FAIL egress tooling "missing nft" · FAIL workspace tooling "missing mke2fs, debugfs"
  FAIL guest image "missing Image" → make image ARCH=aarch64
  and the disk check correctly switched threshold: "want 25 GiB to build a guest from source"
On macOS: stops at the platform check with the Lima instructions and "then run kelyfos doctor again inside the Linux layer", rather than emitting seven Linux fixes a Mac user cannot act on
2026-08-22P3-11 Two things the doctor found about its own design while being written. It first reported a disk failure on a machine that was working perfectly: the threshold was a single 20 GiB number, but building a guest from source needs tens of gigabytes of toolchain and kernel trees while running a built one needs room for snapshots and little else. Quoting the wrong one turns a check into noise, so the threshold now depends on whether the images already exist — 25 GiB to build, 5 GiB to run. And on macOS it originally printed seven failures whose fixes were modprobe kvm_intel and apt install; it now stops at the platform check, because a doctor that hands you seven wrong instructions is worse than one that hands you the single right one. before: "13.7 GiB free … want at least 20 GiB" on a machine that ran sandboxes fine
after: "16.9 GiB free … (enough to run (snapshots and workspace copies))" — and 25 GiB demanded only when the images are missing
macOS before: 7 failures, 6 of them with Linux-only advice · after: 1 failure with the Lima path
2026-08-22P3-12 Policy as code: kelyfos.toml declares image, arch, allowlist, secret names, workspace and resource limits, and bare kelyfos run picks it up. It is found by walking up from the working directory the way git finds its repository root, so running kelyfos from a subdirectory of a project behaves as expected, and a relative workspace is resolved against the file rather than the shell — the file describes its own project wherever it is invoked from. Explicit flags always win, which needs knowing which flags were actually typed rather than which have values, or a default would be indistinguishable from a choice and the file could never override anything. Secret values are refused, not merely discouraged: this file gets committed, so a value in one gets committed with it. Unknown keys and sections are errors naming the file and line, because a policy file with a typo that silently does nothing is worse than one that refuses — it looks applied and is not. The parser is deliberately hand-written: a flat table of scalars and string arrays does not justify carrying a TOML dependency into a security tool. project with kelyfos.toml declaring image=dev, allow=[github.com], workspace=".", vcpus=2, mem_mib=768
bare kelyfos run from proj/src (a subdirectory) →
  policy: /home/…/proj/kelyfos.toml
  egress github.com via proxy · workspace /home/…/proj -> /work (resolved against the file, not the cwd)
  in-guest: nproc → 2 · free → 738 MiB · /work/src/main.py present · KelyfOS (dev)
override: kelyfos run --vcpus 1 → in-guest nproc → 1
refusal: secrets = ["GITHUB_TOKEN=ghp_realvalue@…"] → "secrets must name an environment variable and a domain (NAME@domain), never a value"
refusal: allwo → "kelyfos.toml:2: unknown key \"allwo\""
9 unit tests including the secret-value refusal, unknown keys and sections, errors naming the line, a # inside a quoted string, and Find walking up
2026-08-22P3-8 kelyfos log --export session.html renders a session as a single self-contained file: no server, no scripts, no external requests — a compliance artefact that needs a CDN to render is not one. It opens standalone in a browser and prints sensibly. The top of the report answers the question a reader actually has before the timeline does: counts of commands, failures, files written, egress allowed and blocked, plus how many connections were TLS-terminated and therefore readable by the proxy, and which secrets were used by name. Output is attached to the command it belongs to rather than listed separately, because a transcript where output floats away from its command is not a transcript. The report verifies its own source as part of rendering and says so at the top — a report that cannot tell you whether its evidence has been edited is worth very little as evidence. It is a pure view over the JSONL of docs/events.md: the schema is the product, this is replaceable. kelyfos log --session … --export session.html → wrote session.html (25 events, 8031 bytes)
self-containment check: 0 external references, script tags, @import or fetch calls
content check: contains the egress events, a BLOCKED attempt, "secret GITHUB_TOKEN", a file write, and a command that exited 4
the fake token used in the session does not appear anywhere in the report
banner on an intact log: "✓ audit chain intact — 25 events verified"
after flipping one byte in the source JSONL: "✗ audit chain FAILED — event 13 has been modified — its contents hash to cbbdde7a9767, but it carries 171f4baf47f7", and the CLI warns on export rather than writing a clean-looking report over a dirty log
2026-08-22P3-9 kelyfos watch is a live single-sandbox TUI (bubbletea + lipgloss, as the plan specifies): a header with the sandbox and its state, a status line carrying uptime, boot time, command and failure counts, files written and egress allowed/blocked counters, and a streaming event lane with commands, their output, stderr, egress verdicts and secret uses each styled distinctly. It reads the flight recorder and nothing else — it never opens a channel to the guest, never sends a command, and quitting leaves the sandbox running. That is decision D7's line held exactly: viewers read the JSONL, managers change things, and this is a viewer. It tails from the beginning of the file, so attaching part-way through a session still shows everything that happened before. The line buffer is capped at 500 — an unbounded slice in a long-running session is a slow leak nobody notices until it matters. Driven in a pty at 110×26 so it renders exactly as a person sees it, while a script ran work in the sandbox. Final frame:
KelyfOS sandbox ad1339c7 dev · aarch64 running
uptime 4s · boot 840 ms · 4 commands (2 failed) · 0 files · egress 1 ok / 1 blocked · 0 secret uses
  ready 840 ms · kernel 6.18.45
  $ /bin/sh -c cd /work && git clone … Hello-World
  egress github.com:443 · tunnelled → exit 0 · 1616 ms
  $ /bin/sh -c curl … https://example.com
  BLOCKED example.com:443 · not_in_allowlist → ! curl: (7) CONNECT tunnel failed, response 403
  exit 7 · then exit 5 — counters updated live
after sending q: kelyfos exec "echo sandbox-still-alive" → sandbox-still-alive — quitting the viewer does not touch the sandbox
2026-08-22P3-5 docs/threat-model.md written and linked from the README, which is the launch gate this task exists to be. It states the design assumption plainly — anything inside the guest may be hostile, not buggy but actively working against you, because a prompt-injected agent is an attacker holding your tools — and then spends more words on what is not defended than on what is. The largest open gap is named as such: Firecracker runs as your user, unconfined, until the jailer lands at P4-1, so a VMM vulnerability gets the attacker your account. The TLS-termination trade-off from decision D6 is spelled out with its three real consequences — the proxy sees that traffic in plaintext, certificate pinning breaks for bound domains, and the ephemeral CA is trusted inside the guest — alongside how tightly it is scoped. Also documented as undefended: side channels, denial of service, the supply chain of what the agent fetches, unencrypted snapshots (a snapshot holds the guest's whole memory), and multi-tenancy, which KelyfOS has no notion of. A trust-boundary table marks two rows not yet. The README now says isolation-first architecture, not hardened, which is the plan's own red callout honoured rather than quietly forgotten at launch time. docs/threat-model.md, 151 lines, linked from README under a Security heading
Sections: what it is for · assets in priority order · what is defended (guest escape, exfiltration, credential theft, record tampering) · what is not (host-side before the jailer, unconfined inside the guest, TLS termination, side channels, DoS, supply chain, host tooling, data at rest, multi-tenancy) · trust-boundary table · an evaluation section saying plainly what is and is not reasonable today
No security claim is made anywhere that P4-1 and P4-2 have not earned

A registry clearance search is a lawyer's job before any commercial use, and that is now noted rather than assumed.

2026-08-22P3-1 Networked snapshot restore, implemented under D22 after the Phase 3 acceptance test found it broken. Acceptance step 1 prepares a sandbox by cloning a repo inside it, which needs a NIC, and step 2 restores that snapshot — a path never exercised, because P3-1 was verified on vsock-only machines. It failed with a raw Firecracker fault after the user had already paid for a 512 MB snapshot that could never load. Restore now re-creates the egress path and re-pairs the interface with network_overrides. Getting the load to succeed was the easy half. Making the restored machine actually reach the network took two more findings, both of which come from the same root cause — the guest's view of its network lives inside the memory image and cannot be reached from the host. First, the guest's HTTPS_PROXY names a specific address and port, so a restore that allocated a fresh /30 produced correct-looking plumbing and a guest dialling where the proxy used to be; the snapshot now records the addressing and the restore reproduces it. Second, even with the right address the guest hung for ~138 s per connection: its ARP entry still mapped that address to the MAC of a TAP that no longer existed, and the replacement came up with a kernel-random one. Host TAP MACs are now derived from the sandbox id and pinned, so the replacement is indistinguishable from the original. fork keeps refusing networked snapshots — P3-2 scopes forks to vsock-only and that reasoning still holds, since N forks need N guest identities baked into one shared memory image — but it now says so instead of printing the same Firecracker fault four times. 
$ cat ~/.cache/kelyfos/snapshots/d22/meta.json
{
  "has_network": true,
  "iface_id": "eth0",
  "allow": [ "github.com" ],
  "host_ip": "169.254.237.113",
  "guest_ip": "169.254.237.114",
  "netmask": "255.255.255.252",
  "host_mac": "02:01:b9:6f:4b:e3",
  "proxy_port": 40983
}

2026-08-22P3-7 Gap against §1 found by reading the definition of done line by line instead of assuming it was covered (the PLAN-FEATURES gate depends on §1 being met, so it had to be checked rather than believed). Bullet 2 names the invocation kelyfos run --image dev --workspace . --allow github.com --secret GITHUB_TOKEN@github.com -- <agent cmd>, and run accepted no positional arguments at all — the only way to use a sandbox was run & then exec. Implemented under D23: the trailing command runs on the host for the sandbox's lifetime with KELYFOS_SANDBOX and the kelyfos binary's directory on its PATH, and kelyfos exits with its status. That bullet is now demonstrable as one command, and it is also the shape a real agent wants — kelyfos run --workspace . --allow github.com -- claude hands an agent a sandbox and takes it away again.

2026-08-23P4 Two Phase 4 tasks marked [BLOCKED], and the reason is worth stating plainly: this plan cannot honestly reach 43/43 today. P4-4 is conditional by its own wording — a browser flavor “if users ask for it” — and the repository went public today, so there is nobody to have asked. P4-5 requires deciding “with evidence” whether there is real pull for a paid EU-compliance pack; with no users there is no evidence, and a decision recorded now would be a guess wearing a decision's clothes. Both could be made to look done — build the flavor, write the decision — but ticking a box whose own condition is unmet is exactly what this plan's rules exist to prevent. They are marked rather than quietly skipped so the next session does not go hunting for a 100% that is not available yet. The remaining five (P4-1 jailer, P4-2 seccomp/Landlock, P4-3 reproducible builds and signing, P4-6 multi-lane watch, P4-7 one-command host integration) are unblocked and doable whenever they are wanted; P4-1 and P4-2 are the two that would let the README stop saying “not hardened yet”.

2026-08-23P3-6 CI stops paying full price for documentation, and starts proving its own caches. Deferred until after the v0.3 launch on purpose — a pipeline whose shape is changing is not one to optimise — and taken now under D24, before feature work resumes. Three parts. A changes job diffs the push or pull request and, when every changed path is docs/, any .md, either plan file or LICENSE, skips build and boot; checks is deliberately left unconditional so tools/check-plan.py still audits every plan edit. BR2_CCACHE is enabled in the shared Buildroot fragment with its cache directory templated from the Makefile, and the CI job persists it keyed on versions.mk with no loose restore key. And ci.yml now carries a Monday cron whose run restores none of the three caches, so a build that has quietly become dependent on a cache fails within the week rather than on a stranger’s first clone. cmake was added to the build dependencies because ccache builds with it and Buildroot otherwise builds a cmake of its own.

2026-08-23P0-6 Dependency refresh, run before feature work resumes rather than after something breaks. Every pin in versions.mk was checked against its upstream and all four are already current — Buildroot on the 2026.02 long-term line, the kernel on the newest 6.18 longterm patch, Firecracker on its newest stable, Go on its newest stable — so nothing was bumped and the two Go tarball digests were verified against go.dev rather than assumed. The Go module graph was a different story: go get -u ./... && go mod tidy moved the one direct dependency that had aged, golang.org/x/sys v0.36.0 → v0.47.0, and eight indirect ones with it. That upgrade retires the single finding govulncheck had against the old graph, GO-2026-5024 in x/sys/windows, which was unreachable here for the plain reason that KelyfOS is a Linux program — but a known-vulnerable module in go.mod is a thing a future reader has to re-reason about, and this one cost nothing to remove. go-runewidth v0.0.28 brought two new transitive modules, clipperhouse/displaywidth v0.11.0 and clipperhouse/uax29/v2 v2.7.0; both are MIT, hence Apache-2.0-compatible, and both are actively maintained. No major versions were taken: Bubble Tea and Lip Gloss both have current v2 lines, and declining them for now is recorded as D25 rather than left as a silence.

2026-08-23P3-6 Follow-up to the row above: cancel-in-progress now applies to branches only, not to main. The defect showed itself twice within an hour and both times the same way. This project's protocol produces closely-spaced pairs of pushes — the work, then STATUS.md — and the second push cancelled the first commit's build. Because the status commit is documentation-only, its own run legitimately skipped build and boot, so the pair ended with a green tick against a commit whose code had never been compiled or booted. That is worse than a red build: it is a commit that looks verified. Superseding an older run is the right behaviour on a branch, where only the tip matters; on main every commit is one someone may bisect to. The cancelled run for the E1-3 commit was re-run to confirm it, and the deps commit before it needed the same treatment. 
before the fix:
$ gh run list
32603407221  in_progress            b2360f78   STATUS.md only
32603388615  completed  cancelled   050022df   E1-3  <- cancelled by the above
32601915193  completed  success     d69064eb   STATUS.md only (build+boot skipped)
32601898225  completed  cancelled   966e0ab4   deps  <- same shape, re-run to green

concurrency:
  group: ci-${{ github.ref }}-${{ github.event_name }}
- cancel-in-progress: true
+ cancel-in-progress: ${{ github.ref != 'refs/heads/main' }}

after the fix, both runs green on their own commits:
32603388615  success  050022df  E1-3            (re-run)
32603455113  success  fec01ab3  concurrency fix (re-run)
2026-08-23P2-6 A flaky test found and fixed — one failure in ten, in the test that proves the secret-injection guarantee. Noticed because go test ./... failed once during E1-4 and passed on the retry, which is the moment a flake either gets chased or gets normalised. Forty runs put it at 2 in 20 and then 0 in 40 after the fix. The race was in the test, not the proxy, and the proxy is right: an egress.attempt carries the connection's byte counts, so it cannot be recorded until the connection is done — which is deliberately after the last byte has reached the client. The test read the response body and immediately inspected the record, racing the proxy's own bookkeeping. It now waits for the matching attempt with a five-second deadline. The sibling test was strengthened at the same time, and strengthening it turned up a second fact worth knowing: it asserted only that no terminated attempt existed, which an empty list satisfies, so it could pass by finding nothing. Making it wait for the tunnelled attempt first failed 40 times out of 40 — because a tunnel's record is written when both copy directions end, and the client-to-upstream direction ends only when the client hangs up, which the test was leaving to cleanup. The helper now hands the caller its raw connection so the test can close it and then wait. Both halves of that are the same lesson: the proxy records a connection when the connection is over, because the record carries its byte counts. 
$ for i in $(seq 1 20); do go test -count=1 ./internal/egress/; done
--- FAIL: TestTerminationInjectsTheCredential
    terminate_test.go:151: no terminated attempt was recorded: []
--- FAIL: TestTerminationInjectsTheCredential
    terminate_test.go:151: no terminated attempt was recorded: []
failures: 2 / 20

$ go test -count=1 -race ./internal/egress/     ok  (the race is a happens-
                                                    before gap, not a data race)
first attempt at the fix, which the sibling test caught:
$ for i in $(seq 1 40); do go test -count=1 ./internal/egress/; done
--- FAIL: TestNoSecretMeansNoTermination (5.00s)
    terminate_test.go:184: no matching attempt was recorded within 5s: []
failures: 40 / 40      <- a tunnel's record waits for the client to hang up

after closing the client connection before waiting:
$ for i in $(seq 1 60); do go test -count=1 ./internal/egress/; done
failures: 0 / 60
2026-08-23P3-6 The concurrency fix did not fix it, and the second attempt is the real one. Turning cancel-in-progress off for main was necessary and insufficient: GitHub cancels a pending run when a newer one queues behind the same in-progress run, so with one shared group per ref a rapid three-push sequence still loses the middle commit. That is precisely the sequence this project's protocol produces — the work, then STATUS.md — and it cost the E1-4 commit its build twice for two different reasons. Every commit on main now gets a concurrency group keyed on its own sha, so main's runs never queue behind each other and none of them can be superseded; branches keep one group per ref and keep cancelling, because there only the tip matters. Recorded rather than quietly amended because the first attempt looked correct, passed its own CI run, and was wrong — the failure mode it leaves behind is a commit that appears verified. 
$ gh run list
32605075831  success    862aa172   STATUS.md only   <- queued, took the slot
32605058898  CANCELLED  0e700232   E1-4             <- cancelled while pending
32605028200  success    c0fd19da   flake fix        <- in progress at the time

  no jobs ran in 32605058898 at all: it was cancelled before it started, which
  is the pending-run rule rather than cancel-in-progress.

- group: ci-${{ github.ref }}-${{ github.event_name }}
+ group: ci-${{ github.event_name }}-${{ github.ref }}-${{ github.ref ==
+          'refs/heads/main' && github.sha || 'tip' }}
  cancel-in-progress: ${{ github.ref != 'refs/heads/main' }}

after the fix, both commits build on their own:
32605058898  success  0e700232  E1-4              (re-run)
32605299543  success  9c197ac1  the fix itself
  — and they ran at the same time rather than one queueing behind the other
2026-08-23P3-7 A real defect in kelyfos run -- <command>, shipped in v0.3 and found while building E1-6: when the command exited non-zero, the sandbox was never torn down and the workspace was never written back. defer os.Exit(code) was registered last and therefore ran first — before the shutdown, the sync-back and the session.end that were registered above it — and os.Exit runs no deferred functions at all. The comment beside it said “deferred teardown still runs”, which is exactly the kind of belief a comment can hold and a program cannot. The consequence is as bad as it sounds: this is the invocation section 1's definition of done names, the one a real agent uses, and an agent that did work and then exited non-zero lost every byte of it — while leaving a microVM running and a run directory behind. Reproduced, fixed by returning the status through the existing exitError that main already understands, and reproduced again to confirm. The E1-4 OOM exit path had inherited the same shape a few hours earlier and is fixed with it. 
before — kelyfos run --workspace ./ws -- sh -c 'agent writes; exit 3'
  exit: 3
  workspace:      seed.txt              <- the agent's file is gone
  run dirs left:  2
  firecracker:    2 processes still alive
  session log:    no session.end at all

after:
  exit: 3
  workspace written back to /tmp/e16c/ws
  workspace:      out.txt seed.txt
  content:        written-by-the-agent
  run dirs left:  0     firecracker alive: 0
  session end     command_exited after 998 ms

- if code != 0 { defer os.Exit(code) }
+ if code != 0 { return &exitError{code} }
2026-08-23P5 Phase 5 opened: hardening, exiting at v0.9. P4-1 (jailer) and P4-2 (seccomp / Landlock) promoted out of the backlog into six tasks with an acceptance test that reads the host’s own /proc rather than believing the CLI — the Firecracker process must not be running as the invoking user, its root must not be /, and a guest command must be refused by the kernel for a path its flavor does not grant while the same command in /work still works. The phase exists to make one sentence in the README unnecessary, and it is not finished until that sentence is replaced by another that is also true. Promotion and its boundaries are D27. PLAN.html §6 Phase 5, D27

Buildroot 2026.02.3 → 2025.02.17 (the LTS line, EOL March 2028, per the project’s own download page); Linux 6.18.46 → 6.12.105, because 2025.02.x supports kernel header series only to 6.12 and kernel.org gives 6.12 and 6.18 the same projected EOL of December 2028.
