Full aarch64 rebuild from a fresh tree, config verified symbol by symbol, then 117 acceptance checks across six suites and fourteen cookbook recipes, all green. versions.mk, D28
2026-08-24P5-0 docs/hardening.md: what a compromised agent can reach, before anything is built to change it. The inventory first, because everything after is measured against it — an agent is root in its own guest and always has been, the read-only root and the tmpfs overlay and the caps are what limit it, and the syscall surface of the guest kernel is not among them. On the host the VM is the boundary and nothing stands behind it: Firecracker runs as the invoking user, in that user’s mount namespace, with that user’s SSH keys and every session record addressable if the boundary ever fails. Three facts were read rather than assumed, and two of them changed the plan. The jailer’s own documentation for the pinned v1.16.1 says the caller must hard-link or copy every resource the VM touches into the jail — kernel, rootfs, workspace, plugins, snapshot state, and the vsock socket every host-side command dials. That sentence is most of P5-1, and it forces the rule that the flight recorder stays outside: a record the jailed process can reach is not a record. Firecracker’s seccomp filters are already on by default, a protection this project has been getting free and never checked, so P5-2 is to prove it from /proc rather than to write a filter — its own docs warn that a custom one can disable the boundary altogether. And the guest kernel has CONFIG_SECCOMP_FILTER=y but CONFIG_SECURITY_LANDLOCK is not set, with landlock absent from CONFIG_LSM: P5-3 needs a kernel config change and a rebuild, and needs both halves, because compiling an LSM in without naming it reads as protection in a config file and is none at runtime. One piece of good news from D28: 6.12.105’s Landlock ABI is 6, the newest there is and the same one x/sys/unix exposes — the kernel this project moved back to costs nothing here. The longest section is §5, what remains reachable afterwards, written now so the sentence that replaces “not hardened yet” can be checked against it rather than composed to sound good. docs/hardening.md
2026-08-24P5-1 Firecracker runs inside the jailer, on every entry point, and the host’s own /proc says so. Fourteen checks in dev/accept-jail.sh, none of which ask the CLI: mountinfo shows the VMM’s root is this sandbox’s chroot, its whole filesystem is the jail’s own contents with no /etc, /home, /usr or /var in it, it is not root, NoNewPrivs is 1, and Seccomp is 2. The design turns on one fact found by running it: the jailer accepts a chroot directory that already exists with files in it. That is what lets the sandbox’s run directory be the jail root, so the host binds its listening sockets and writes the machine’s configuration before the VMM starts and they are inside the jail by construction rather than by copying. The flight recorder stays outside, because a record the jailed process can reach is not a record. Every entry point, structurally. The refusal lives in sandbox.New and sandbox.Restore rather than at six call sites, so run, team up, fork, snapshot restore, serve-mcp and shim cannot each forget it. Without passwordless sudo a run refuses with [jail.no_sudo] whose fix line is the sudoers line, built from this machine’s own user and jailer path so it can be pasted rather than adapted. doctor checks it; the quickstart now installs it. The posture is in the record (the owner’s ruling): session.start carries jailed, true or false, so a transcript can never make an unjailed run look like a jailed one — the same rule that made an egress attempt’s mode say how much the proxy could actually read. --no-jail discloses in the terminal on every run that uses it. Three things broke and were fixed rather than worked around. --new-pid-ns makes the jailer’s parent return, so every run reported the VMM exiting the moment it started; it is dropped, with the reason written where the flag would have gone. A jailed VMM can only create files inside its chroot, so snapshot save asks for in-jail paths and the host moves the state and memory out afterwards. And a snapshot records its drives by the path written in it — so the three-agent recipe, whose cached template predates this change, could not find /home/…/rootfs.ext4; the rootfs is now staged at both names, and all fourteen recipes pass. D29 amends the acceptance rather than quietly passing it. The item asked that the VMM not run as the invoking user; it does, because the alternative on the sudo path is root. It also named /proc/<pid>/root, which returns / for a chrooted process and / for an unjailed one — the same answer for opposite facts. Both corrected, with the residual named in docs/hardening.md §5. Regression: 33/33 on the epic acceptance, 12/12, 13/13, 18/18, 19/19 and 22/22 on the per-feature ones, 14/14 recipes, and the whole Go suite. internal/sandbox/jail.go, dev/accept-jail.sh, D29
2026-08-24P5-2 The host syscall filter, read back out of the kernel rather than inferred from a flag nobody passed. Which filter is settled structurally: the jailer forwards everything after -- to Firecracker verbatim, so internal/sandbox/jail_test.go asserts that tail carries neither --no-seccomp nor --seccomp-filter, which makes the filter’s identity the pinned binary’s identity. That it is on is read from /proc on every thread of the VMM once the guest answers — per thread because Firecracker installs without TSYNC and one unfiltered thread is a hole a process-level read cannot see — and a VMM without one is refused with [seccomp.not_in_force], proved by causing it rather than by asserting it. What it permits was pulled out of the running kernel with PTRACE_SECCOMP_GET_FILTER and interpreted by an abstract interpreter over the real instructions, and agrees syscall for syscall with the JSON Firecracker publishes for v1.16.1 on both architectures. bash dev/accept-seccomp.sh → 15 passed, 0 failed (aarch64, dev machine)
CI 32704409463, boot job on the bare-KVM reference → 14 passed, 0 failed, 1 skipped (x86_64; the skip is the record it was there to produce)
vmm 179 insns sha256 e2b10f5c… · api 103 830d937c… · vcpu 103 477f0d1c… (aarch64)
vmm 182 insns sha256 5fccc87e… · api 105 6c84e690… · vcpu 106 2ae943f4… (x86_64)
distinct syscalls vmm/api/vcpu = 50 / 31 / 24 on both, against 50 / 31 / 24 in resources/seccomp/<triple>.json at v1.16.1
unlisted syscall → TRAP(0) · foreign arch → KILL_THREAD · the two arch lists differ in exactly two names: open/stat vs openat/newfstatat
negative control: a PATH wrapper appending --no-seccomp → refused, all threads named, 0 firecracker processes left
record: dev/expect/host-seccomp-{aarch64,x86_64}.txt · page: docs/host-seccomp.md
regression: dev/accept-jail.sh 14/14, go vet + go test ./... clean, make docs clean

The smoke test asserted that ~/.cache/kelyfos/run was empty after teardown, which stopped being the right question when the jailer’s path scheme <base>/<exec-file>/<id>/root put a firecracker/ level underneath it. Nothing was actually left behind — the whole of what survives is one empty directory — so the check now tests the claim it meant: no sandbox state and no sockets anywhere under run/, at any depth, printing what it found when it fails. 2026-08-24P5-6 A P5-1 regression found while reading the same /proc, turned into a task rather than a footnote (D30). kelyfos run --cpu-quota is refused on every jailed run on a host whose cgroup path resolves to the systemd user manager, which is the dev machine the quickstart describes: the jailed path never calls WrapArgv so no scope is created, and --parent-cgroup is passed only in direct mode so nothing places the VMM either. It is fail-closed, not silent, which is why it went unnoticed rather than unreported. Not fixed here, because it is a design question about how the jailer’s cgroup handling composes with E1’s two modes and not a line — P5-6, before P5-4. kelyfos run --cpu-quota 150% (jailed, default) → refused: "cpu quota not applied: …/session-4.scope/cpu.max is ‘max 100000’, expected ‘150000 100000’"
kelyfos run --no-jail --cpu-quota 150% → ready, cgroup …/user@501.service/app.slice/kelyfos-64501949.scope — the same command works unjailed
same refusal under sudo -n -E, so it is not a privilege problem
internal/sandbox/sandbox.go Start: WrapArgv is on the else branch; jail.go jailArgv: --parent-cgroup only when slice.Direct()
2026-08-24P5-6 The cgroup and the jail compose, and it was not the one-line fix D30 guessed at. Two independent defects, not one: Start and Restore replaced the command line with the jailer’s instead of also wrapping it, so on the systemd path no scope was ever created — and on the direct path --parent-cgroup was passed without --cgroup-version 2, which the jailer’s own default of version 1 turns into a silent no-op on a v2 host. Fixing only the first would have left CI, which runs as root and therefore always takes the direct path, testing a broken mechanism and reporting green. Three acceptance scripts were also still reading the pre-jail run-directory layout, so E1’s own acceptance had been measuring nothing rather than failing. D30 asked to be answered plainly if the fix turned out to be small; it did not, and the record says so here rather than in a decision that could be read as retrospective justification. before: --cpu-quota 150% jailed → refused, VMM in session-4.scope
after (systemd path, dev machine): cgroup …/app.slice/kelyfos-3ca95748.scope · /proc/<pid>/cgroup agrees · cpu.max 150000 100000 · still chrooted, uid 501, seccomp filter on all 4 threads
after (direct path, KELYFOS_CGROUP_ROOT): VMM in 0::/kelyfosprobe/kelyfos-88c38461, the slice KelyfOS made — the jailer moves into the named parent itself rather than a child, and teardown removed it
dev/accept-jail.sh 17/17 (was 14/14; three new checks close this phase’s acceptance item 2)
dev/accept-e1.sh 13/13, 0 skipped — and now measuring: “host cgroup accounting shows 0.49 core(s) consumed against a 0.5 ceiling”, throughput 10.02 Mbps against a 10 Mbps cap. Before this task the same suite reported 10 passed / 2 failed with both figures empty
go vet + go test ./... clean; new unit test keeps --parent-cgroup and --cgroup-version together
primary sources: jailer.md and src/jailer/src/{env,cgroup}.rs at v1.16.1 — “If no --cgroup parameters are specified and --cgroup-version=2 is passed, the jailer … will move the process to the specified cgroup”, and a parent that does not exist is a silent no-op
2026-08-24P5-3 Per-flavor confinement on everything the supervisor spawns, and the toolbox still works. The guest kernel gained Landlock — both halves, compiled in and named in CONFIG_LSM, because an LSM that is built and not named answers ENOSYS exactly like one that was never built. Landlock takes the filesystem: read and execute the whole image, write only /work, /tmp, /run, $HOME and seven named device nodes; seccomp refuses twenty-eight syscalls with EPERM, a refusal list rather than an allowlist for the same reason P5-2 refused to write one for the VMM. Both are applied by a re-exec of the supervisor that restricts itself and then execves the real program, in reaper.startAndRegister — the one place every child is started, so exec, the plugins and the shell get it without any of the three remembering to ask. /sys/kernel/security/lsm → capability,landlock · guest reports landlock abi 6
dev/accept-profile.sh 23/23: a write to /etc, /usr and /lib refused by the kernel, the same write in /work fine; mount and reboot EPERM; a spawned process's own /proc/self/status reports Seccomp: 2, NoNewPrivs: 1, and so does a shell on the pty channel
toolbox intact: git commits in /work, python3 runs, mv crosses /tmp→/work (REFER granted on purpose), busybox still dispatches on argv[0], a missing command is still 127 and not a confinement failure
two profile bugs found by running it: git opens /dev/null read-write, which the first profile refused — fixed by naming seven device nodes rather than granting /dev, so /dev/vda and /dev/vdb stay unwritable; and Landlock refuses ptrace between sibling domains, which broke an E4 check that read another process's /proc/<pid>/exe
regression, all green: jail 17/17, seccomp 15/15, profile 23/23, denials 12/12, shell 13/13, runs 18/18, notify 22/22, forward 19/19, e4 22/22, e5 33/33, e1 13/13, the Go suite, and the cookbook
two pre-existing failures found by the sweep and fixed: accept-e4 grepped a refusal wording that E5-4 changed and nobody re-ran; accept-runs counted a session directory left root-owned by a sudo diagnostic

2026-08-24P5-7 A restored machine says what it brought back, and the chain keeps it. A restore gets no ready frame — the machine was already running when its memory was written to disk — so the host asks it over the control channel, on the resync round trip the restore already makes and which is already the proof the machine is answering. The posture then rides session.ready rather than session.start, which is both more honest and wider than the ruling asked: a wall that was chosen belongs where the chain opens, a wall that was observed belongs where there was something to observe — and session.ready is emitted once per machine on all eight paths, including team members and the shim, which session.start never covered. Restoring a pre-confinement snapshot warns in the terminal with the fix that fixes it, and does not refuse (D32). dev/accept-profile.sh 29/29 (was 23/23; six new)
restore prints profile landlock abi 6 · dev · … and a command inside the restored machine reports Seccomp: 2
kelyfos log --session <restored> --json → "profile":"landlock abi 6 …" and "jailed":true on session.ready
the warning is a pure function with its own test, because staging its main case needs an image built before the feature existed: three states, three messages, and a test that the two unconfined ones never collapse into one — one says commands are refused, the other says they are not
P5-1’s jailed gap is closed by the same change: it was on one of eight chain-opening sites and is now on all of them
found on the way, not fixed here: snapshot restore fails for any machine that had a workspace. Reproduced standalone. It is P5-9
2026-08-24P5-9 A snapshot of a machine with a workspace could not be restored at all, and the fix is an ordering. Firecracker will not load a snapshot until every block device’s backing file exists at the path written in it, and since P5-1 that path is the chroot-relative /workspace.ext4 — while Restore staged the captured workspace only after the load, which was enough when the recorded path was an absolute host one that still existed. The copy now goes into the jail before the load, by copy rather than by hard link, because two forks of one snapshot that shared those blocks would make the independent-fork claim false in the most damaging possible way. Afterwards the jailed path has nothing left to repoint: the file is already this machine’s own at the name the snapshot records, and repointing it at a host path would name a file the jailed VMM cannot open. before: snapshot restore → firecracker 400 “Error manipulating the backing file: No such file or directory (os error 2) /workspace.ext4”, reproduced standalone
after: sandbox 2233f65d restored from "wstest" in 179 ms and cat /work/marker → the content written before the snapshot
dev/accept-jail.sh 18/18 — its snapshot section now attaches a workspace, which is the reason this was invisible: no suite snapshotted a machine with one
regression: accept-e2, accept-e5, accept-profile and all fourteen cookbook recipes
2026-08-24P5-8 The sibling-ptrace refusal named where a person will look, and proved rather than asserted. It is in docs/denials.md’s own section for what is deliberately not in the catalog — a refusal the guest kernel makes to a syscall KelyfOS never sees has no raise site, and the catalog fails the build for an entry nothing raises (D33) — and in the generated profiles page, which is where a reader of dev’s ptrace line lands. Both halves are checked with nothing but a shell: reading another process’s /proc/<pid>/exe goes through the same ptrace_may_access the attach does, so a command can introspect a child it started and cannot introspect a sibling. One claim corrected while writing it: the profile’s own comment said dev ships gdb-adjacent tooling. It ships none, and the sentence now says what is true — this is about what somebody installs into a dev sandbox. dev/accept-profile.sh 31/31 (was 29/29)
a command reading its own child → /bin/busybox; the same command reading a sibling → pid=145 exe=[]
the first version of the sibling check passed for the wrong reason — it scanned /proc for a command line that was in its own arguments, so it found itself. It reads a pid the sibling wrote down instead
docs/denials.md §“What is not in the catalog”, docs/reference/profiles.md §“Attaching a debugger” (generated)
2026-08-24— Both P5 deviations endorsed as improvements, and one of them promoted to doctrine. The posture rides session.ready rather than session.start, and D32’s event choice is superseded rather than tolerated: an opening event is per-command and cannot carry five machines’ postures. The rule that came with it now governs every record field added from here — a choice may ride the opening, an observation rides ready — and docs/events.md states it as a rule rather than as a note about two fields. The absent catalog entry for the sibling-ptrace refusal stands, with the overrule declined: an entry nothing raises is the forged promise F-D4’s gate exists to catch, and no guest-side refusal mechanism is to be built to create one. the product owner’s rulings of 2026-08-24, logged in D32 and D33 beside the deviations they answer

2026-08-24P5-4 Correction to the team figures P5-4 published, caught while refreshing the launch post. The row above quotes 737 ms cold / 149 ms forked. Those are real and they are the wrong pair to headline: they come from prove-team.sh, whose team runs under a deliberate 200% CPU cap — five agents sharing two cores, which is why its cold number is slow and its forked number is fast. The figure the README replaced was measured on demo-team.sh’s graph, and the comparable pair from the same run is 412 ms with all five cold and 286 ms with four forked from a cached template. The README now carries those, with the condition named, and says the master boots cold either way because a fork cannot carry a network identity. Two numbers from one run are not interchangeable just because both are true. caps run 32715397107 — team.log (prove-team, cpu.max 200000 100000): 737 ms / 149 ms · demo.log (demo-team): 412 ms (5 cold) / 286 ms (4 forked, 1 cold)
the pre-D28 pair this replaces was 366 ms / 215 ms, measured on demo-team’s graph
2026-08-24P5-5 The launch assets, committed and posted nowhere. 2026-08-24P6-0 Phase 6 drafted spec-first, against the code rather than against the backlog’s wording. Eleven parallel audits — one per pillar, each required to cite file:line and to put what it could not verify in an open-questions list rather than in a sentence — plus two adversarial critics reading the combined findings for gaps and for over-commitment. Their verdict is in D34 because acting on it is the decision: as briefed this was three to four phases wearing one phase’s name, and two pillars would have produced claims this project cannot honestly make. Every pillar is in. Three are scoped to their honest version with the case in its own row — D36 (the credential handle is three quarters buildable and the fourth quarter needs a guest→host channel that does not exist), D37 (scrubbing ships as echo suppression; the general recogniser is declined rather than deferred, because a false positive inside a tarball is undiagnosable from inside the guest), D38 (reproducibility is measured and the measurement is the claim). D35 dispositions all seven P4 rows. D40 gives §8 a ninth rule, which is F-D9 restated here after it evaporated with the file that held it — the reason Phase 5 shipped v0.9 with no token re-measurement and no docs-only exam. Three findings that changed the draft rather than decorating it. (1) Two of the four named fuzz targets are not where the brief thinks: internal/vsock parses no bytes at all, and the MCP stdio protocol is internal/proto plus encoding/json — so P6-3 names the trust boundary instead of a list, since “every untrusted-input parser” has no completion criterion without one. (2) §1 has required a verifiable session report since this plan was written and the export is not one — it embeds no events and renders the exporter’s own verdict as a green badge. That is an unmet definition-of-done criterion needing no cryptography, so it became P6-6 and it lands before signing. (3) The MCP specification moved: the current revision removes the initialize handshake entirely, so P6-13’s check is written dual-era from the first line and P6-14 has to state a policy rather than a version. The client landscape was surveyed live for P6-13 — two independent surveys reconciled by a third pass that re-fetched every disputed claim — and the answer is D41: six supported, the rest generic, four rejected on evidence (two archived, one retired as a brand, one with 48k stars and no MCP implementation at all). Its finding is not the list but the shape: one template with a swapped key is impossible, because Claude Code has no working-directory field at all while others do and expand variables differently — so half of them would silently attach the wrong policy under a shared snippet, which is F-D44’s failure once per client. And one correction made to an audit before it could decide anything: a reader argued P4-6’s residual is forbidden by D7’s “no fleet view”. D7 names P4-6 by id in the sanctioned half of that sentence and attaches the prohibition to a server with auth and persistence. P4-6 is parked on cost and value, not on principle. 2026-08-24P6-1 The honesty sweep, and it was not the list P6-0 wrote. Six parallel readers, one per class of claim, each required to name the file:line of the claim and of the code that refutes it — a finding without both halves was not a finding — then an adversarial reconciler that re-checked every one and dropped eight, two of them as unverified rather than asserted. What survived was 59 verified claims against the twelve P6-0 knew about. The three that matter most were all understatements, which is the direction nobody audits for. docs/threat-model.md’s summary table — the table a reader consults to decide whether to trust this — still said the two headline v0.9 layers were “not yet (P4-1)” and “not yet (P4-2)”, four releases after they shipped; it also said the audit record was absent under the shim, which F-D33 fixed. And CONTRIBUTING.md told a security reporter that KelyfOS is “pre-v0.1 and makes no hardened-security claims yet”. Three were overstatements of the kind this phase exists to catch: the generated profiles page said “nothing else is writable” while /dev/pts and /dev/shm are granted the same write rights as /work — and /dev/shm is a tmpfs the kernel sizes at half the machine’s RAM, so a general-purpose writable area sat outside a list a reader would take as closed; the README said every [resources] cap is enforced on the host when scratch is applied by the guest’s own kernel; and it said every session ends with a resource.summary when only run and team members emit one. Fixed in the source rather than in the page wherever the page is generated, because a hand edit there is reverted by the next build: the supervisor’s profile dump now emits its device grants, the recorder schema moves jailed and profile to session.ready where D32 put them and all eight writers actually set them, and the flag extractor learned the one shape flag.PrintDefaults formats differently — a one-letter boolean, whose usage goes on the same line after a tab. That is why kelyfos log -f was defined, named in the usage prose, and absent from the reference with CI green throughout: the generator and the committed file agreed, and both were wrong. Four code fixes came out of doc claims rather than the reverse: the host-side argument summariser was missing data, so base64 file contents could reach the record on a door its own comment promised covered “a tool that does not exist yet”; serve-mcp’s OOM event carried the resident set without the cap it was measured against; install-firecracker.sh carried a fallback version that made it a second place naming a Firecracker, which its own header denied; and the supervisor’s version was a constant reading 0.2.0-p2, printed on every boot and written into every chain for seven releases — now stamped from git the way the CLI’s always was. Deliberately not done here: the guest os-release is still 0.1.0-dev, which needs the overlay templated and an image build to verify; it goes to P6-9, where that build is already open. Fixing a build I cannot run would be a claim of a different kind. 59 verified, 8 dropped, 11 routed to tasks · 24 files · docs regenerated and proved idempotent on a second run

2026-08-24P6-2 A security contact that exists, and a scanner that runs when nothing has changed. SECURITY.md is new and its short half is the reporting instructions; its long half is what is and is not a vulnerability here, which this project can state with unusual precision because it already publishes what it does not defend. Nine documented design decisions are listed as not findings — root inside your own guest, the chroot not being the boundary, the shared uid, --no-jail, side channels, the unsigned artifacts — against seven that are, of which the sharpest is a record that overstates the walls that were around a run. Three documents that dead-ended at “raise concerns privately with the maintainer” now point at it. The channel is GitHub’s private vulnerability reporting rather than a published address (D42) — and it is [BLOCKED — needs John], because it reads enabled:false and turning it on is an owner action. So the document is written to be true either way: it names the button, and says what to do when the button is not there, which is a public issue containing only a request for a private channel and no details. A SECURITY.md that would be wrong on the day it landed is precisely what P6-1 spent a commit undoing. No response time is promised, deliberately: a number a solo maintainer cannot keep is worse than no number, and what is promised instead is a plain answer including a plain disagreement. govulncheck is promoted from a habit into CI — it has caught a real advisory here before, so this is a discipline being written down rather than new tooling. Its own workflow, its own day, and a finding fails the run rather than opening an issue. It deliberately does not run on every push: a scan of an unchanged dependency set against an unchanged database repeats the last answer. And §8 rule 8 is amended in the same commit to say that “the latest CI run” means every workflow that gates main — a rule that read only the build would never see the one workflow built to go red when nothing changed. new: SECURITY.md, .github/workflows/security.yml, make vuln, GOVULNCHECK_VERSION in versions.mk

2026-08-24P6-3 Sixteen fuzz targets, and seven real defects out of writing them. The boundary is declared first (D43) because “every untrusted-input parser” has no completion criterion without one, and the targets follow from the declaration rather than from a list somebody kept. The findings are the point, and four of the seven came from asserting a property rather than waiting for a panic. Two were silent failures, which is the worse kind: a credential bound to github.com. — the fully-qualified spelling — parsed cleanly and then matched nothing, so the request went out unauthenticated and the only symptom was a 401 from somewhere else; and mem large enough to overflow int64 became a negative ceiling that every flag was afterwards compared against. The credential bug had a cause worth naming: one normalisation expression existed in four copies with three different behaviours, and the copy in containsDomain carried a comment promising it matched the proxy. There is one now. Three were the same class arriving three times — agent-chosen text reaching a rendered line of transcript: a tool-call argument key containing a newline, a short string value, and an OOM victim’s process name, which is whatever the agent called its binary. The chain was never at risk (marshalling escapes it); what was at risk is what a person reads. And two were the proxy accepting things that are not what they claim to be: a port of 99999 and a host of /, both refused downstream and both reaching the record first as a destination that is not one. The runner discovers its targets instead of listing them, so the drift a guard test would have detected cannot happen — and the one failure a discovering runner still has, finding nothing and exiting cleanly, is checked. The coverage claim is scoped rather than total, and names what is left out with the reason; sixteen targets is not “every parser”, and this phase opened by deleting claims of that shape. new: 6 fuzz_test.go files, dev/fuzz.sh, make fuzz, a fuzz job in security.yml, a 10 s pass in ci.yml’s checks

2026-08-24P6-3 Two more, from the enumeration that finished after the commit — and the first is the sharpest thing this task found. The parser inventory P6-3 dispatched returned 54 candidates against the 16 that were built, and reading it rather than filing it turned up two the harnesses had not reached. A guest chooses the string on the boot line that says how it is confined. run prints profile <value> straight from the ready frame, and that value is whatever the guest sent. It is the same class as the three control-character findings above, arriving a fourth time — but at the worst possible address: an escape sequence in it can rewrite a line that has already been printed, and the line it can rewrite is the one a person reads to learn which walls are around their sandbox. This product's central claim is that a record can never make an unconfined run look confined; a terminal is not the record, but it is where the claim is first read. Sanitised where the value is stored rather than where it is printed, so the record, the warning and the terminal are all covered by one change. And the buffering Exec had no ceiling. It is the form serve-mcp and the shim use — the two long-lived doors serving other people — and it appended every output chunk a guest sent, for as long as the guest kept sending. kelyfos exec is unaffected because it streams to a terminal. Bounded at 16 MiB, which is the ceiling an MCP frame already imposes downstream, so the limit is now stated with a fix line instead of arriving as a frame failure further along. The duplication recorded as a debt an hour earlier is discharged in the same commit, because it turned out to be the cause: three copies of the same six-line helper existed by the end of P6-3, which is precisely why the same bug was found three separate times. There is one, proto.SafeText, and the guest-message package is where it belongs. enumeration: 54 candidate parsers across four lanes, read rather than filed — the two it caught are the two no harness covered
host/run.go:723 printed sb.State.Profile raw; now sanitised at internal/sandbox/sandbox.go:562 and :872, plus the [profile.not_enforced] reason
internal/sandbox/exec.go: MaxExecOutput = 16 << 20, checked before the append rather than after
three copies of the helper collapsed into proto.SafeText; gofmt, vet, 14 packages and all 16 fuzz targets clean afterwards

2026-08-24P6-4 The credential window narrowed — and two live defects closed on the way there, both found by looking before building. Four commits, because the enumeration this task opened with kept returning things that had to be fixed before the feature made sense. A restored machine recorded nothing about egress. Five proxies exist; four wired their audit hooks by hand and the fifth, kelyfos snapshot restore, never grew them — so a restore wrote a chain with a start, a ready and an end and nothing in between. Not a broken-looking chain: a finished-looking one. The four copies are one function now, and an AST test fails any file that builds a proxy without calling it. The credential went to whatever host the guest named. Go prefers a request's own Host header over the URL's when it writes upstream, so a guest could open a tunnel to a bound domain, take that domain's certificate, and have the credentialed request processed as a different virtual host on the same edge — while the record named the tunnel's target. That is a defeat of the only thing binding a credential to a domain has ever meant, and it predates this task entirely. And main went red, which is how the scheduled fuzz sweep proved its worth on its first outing: three minutes a target found a parser bug forty-five seconds had not. Its root cause was one layer under P6-3's: the normaliser trimmed one trailing dot where the host side trims all of them, so .. bound "." and 0.. bound "0.". Patching shapes did not converge, so a domain must now have the shape of a name. The feature itself is smaller than D36 promised and the difference is D44, written before the code on an adversarial review of the design — commissioned because P6-14 freezes this grammar, and every fatal finding re-verified here before it was accepted. Method scoping is cut: the policy file cannot carry a comma and + is an ordinary path character. Call-binding is cut: it needs a structured home the string grammar cannot give it. The guest's request path is never recorded in any form, because a path is the credential on several real APIs and this record is meant to be forwardable. What shipped: NAME@host[:scheme][/path], binding a credential to one endpoint on one exact host; a matcher that refuses to compare a path a server could re-segment; per-request selection, so two credentials on one host are both reachable; and secret.withheld — which secret, which domain, why not — because a credential that silently fails to attach is the failure this codebase has now found five times. commits 2052f13, 4ba3dca, 7589719 and this one
the path rule was measured before it was written, against Go's own parser — /repos/../admin, /repos/%2e%2e/admin and /repos%2f..%2fadmin all have a URL.Path under /repos/ and all three reach /admin; the rule refuses all of them and still allows /repos/my%20repo/x, which is an ordinary request
the host-misdirection test drives a real terminated session: with the check disabled the upstream reports Authorization: Bearer ghp_… on a request addressed elsewhere
a new event type rather than new event fields, and the reason is measured: a chain written by a build with one more Event field makes an older build report event N has been modified — so docs/events.md §3's “adding a field is not breaking” is false, and the fix is now P6-6's, before the v1.0 freeze
the six secret-parsing sites had five behaviours and now have one grammar: host/teamplan.go was a fourth hand-rolled parser that took everything after the @ as the domain, shim and snapshot restore raised uncatalogued errors, and two serve-mcp paths dropped a project credential in silence — that drop is right and is now recorded rather than silent
gofmt, vet, 14 packages, all 16 fuzz targets and make vuln clean; the compatibility pin holds, with the three deliberate breaks listed in it
2026-08-24P6-5 Echo suppression, named for what it is. KelyfOS keeps a credential out of the guest by construction, so there has never been anything to redact; the case construction cannot reach is the value travelling in a direction this product did not send it — a legitimate, allowlisted, secret-bound call whose response carries the credential back. An API reflecting the header it rejected, an error page quoting the token, a redirect carrying basic credentials. It lands in the guest, in the agent’s context, and in everything the agent writes afterwards. What shipped is exact matching on the bound values and nothing else, which is D37’s ruling and the reason the feature has this name. General credential detection was declined outright rather than deferred: pattern-matching a byte stream the agent is about to parse means a false positive silently corrupting a tarball or a JSON document, with no symptom pointing back at the proxy. The three limits are documented before the capability, because a reader who misses them assumes more than this does: the tunnelled majority is ciphertext and can never be covered; a compressed body is not covered, because the terminated transport deliberately does not decompress and gzip of a credential does not contain the credential; and a value under eight bytes is not scrubbed, because replacing a short string everywhere would corrupt far more than it protects. The replacement keeps the byte length exactly — not cosmetic, since a terminated connection carries many requests and a body whose written length disagreed with its Content-Length would poison every exchange after it. And the record says the proxy altered bytes: secret.scrubbed, once per credential per connection, on the same reasoning that made mode say how much of a connection the proxy could read. mode answers what it could see; this answers whether it changed anything. Two things the work itself turned up. The fuzzer refuted the first invariant rather than the code: a value made largely of the filler byte can be re-created by the act of replacing it, so the property is now stated against the input’s positions rather than as “the value does not appear in the output” — the imprecise statement was hiding the precise one. And the first thing echo suppression caught was this project’s own test, whose upstream reflected the credential back as the response body; it observes the header server-side now, which is what it should always have done. new: internal/egress/scrub.go, secret.scrubbed, wired at every door that builds a proxy
the hard case is a value split across reads, and it is tested and fuzzed: a reader handing over three bytes at a time cannot smuggle a credential past the carry-over
FuzzScrubPreservesEverythingButTheSecret checks what a byte-stream rewriter has to promise — the length never changes, every byte is the original or the filler, and every occurrence present in the input is gone from where it was — clean over 2 minutes after the invariant was corrected
end to end: a server echoing the credential in both a header and the body, and the guest receiving neither, with the body length unchanged
17 fuzz targets now; gofmt, vet, 14 packages and make vuln clean
2026-08-24P6-6 The export carries its own record, and the badge it wrote about itself is gone. §1 has asked for a verifiable HTML report since the day this plan was written, and what existed was a page that rendered the exporter’s own conclusion as a green tick. A reader was trusting a sentence the file wrote about itself. The record it was rendered from now travels inside it — base64 of events.jsonl, exactly as the host wrote it, in a <pre id="kelyfos-chain"> at the foot of the page — the chain head is printed as text, and kelyfos verify <report.html> re-runs the same walk the recorder already implements: offline, no key, no network, no trust root of ours. It takes a raw events.jsonl too, so the sender and the receiver check the same thing with the same command. The page and the record cannot disagree by construction: Render takes the record’s bytes and reads its events from them, so there is no second source to drift from. What replaced the badge is a statement, not a better verdict — what the file carries, and the command somebody else runs. The failure case is kept, because the asymmetry is the point: a file certifying itself is worth nothing, and a file reporting a problem with itself is worth reading. The limit is stated before the capability, on the page, in the command’s own output and in the threat model: verification covers the record, not the page’s rendering of it, and --replay prints the record’s own account so the two can be compared. Two live defects closed on the way, both found by an adversarial review of the design commissioned before the code. A chain with no digests at all verified — "hash":"" on every line, the cheapest forgery there is, because the digest of a line with an empty hash was defined as empty and matched it. It predates this task and was harmless while the file being checked was one this machine wrote; kelyfos verify is what made a stranger’s file the input. And html/template escapes + in element text, which is an ordinary base64 character: the island was already shipping corrupted for any record whose encoding contained one, and the reader would have been told an untouched record had been modified. That is the exact false alarm this task’s first half removed, and it had been reintroduced at the export. Declined, with the reason: having the verifier cross-check the numbers the page prints against the record. It couples the verifier to the template, it catches only an edit nobody would bother to make, and a page that is partly checked invites a reader to treat the rest as checked — which is the badge’s failure wearing a hex string. the hash preimage half of this task landed earlier in commit 95e139e
end to end on a real microVM, as cookbook recipe 6 now runs it: export, verify, extract with sed | base64 -d and cmp against the flight recorder — byte-identical; edit the page and the record still verifies and still disagrees; edit the record inside the page and it is refused
TestTheIslandSurvivesTheTemplate was written after the escaping defect was measured and fails without the fix: the template escaped the island’s + characters, then illegal base64 data at input byte 267
TestAChainWithNoDigestsIsRefused covers both shapes — every digest blank, and no hash key at all — plus a real chain with one digest removed
two new fuzz targets on the extractor, which is the first thing that touches a file a stranger sent: 19 targets now, all clean; the forged-chain shape is a seed in the recorder’s corpus

2026-08-24P6-21 M-3: the review window stops destroying the edit somebody made while they were reading it. First of the audit work on the owner’s ruling, and the arithmetic agrees: the change is small and the failure is silent data loss in the default --review flow. Stage fingerprinted the host directory, the summary printed, a person read it for as long as they took, and Commit renamed that directory away without looking again. Every edit made in that window was gone, and nothing said so — the run reported a successful write-back. Commit looks again, immediately before the swap, and diverts to .kelyfos-out exactly as Stage would have done had it been asked at the right moment. A fingerprint that cannot be taken is not treated as a change: refusing to write back a whole session’s work over a failed stat is the worse mistake, and Stage has already had the fatal version of that question. An explicit diversion — a declined review — is not second-guessed by the new check. And .kelyfos-previous is kept. 2026-08-24P6-6 (cont.) Two defects in what P6-6 shipped, one of them a claim it had just finished insisting on. The adversarial review commissioned for P6-6’s design finished after the commit landed and found both. They are corrected here rather than folded backwards, because the row above is the record of what was believed at the time and this is the record of what was wrong with it. The false claim. The new footer said verification checks that “no line was altered or removed after it was written”. Measured: a 72-event chain cut to 40 verifies clean and reports a different head. Removal from the beginning or the middle is caught — seq stops matching its line number, prev stops matching — but truncation at the end breaks nothing, because nothing after the cut exists to break. The result is byte-for-byte what a shorter session would have written, and indistinguishable from a session still running. An export is produced by the party being audited and head -n -20 is the cheapest edit available to them. The sentence is withdrawn from the page, from docs/events.md §5 and from the cookbook, and replaced with what the code earns. kelyfos verify now also observes whether a record ends with session.end — an observation and never a verdict, because “no end event” is an open session as often as a truncated one, and a policy that called it forgery would accuse every report an older build exported. The decline, reversed on the part that mattered. The row above declined cross-checking the numbers the page prints, on the argument that a partly-checked page invites a reader to treat the rest as checked. That argument survives for the timeline and fails for the head. Measured: replace the displayed head with sixty-four nines, leave the island untouched, and kelyfos verify printed chain intact, the true head, and exit 0. The head is the one number this product tells a reader to write down and compare against a head they were given separately — a file able to change it quietly turns that instruction into a trap, and it is the one edit with a payoff. The head, the event count and the session id are marked in the page, read back, and compared; a mismatch is a failed check with both values printed and exit 1. A missing marker fails too, because every export writes all three and deleting an id is the neatest way to switch a check off. The timeline stays unchecked and the page now says so in those words. both reproduced here before either was accepted: head -n 40 on a real 72-event record → chain intact, 40 events verified, exit 0, a different head; and the all-nines page → chain intact with the true head and exit 0
TestARecordCutShortAtItsEndStillVerifies pins the limit the documents now state, rather than pretending it away
TestAPageThatLiesAboutItsRecordIsCaught covers an edited head, an edited count, an edited session and a deleted marker
the cookbook recipe now makes the lying page and watches it refused, on a real microVM: MISMATCH: the page says chain head 9999…; the record says 303c6d1d…
a raw events.jsonl is not asked what it claims about itself — it is the record, it makes no claims, and checking it against three it never made reported three mismatches for a file that was simply what it is. Caught by running it

2026-08-24P6-6 (cont. 2) Five more defects in P6-6, four of them regressions it introduced, found by an adversarial review of the diff itself. Fourteen findings, deduplicated to five, every one reproduced here before it was touched. Two review passes on one task is not thoroughness for its own sake: the first reviewed the design and the second reviewed the diff, and they found different things. A failed export destroyed the report already at that path. Moving the parse inside Render put os.Create ahead of it, so a record with one unparseable line — a host killed mid-write is enough — emptied last week’s report to zero bytes and then wrote nothing. It now renders beside the destination and renames into place, so a failure leaves what was there alone and leaves nothing behind either. The command’s event count was the verified prefix. Both halves were wrong and the second was the one this task exists to prevent: a command pointing a reader at a check that fails. log --verify called an empty chain intact while the exit-code table P6-6 edited said otherwise and verify implemented it. One rule now, verifiedChain, behind both entry points — the same file must not get two answers depending on which command somebody reached for. Both print the chain head now, for the same reason. And a 0-byte flight recorder was refused as “not a flight recorder”, which is what a process that died before its first append leaves behind. 2026-08-25P6-22 The hostile corpus, complete: nine fixture groups, twenty-one recorded breaks, and a ledger that can only shrink. Every case drives the real code with no VM — a crafted ext4 image, a Unix socket speaking the vsock handshake, an httptest handler, a broker frame decoded by the product’s own reader. The corpus runs on every push under a job named for the question it asks. The ledger is what lets the corpus fail first while main stays green. A red branch until P6-24 would make §8 rule 8 stop meaning anything — every session would open by reading a red build and carrying it. So the failures are written down and the check is symmetric: an unlisted failure is a new break on the commit that caused it, and a listed case that starts holding must take its own line off in the commit that fixed it. It earned itself four times. It refused an over-listing (dirent/.. was recorded broken and holds); it caught a case keyed by a name containing a NUL, which a text ledger can never match; it panicked when a fixture that must chdir moved the ledger out from under itself — a ledger that failed to load quietly would read as a ledger with nothing on it; and it removed exec/flood-with-bytes, which holds, because ac8968f bought exactly that. Two fixtures do not test the finding that prompted them, and say so in their own files. M-9 as worded was fixed before the audit was read; what is live is that the ceiling counts bytes, so frames carrying none — or a stream name no case matches, or blank lines inside a single Read — make Exec never return, and the timeout in its signature is a number mailed to the untrusted party rather than a deadline on the socket. And the OpTrust stub found something while it was being built: a guest’s refusal reaches the operator’s terminal with its control bytes intact, so a guest answering \x1b[1A\x1b[2K\r erases the line the host printed about it. proto.SafeText exists for this and is not applied here. What the corpus does not assert is that the shim should authenticate. That it does not is documented and deliberate; the ledger records the consequence, so the decision stays visible to somebody who is not reading the source. 9 fixture groups across 5 packages, 21 cases recorded broken, all green under the ledger
internal/hostile holds the rule once: the corpus spans three packages now, and a helper copied into each is how one rule becomes three — the duplication this project already found in the MCP argument summarisers
the exec cases separate in under a tenth of a second or never, so a 3-second budget is a twenty-fold margin; the whole corpus adds about 16 s to a push
every case verified to fail for the reason claimed, not merely to fail: the shim’s count case reports with 64 sandboxes already registered, POST /sandboxes answered 500 … it went on to build one rather than refusing on a count

2026-08-25P6-24 The trust boundary the threat model did not have is closed, and the table has the row now. C-1, H-1 and H-2 — the v1.0 gate. A workspace is not a mount: the host packs a directory into an ext4 image, the guest writes that filesystem, and the host walks it back on its own disk as the invoking user. Every name, every mode and every symlink target in it is chosen by the untrusted side, and debugfs -R "rdump / <tree>" joined those names to the destination — so an entry called ../../pwn. put the guest’s bytes two directories above the tree. Two layers, and the second exists because the first will one day be wrong. The image is enumerated and validated before anything is written, and refused whole and by name if one entry is unusable — refused rather than repaired, because a name that has to be repaired was built to be repaired and the repair is a guess about intent. Then the extraction writes through an os.Root: openat2 with RESOLVE_BENEATH and RESOLVE_NO_SYMLINKS, from the standard library, no new dependency. debugfs never chooses a destination again — it dumps into staging files this package numbers, and every guest-chosen name is used only through the root, so the kernel is what refuses rather than this code’s arithmetic. H-1 is the same principle at the other end. The supervisor is PID 1 and is not confined by the profile it applies to what it spawns, so write_file handed an agent /dev/vda and /dev/vdb — which profile.go’s own comment says it withholds, three lines above the list it is explaining. The tools now get exactly the reach the profile gives a confined child, so PID 1 stops being the way around the confinement it enforces. Reads are deliberately not confined, and the fixture that said they should be was withdrawn: the profile grants read beneath / to every confined child, so restricting reads would make the tool weaker than the thing it serves while closing nothing, and would break reading /etc/os-release. The mode rule was wrong first, and the acceptance suite is what said so. Stripping group-write turned every 0664 file in a umask-002 user’s project into 0644, so --review reported files the sandbox had never touched as modified and a sync-back quietly rewrote somebody’s own permissions. A boundary that rewrites the user’s files to protect them from the user is protecting nobody. World-write never survives, and neither do setuid, setgid or sticky; group-write is a permission the person already chose. the ledger is the evidence and it shrank from 21 lines to 13: every case for C-1, H-1 and H-2 came off, each in this commit, which is what the symmetric check demands
the full acceptance suite, re-run on the final code — 13 suites, 239 checks, 0 failed, and all 14 cookbook recipes. accept-e5 is the one that failed first and it is 33/33 now
TestRoundTripKeepsARealProject is the other half and the half a boundary fix most easily loses: nested directories, a dotfile directory, a name with spaces, a name that is not ASCII, a script that stays executable, a 0664 file that stays 0664, and symlinks of both shapes — fast (in-inode) and slow (in a data block), which debugfs reports two different ways
measured, on 2000 files in 101 directories: rdump 0.01 s, the validated path 0.075 s — enumeration is 0.006 s of it and the rest is the per-file copy through the root. Seven times the old cost and 75 ms in absolute terms
the boot and restore bars are untouched by construction, not by assumption: sandbox.New contains no reference to listImage, extractImage or Stage — every caller is at teardown (run.go:419, review.go:52, sessions.go:542, team.go:593). Re-earning the bars themselves on the bare-KVM reference is P6-18’s and needs that machine
the documentation rode this commit and not an earlier one: the trust-boundary table has the workspace device as a first-class guest→host path, and the README’s “Both layers exist now” says which layer was incomplete until v1.0 and what closed it
2026-08-25P6-25 The clamps a hostile agent can reach, and the ledger is down to one line. H-3, H-4, H-5, H-6 and M-9 — the rest of the v1.0-blocking set. Each is small; each was a denial of service on the host, driven from the untrusted side. M-9, under the description D46 corrected it to. Not “no total-output ceiling”, which ac8968f fixed before the audit was read, but the four ways a stream that never ends made sandbox.Exec never return. The timeout in its signature was a number mailed to the untrusted party — Connect clears the deadline it dialled with, so nothing on the host’s side ever stopped reading, while every other guest→host channel in the package sets one. It has a deadline now, at the command’s own budget plus a grace; the stream switch has the default the CLI’s copy always had, so the library serving other people is no longer the lax one; frames are counted as well as bytes, because empty frames advance a byte ceiling not at all; and proto.Reader.Read bounds the blank lines it skips, which sits below every channel rather than in any one of them. H-3 is a ceiling where there was only a floor, and it is a function rather than four lines inside Serve so the rule can be checked without waiting fifteen minutes to watch it work. H-4 is the store weighing what it spends. The byte ceiling counted values, so a key cost an agent nothing against the only budget there was. Keys are weighed with their values now, there is a count and a key length — and writing an empty value removes the key, which is the only way an agent has to make the store smaller. There was none: no delete, no op, no tool, so an agent that filled it could not give any of it back and nor could anybody else. The record says delete rather than put, because a reader should not have to infer which happened from a byte count. H-5 is the record keeping a digest where it was keeping a body. describe did not look at the outcome, so a refused message was written in full — and the digest beside it is why nothing is lost. H-6 is both halves, against the briefing. A ceiling of sixteen sandboxes, and a bearer token that can be required — unauthenticated stays the default, because that is the documented decision and the shim is a developer’s stand-in on a machine you already trust; what was missing was the choice. Compared in constant time, because a credential compared with == leaks its length. And the last thing the corpus found rather than the audit is fixed at its source: (*proto.Error).Error() renders through SafeText, so a guest can no longer write on the operator’s terminal. At the one place every path formats a guest’s error, rather than at each of six callers — a rule enforced in six places has six chances to be forgotten. the ledger is one line, and that line is a recorded decision rather than an open defect: the shim is unauthenticated by default on purpose, and now says so beside the token that changes it
the full acceptance suite, again on the final code: 13 suites, 239 checks, 0 failed, 14 cookbook recipes — these clamps sit on live paths and the suite is what says they did not narrow one
two fixtures were rewritten rather than satisfied, and both for the same reason: a ceiling of fifteen minutes and one of sixteen sandboxes cannot be observed by waiting for them, so the assertions moved to the rule and the counts now come from the constants rather than from numbers typed beside them
the clamps are bounded but not brittle: waitFor is checked at zero, at negative, at an overflowed millisecond count and at exactly MaxWait; the store's ceiling is a ceiling rather than a wall, with a case proving a removal makes room again
gofmt, vet, 14 packages, make docs clean; the guest-facing tool descriptions, docs/teams.md, docs/e2b-shim.md and docs/events.md state each new bound where the person who meets it will be reading
2026-08-25P6-26 Two claims the code did not keep. Neither blocks v1.0 and both come before the trivia, because the shape of them is what this phase exists to remove. M-2: fork applied no CPU quota, because it read no policy file at all. docs/resources.md describes cpu_quota as cgroup v2 cpu.max on the Firecracker process and carves out no exception for this command, so a reader would plan around a ceiling that was simply absent from every forked machine — and the way to get an uncapped machine was to ask from the side that was not looking. A cap that one entry point ignores is worse than one never claimed. It reads the file now, takes --policy and --cpu-quota under the same ceiling rule as everywhere else, and applies the quota per fork: the quota is what one machine may consume, and N forks are N machines. snapshot restore still reads no policy file, which is a separate gap and the page now says so rather than leaving the two to be assumed alike. M-5: an agent’s name reached the kernel command line unexamined, and the comment beside the line that writes it says the command line is “the one thing inside the guest that the guest did not write”. That is the reason the channel exists, and it held only while a name could not carry a space. Measured before anything was written: worker init=/bin/sh produced a line with two init= parameters, and w<tab>kelyfos.spawn=1 granted itself a spawn budget the host never gave it — which the report does not mention and which is a privilege escalation inside the team model rather than a curiosity about kernel arguments. Two layers again, and the second is where the rule is not enforced twice by accident. The topology refuses the name when the file is read, with the offending character named, because that is the check a person actually meets. bootArgs checks again, because the sentence it is protecting is load-bearing and one check is one thing to forget. The second layer drops rather than refuses, and it is the one place P6-24’s refuse-don’t-sanitise rule does not apply: by there the machine is being built and the caller has no error path worth taking, and a guest that does not know its own name is a smaller wrong than a guest somebody else configured. M-5 measured before the fix and pinned after it: the fixture asserts the count of init= parameters and that kelyfos.spawn= cannot appear from a name, and it asserts the ordinary case still reaches the guest — a guard that dropped every name would have stopped the team feature working quietly
the name rule is checked against what people actually use (master, worker-1, worker_2, api.v2) as well as against what they must not, because a rule that refused those would have made the check the problem
the full acceptance suite: 13 suites, 239 checks, 0 failed, 14 cookbook recipes — the team suites are what a name rule most risks and they are among them

2026-08-25P6-7 Signed exports, with a key the reader can anchor — a promotion of something that already worked rather than a substitute for it. kelyfos log --export --sign-key <key>, ed25519 from the standard library, no new dependency. The key is the user’s: PEM PKCS#8, which is what openssl genpkey -algorithm ed25519 writes, so a person needs no tool this project ships to make a key whose whole value is that it is theirs and not ours. A per-run ephemeral key is refused rather than offered, exactly as the task said: it proves one process made both halves, which the chain already proves. What is signed is the record and not the page. The chain head and a digest of the embedded events — the page carries a generation timestamp, so signing it would make two honest exports of one session disagree, and a reader comparing them would be looking at a difference that means nothing. A re-export produces the same signature, which is tested. The verifier reports a vocabulary rather than a verdict. The chain intact or broken, crossed with unsigned, signed by a key you named, or signed by a key only the file knows about. That last is the one stated carefully, and it is where a signature would otherwise become the badge P6-6 removed: a signature whose key came out of the same file proves only that whoever made the file had a key. --key checks against one the reader already holds, which is the only version of the question worth asking, and the page says so about itself in those words. An unsigned report is an ordinary report. The signature is optional by construction: an unsigned page carries no signature markup, and verify says so as a fact rather than a fault — “an unsigned report verifies, and the chain proves what it proves either way”. A record that does not verify is not signed at all, because a signature over a broken chain would be a statement about nothing that still reads as a statement. five behaviours checked end to end on a real microVM, in cookbook recipe 6: signed, checked against the key held separately, refused against a key that did not sign it, an edited signature caught, and an unsigned report still verifying
exit codes verified separately from the prose, because the first reading of them was a pipeline reporting tail’s status: right key 0, wrong key 1, key named with nothing signed 1, edited signature 1, edited record 1
TestASignatureSurvivesReExport is the property that decided what gets signed; TestASignatureIsAboutThisRecordAndThisKey checks a different record, a different head and a forged key claiming the same signature
a usability defect found by using it: kelyfos verify report.html --key k printed usage, because Go’s flag package stops at the first positional — and that is the order a person types, since the file is what the command is about. Flags are taken on either side of the path now

Nothing above the weakest provenance claim was reachable while a laptop was assembling this — an attestation over bytes a laptop produced attests to the laptop — which is why the task comes before P6-11 rather than with it. Drafted, never published outright. The last step before a stranger downloads anything is a person looking at it, and a workflow that published on a tag would take that person out of a loop they are the point of. SHA256SUMS was appended to by two targets and truncated by nothing. A release cut twice carried a file listing every artifact more than once, and nothing checked the file against the directory it described, so a stale line outlived the release that produced it. The key and the path carry the flavor now, in CI as well as here. What this is not is a provenance claim. It is the thing that makes one possible, and P6-11 is where the claim gets made. 2026-08-25P6-10 An SBOM per architecture, covering the seam rather than the convenient half. Three sources merged: Buildroot’s packages, the guest supervisor and the host CLI. The toolchain is a component too: a guest userland whose compiler is missing from the inventory has a hole exactly where a supply-chain question gets asked. The count is emitted from the document rather than transcribed, as the task asked, because a total written into a release note by hand is a total that is right once. 2026-08-25P6-12 The CLI ports to macOS, and doctor owns the Linux layer. The compile-level delta was seven lines behind two build-tag pairs, exactly as the task predicted — four SysProcAttr cgroup fields and three termios ioctl constants, which Linux calls TCGETS/TCSETS and darwin calls TIOCGETA/TIOCSETA. Both are now named constants rather than two implementations. The binary is a smaller program and it says so. Every command that needs a guest refuses with the way in rather than letting somebody discover a missing /dev/kvm. What works is doctor, verify, version and help — verify deliberately included, because the machine a report is sent to is often a Mac and checking one needs no guest, no daemon and no network. A signed report verifies natively there, and a page that lies about its own chain head is caught there. doctor provisions, starts, stops and reports on the layer, from a configuration embedded in the binary — because somebody who downloaded a release has no source tree to read a template from. On a Mac the report is the layer’s: its state, whether it matches this binary, then the in-VM doctor’s own output, which is where the checks that mean anything run. The old shape printed [FAIL] platform and told a Mac user to type limactl; both halves of that are now wrong. Drift detection, and the obvious version of it is a check that can only say yes. Comparing this binary’s config against Lima’s frozen copy reports drift for every instance including one made a second ago, because Lima normalises a template when it freezes it. So the digest of what an instance was made from is recorded beside it at creation, and a missing marker is reported as its own answer — “made by hand, so what it was made from is unknown” is a different thing to tell somebody than “made from something else”. P4-7’s “same commands everywhere” is withdrawn in the README, in the words the task asked for: it needs a transport across limactl shell, an interrupt does not cross it, and a Ctrl-C would orphan a microVM and silently discard the workspace it was syncing back. run natively on this Mac, not cross-compiled and hoped for: kelyfos run refuses with the layer instructions and exits 2; doctor reports the real kelyfos-dev instance as running and correctly says it was not created by kelyfos doctor, exit 1 — which is true, it was made by hand months ago
a signed report verified on macOS against a key held separately, and a page with an edited chain head refused there, exit 1
the same flags on Linux answer with one sentence rather than flag provided but not defined, because a tool whose flags depend on the platform is a tool somebody has to remember two versions of
the second copy of the truth is gated rather than hoped against: host/lima.yaml is compiled in and dev/lima.yaml is the one to edit, and a test fails loudly when they disagree and names the command that fixes it — proved by making them disagree

2026-08-25P6-13 kelyfos connect <client> — one command instead of a paragraph of JSON. Six writers, one per client, because D41 proved a shared template is impossible: VS Code’s key is servers and not mcpServers, Codex is TOML with mcp_servers in snake_case, and Claude Code’s user-scoped file holds a live sign-in session and is never touched. The four rulings the task asked for, made and recorded. A client that is not installed is a plain failure, not a catalog refusal — the catalog is for what KelyfOS decided to deny, and its IDs are part of the surface P6-14 freezes, so six client-specific IDs would freeze six third-party product names into a stable promise. The policy path is absolute, always, which settles F-D44 against recipe 9: the expansion matrix is asymmetric across all six and half of them would silently attach the wrong policy. A file that already holds other servers keeps them, along with every key this command has never heard of, because a configuration file belongs to the person whose machine it is. The write is redirectable by KELYFOS_CONNECT_HOME, since a test that wrote into a developer’s real ~/.claude.json is a test nobody dares run. --check starts the server the file names and completes a real handshake, and reports what that honestly proves — this server starts, here, now, and speaks MCP — as distinct from what it cannot: that the client will find the file, expand the same variables, or speak this era of the protocol. Dual-era from the first line: it probes initialize and treats an error as a live server on a revision that has removed it, which costs one round trip and needs no rewrite when the revision moves. Codex is written by line surgery rather than a TOML round-trip, deliberately: it is the user’s whole-product file, it may carry comments, and nothing in the standard library preserves them. Only this project’s own table is replaced. And the argument parser is now shared. kelyfos connect cursor --project . printed usage, because Go’s flag package stops at the first positional — the same defect P6-7 found in verify. Fixing it twice would have been two commands that disagree about their own arguments, so it is one function. every client asserted byte-for-byte rather than parsed: the absolute policy, the absolute binary, serve-mcp, and no trust field anywhere — which D41 makes binding, because it bypasses every tool-call confirmation and is exactly the field a later “make setup smoother” change would reach for
the shapes that differ, asserted as shapes: VS Code gets servers and not mcpServers, Codex gets snake_case TOML and not JSON
merge, idempotence and removal proved on a file holding another server and an unknown key — the second run is byte-identical, and --remove takes exactly one entry
a real handshake against a real server, in cookbook recipe 9b which CI runs: the file is written, the server it names starts, and it answers
a test pins that the generic-only clients have no writer, so a later “while we are here” cannot quietly add one D41 rejected on evidence
gofmt, vet, 14 packages, make docs clean; 15 recipes now
2026-08-25P6-14 The compatibility promise — the document that makes the number mean something. docs/compatibility.md. It is short because most of the work was already done: seven surfaces have a machine-readable source and a CI-enforced generated page, so the promise cites rather than re-lists and cannot go stale by the mechanism that already keeps the reference honest. What a version attaches to, stated for the first time. Three independent version constants exist and their relationship had never been written down. They are not in lockstep and never have been: every release has shipped proto.Version = 1 and recorder.Version = 1, and that is correct — the product changed a great deal and those two contracts did not. What does not stabilise is said as loudly as what does. Guest confinement profiles may narrow in a minor release, because a profile that could not be tightened without a major release is a profile nobody tightens. The E2B subset tracks somebody else’s API. And D41’s required clause: client configuration formats are external surfaces, outside the drift gate and outside this promise, re-verified on their own cadence. A deprecation mechanism, where there was none — announced, then warned on stderr (a warning on stdout corrupts a pipeline, which is the deprecation causing the outage it was meant to avoid), then removed in a major release, with a minimum of one minor release and thirty days. Security is the stated exception and says so rather than calling itself a patch. The three questions, settled. A new event type does not break a consumer, and one that breaks is wrong — §3 has always said to ignore the unrecognised. The MCP revision is not ours to promise: KelyfOS speaks one it has implemented and says which, rather than claiming the latest. And the record’s field order is frozen ABI, which nothing checked. A digest is computed over the struct in declaration order, so reordering the fields changes every digest KelyfOS has ever written — every chain retroactively unverifiable, which is tamper detection firing on legitimate records. TestTheEventFieldOrderIsFrozen now pins all 62 fields and fails with an explanation rather than a diff. the field-order test proved against a real reorder: swapping path and sha256 reports the record’s field order changed at position 28, and the message says what it costs rather than only what differs
it distinguishes the safe case from the fatal one in its own failure text: a field added at the end is safe and stays safe, because P6-6 made verification work from the bytes as written
a gate caught the document itself: TestEveryDocumentUnderDocsIsInTheLLMsSet failed because compatibility.md was not in the generated set — llms-full.txt would have called itself every documentation page while missing the one that says what the version means
gofmt, vet, 14 packages, make docs clean; llms-full.txt is now 498,747 bytes, about 129,599 tokens by gendocs’s own character estimate
2026-08-25P6-15 The E3-0 pattern re-run whole — the findings half. Every hand-written document read against the code that implements it, which is the only version of this audit worth running: read against each other, the documents agree, because agreeing with each other is not what they drifted from. The generated set is excluded by name — a hand edit there is reverted by the next make docs and turns the pipeline red, so a finding against a generated page is a finding against its generator. 21 documents, 202 candidates, and then a second pass whose instruction was to refute: quote the line, read the code, report the candidate as unreal if the document was right. 174 survived and 28 were refuted — a 14% false-positive rate on the first pass, which is the entire argument for the second one. 44 FALSE, 41 STALE, 54 IMPRECISE, 35 MISSING. The record is dev/docs-audit-2026-08-25.md, under dev/ rather than docs/ deliberately: it is a dated artefact of one audit, not a page anybody maintains, and the docs/*.md gate would otherwise pull it into llms-full.txt. 2026-08-25P6-15 The corrections — and the second pass that stopped fourteen of them being new defects. 157 of the 174 findings corrected across nineteen documents, one writer per document so no two touched the same file. Then every document was re-read by somebody whose instruction was to check the corrections against the source, not against the brief — and that pass found fourteen sentences that were newly false: a correction that overshoots is not a fix, it is the same defect pointing the other way, and it arrives wearing the authority of having just been checked. The pattern in all fourteen was the same — a true correction given a false scope. “The arguments are recorded by size” for three argument names out of all of them. “`ip`, `nft` and `jailer` are the three commands KelyfOS runs through sudo” when a fourth removes the jail directory. “--new-pid-ns is the one jailer flag KelyfOS does not pass” when two others are not passed either. “Delivered sixty-four times over, and the sixty-fifth is told unreachable” when sixty-four is a capacity and a dead agent’s unread messages already hold slots. Each replaced a vague overclaim with a precise one, which is worse, because a precise false statement is the kind a reader acts on. Two of the fourteen re-introduced the exact claim this task had just removed from the README — SECURITY.md and the Makefile both said release artifacts carry a provenance attestation, in a repository whose only tag predates the workflow that would make one. The cookbook said every head -N under dev/ and in the workflows was now sed -n '1,Np'; sixteen remained. 2026-08-25P6-17 The generated set regenerated after the documents settled, and the two commands that print its size stopped being able to disagree. The reference, llms.txt and llms-full.txt are current against a corpus that P6-15 corrected in nineteen documents and P6-16 grew by two; the set is 544,314 bytes, 541,658 characters, 10,844 lines, about 141,425 tokens, and make tokens is the committed invocation that says so. The command found a defect in its own first half. The measurement tool committed earlier in this phase carried a comment claiming its divisor “is the same constant tools/gendocs prints with, so the two cannot disagree about the same file”. It was not: they were two independent const charsPerToken = 3.83 declarations in two packages, holding the same value by coincidence and by nothing else. Repinning one would have left two commands reporting different sizes for one file, each correct by its own arithmetic — the second-copy-of-the-truth failure this project names everywhere else, inside the task whose entire subject is a number that can be reproduced. There is one constant now, in internal/docsize, imported by both, with the provenance of the ratio moved to sit beside it — the cl100k_base and o200k_base measurements from E3-2 and the E4 exit, which is what makes 3.83 a measured figure rather than a guess. A test pins the ratio and fails with the reason a change to it needs the provenance re-measured, and a second pins that the estimate counts characters rather than bytes, which for a corpus this full of em dashes is a 6% difference rather than a pedantic one. Still an estimate, and still says so. A token count is a property of a tokenizer, this project depends on none, and no tokenizer was installed to produce a better-looking number. KELYFOS_TOKENIZER remains the hook for anybody who has one, and the output labels measured and estimated separately rather than running them together. the refactor is provably cosmetic: make docs after it leaves the generated set byte-identical
both commands print 141,425 for the same file, now necessarily rather than coincidentally
llms.txt’s own stated size — “roughly 141k tokens” — is generated from the same constant, so the three cannot drift apart
