2026-08-25P6-28 Fourteen of the fifteen closed, and the fifteenth given a decision instead (D52). These are the cases where the document was right and the code was wrong — the useful half of an audit that reads one against the other. What a user gets back. kelyfos shell stops reporting exited 0 when the supervisor died mid-session, which was a false success a script would believe and which contradicted both docs/protocol.md §5.7 and proto.ShellExit’s own doc comment. A file.write event is no longer written from the request, so a write the guest refused stops appearing in the chain as one that happened, with a path, a size and a digest of content never stored — the worst thing a record can carry. pause stops storing a session and telling you to resume it when resume is guaranteed to refuse. A forked team member emits its resource.oom. scratch above mem is refused on all four entry points rather than two. And the SBOM attestation stops covering a binary no SBOM ever read. And the one that was hiding behind a CI timeout. The guest called POWER_OFF, which on x86_64 stops the guest and leaves the VMM running — Firecracker virtualises no power management there, and the only signal it watches is a reset on the emulated i8042 line. reboot=k was already on the kernel command line for exactly that. Every development machine here is arm64, where PSCI makes both calls work, which is how a headline feature shipped unable to stop a machine on the more common architecture. Two adversarial passes ran over the fixes and both paid for themselves, because three of them were worse than what they replaced. The argument summariser’s first fix left every non-content key on an unbounded json.Marshal: frames cap at 16 MiB, the recorder’s own readers cap a line at 8 MiB, so one call could write a chain line kelyfos verify can never read again. Its repair then spent one 120-byte budget across a whole array and cut a real eight-domain allowlist short — and the allowlist is recorded nowhere else. The mode fix stopped forcing u+rw and began stripping setgid from every extracted directory, silently regrouping a shared-group checkout; its repair left the same bug one layer down, in the walk over the person’s own .kelyfos-previous backup. And the shell fix put unbounded guest-chosen bytes into the signed record and rendered a guest string raw into kelyfos log, where an escape sequence rewrites the audit view as somebody reads it — a class this phase had already fixed once, with proto.SafeText sitting there for it. pause-and-resume on the x86_64 runner: 4 seconds, PASS, against a 23-second failure before — the fix proved on the architecture that had the defect
one test in this batch passed against a deliberately broken build until it was rewritten: its directory was removed rather than restored, so the path it claimed to check never ran. It now fails against the break and passes against the fix
fixing Commit to carry the person’s own setgid forward made docs/threat-model.md’s sentence about the workspace root true rather than needing it weakened

2026-08-25P6-19 The docs-only exam, and the trust defect it found in v1.0’s own new work. Three readers, E3-5’s bar: the binary built from the commit under test, llms-full.txt, and nothing else — no source tree, no docs/, no README, no plan files, no git history, no web. Tasks chosen against the surface this phase added rather than repeated for comparability: trust a report somebody sent you (on macOS, with no guest at all), attach a client and prove it works, and upgrade from v0.9. 24 findings survived adjudication of 28 reported; the other four were rejected because the documentation does say the thing, which is a finding about findability rather than truth and is recorded as one. The sharpest is a trust defect, and it is P6-7’s. The exported report prints the signing key’s fingerprint and tells the reader it “means something only if you recognise the fingerprint from somewhere other than this page” — which makes it the one value on the page a reader is instructed to act on. It was the one value kelyfos verify did not check. Swapping it for the fingerprint of a key the reader genuinely trusts left verification reporting a fully clean result, and deleting its marker passed where deleting any of the other three failed closed. Compounding it: the page prints sha256(key) and verify prints the raw key hex, with no document bridging the two — so a reader who wanted to cross-check by hand had no documented way to do that either. Fixed in this commit, with the mismatch worded apart from the others because a fingerprint comes from the signature and not from the record. Two more that let a reader overclaim. The cookbook’s own check.py — the documentation’s proof that a client configuration is right — passes against a serve-mcp running with no policy and no ceiling at all, which is the exact failure F-D44 exists to prevent. And the export’s summary card reads “0 FILES WRITTEN” for a session that demonstrably wrote a file, because a shell redirect inside the guest produces no file.write event — the card carries no hedge where the footer does. the transcript is committed whatever it says, per the task: docs/exam/2026-08-25-v1.0.md
the fingerprint fix is proved against the attack the exam used: a page naming a key it was not signed with, and a page with the marker deleted, both now exit 1 — and the test was run against the unfixed code first and watched to fail
the reader found it by fuzzing the page’s fields one at a time, which is how it should have been found and is not how it should have shipped
one reader did not finish its task, which is a result rather than a failure of the reader
2026-08-25P6-29 / P6-30 / P6-31 The three tasks the owner’s rulings created, done together because each is small and none is independent of the release. P6-29 — the DCO is gated where it can be true. tools/check-dco.sh checks the commits a push or a pull request adds, not the history: the requirement has stood since v0.1 and no commit before v1.0 carries a sign-off, so a check over git log would fail on every branch forever and be switched off within a day — and rewriting that history would invalidate every clone and every commit hash this file cites. Merge commits are exempt, because a merge carries no new work and GitHub’s own merge commits cannot be signed by a contributor. A base that is absent or all-zero — a branch’s first push, a scheduled run, a force-push — says so and passes rather than inventing a range. CONTRIBUTING.md states the gap in as many words, which is the “false until true” convention the owner asked for. P6-30 — Dependabot scoped rather than merely enabled. Two ecosystems, both consumed by this repository and neither able to reach a guest: gomod, and github-actions, which is the supply chain most likely to be attacked and least likely to be noticed. Deliberately absent: the guest toolchain. Buildroot, the kernel, Firecracker and Go are pinned with checksums in versions.mk, and a bot cannot know that a kernel bump needs an image rebuild, a boot measurement and a re-run of the acceptance suites — a PR doing that would look like every other PR while being none of them. And the rule D54 exists for is unchanged: the workflows that boot microVMs are dispatch-and-schedule only, which is what keeps any untrusted branch off a runner that starts VMs, and a bot-authored one is not a reason to widen it. P6-31 — Gatekeeper, documented where somebody meets it. The darwin binaries are unsigned, so a download through a browser is refused with “cannot be opened because the developer cannot be verified” — which reads like a corrupt download and is not one. The README says that, gives the clearing step, and puts the reminder to check SHA256SUMS first in the same breath, because that step asks a person to override the one thing macOS does on their behalf. Named in compatibility.md §7 too, where the promise’s limits live. the gate proved three ways in a throwaway repository: a signed commit passes, an unsigned one exits 1 with the fix lines, and a merge commit is exempt
proved against this repository’s own history too, which is the case it must not fail on by accident: tools/check-dco.sh HEAD~2 HEAD names both commits and exits 1
this row’s own commit is the first in the repository to carry a sign-off, which is what gating new commits means in practice
both YAML files parse; make docs clean
2026-08-25P6-20 The quickstart timed against the release it ships in, the first screen rewritten for a stranger, and the post brought to v1.0 — and the release candidate earned itself on its first run. rc1 failed. Nothing had ever caught it because no release had ever run this workflow — the SBOM was generated, merged, checksummed and published every time, and only the step that attests it failed. The serial is derived from the components rather than generated, because a random UUID per run is the obvious implementation and would have broken P6-9’s measurement in silence: two builds of one commit are byte-identical, and an SBOM that differs every time makes repro-check report a difference that means nothing. rc2 is green, twelve assets, drafted rather than published. 2026-08-25P6-27 The long tail: twelve confirmed findings fixed, two fixes reverted for being worse than what they fixed, and one defect found that none of the twelve described. What a user gets back. A trust-anchor refusal no longer leaks a running microVM at three sites. Reply claims its correlation tag before sending, so delivered means delivered. A declared agent can no longer be given a name the host would mint, which used to hand a spawned worker that agent’s entire edge set. Team workspaces resolve against the policy file like every other policy-relative path. secret.use is recorded when the credential goes on the wire rather than only when the round trip succeeds. The /30 allocator skips the cloud metadata range. And the jail write-back is atomic, rather than unlinking the host’s image and rewriting it where an interruption leaves nothing. The one nobody reported. D-1’s own work turned up a filesystem-independent sibling: resuming a paused session emptied the person’s project directory and printed “workspace written back”. Not a failed sync — it extracted nothing, renamed the project away, put an empty directory in its place, and reported success. The agent’s work was in neither directory. Restore points a jailed machine’s workspace image inside the run directory, ten lines under a comment saying the image must outlive the guest; cleanup() deletes that directory, and resume syncs after Shutdown returns because a defer registered first runs last. Reproduced before it was believed, fixed at the one moment the guest has stopped and the jail still exists, and the sync now refuses when it cannot read its source rather than treating a missing image as an empty workspace. Two fixes reverted, which is the other half of the task. The staging-tree reservation created <dir>.kelyfos-out at Stage time and required it to still be empty at commit — so anything landing in it during a review, a .DS_Store from opening the folder, turned a benign collision into total session loss with no image to recover from. And a new “two agents given the same workspace” refusal was scope that had been deliberately declined, masked the older duplicate-name refusal, and contradicted its own fix line. Both were strictly worse than what they closed, and both went back. three adversarial passes over the fixes, and each one paid for itself: an unbounded json.Marshal that could write a chain line the recorder’s own readers can never parse, a budget that cut a real eight-domain allowlist short, setgid stripped from every extracted directory, and guest-controlled escape sequences rendered raw into kelyfos log
the reverts are the rule every agent in the batch was given, applied to the batch: if it cannot be made safe, put it back
the two problems the reservation addressed — staging trees accumulating after a Ctrl-C, and a narrow rename race — stay open and are written down, because both are visible where what replaced them was silent

2026-08-27P7-6 The layout, once — internal/graph added. A pure function from agents, directed edges and the resources they touch to placed nodes and routed edges (Layout, returning a Placement), plus the transitive closure the reach view needs (TransitiveClosure, returning a Closure). One implementation, two backends: Terminal renders a Placement to a rune canvas with a legend, SVG scales the same Placement to pixel coordinates only — numbers, never markup, so nothing it returns needs escaping downstream.
Deterministic by construction. A shared normalize step sorts and validates before either public function computes anything, so Layout and TransitiveClosure always agree about which agents and resources exist. Order in the input slices never affects the output (TestNormalizeIsOrderIndependent, TestLayoutIsDeterministicAcrossInputOrder, TestClosureIsDeterministicAcrossInputOrder). A dangling edge or access reference is refused rather than dropped — dropping it would make a reach view understate what a team can reach, the one direction this must never fail silently in — while a self-loop edge is silently skipped, mirroring internal/team.NewTopology’s own precedent for the same case.
The reach view answers the task text’s own example. A hub with a bidirectional edge to N workers has no edge naming two workers together anywhere, and is not thereby isolation: TestStarTopologyIsNotIsolation asserts every worker reaches every other in exactly two hops through the hub. A second, quieter path is modelled too: a StoreKey resource one agent may write and another may read opens a directed reach edge with no declared team.edge at all (TestSharedStoreKeyOpensAnIndirectChannel); a Domain or Secret resource deliberately does not, because those are destinations outside the team rather than state shared between two agents inside it (TestDomainAndSecretAccessDoNotOpenAChannel).
Four golden fixtures under testdata/golden/ freeze Terminal’s rune output for a star with one resource of each kind, a pipeline, two disconnected components plus an isolated agent, and a single agent — hand-verified against what Layout’s placement and routing rules should produce before being committed as golden. A byte-driven fuzz target (FuzzLayoutNeverPanicsAndIsDeterministic) builds bounded random graphs from raw fuzz bytes over a small ID pool, so duplicate IDs and dangling references — the two cases normalize is written to catch — occur often rather than needing a lucky corpus; on every input it asserts Layout and TransitiveClosure never disagree about validity, never panic, and are byte-identical across a repeated call on the same Input.
No files shared with any other lane (D62): a new package, importing nothing from internal/recorder, internal/team or internal/config — a caller converts its own domain type into a graph.Input, which is what lets kelyfos team graph reading kelyfos.toml (P7-7) and the exported report reading a session’s fold (P7-1/P7-8) agree about a topology without a third copy of this computation existing anywhere. Adds no CLI command, flag, MCP tool or kelyfos.toml key, so §8 rule 9’s cookbook-recipe obligation does not apply this task, and make docs produced no diff (checked below) — the generated reference, llms.txt and llms-full.txt are unchanged, so no re-measurement was needed. Per the brief’s surface table this task gets light review (not one of RECORD/PROXY/BROKER/RENDER) but is still not self-ticked — the checkbox is left unchecked for the orchestrator’s review dispatch.		 commands run inside limactl shell kelyfos-dev, this worktree (branch phase7/lane3-layout off 4a57db1): gofmt -l ./internal/graph/ → clean; go vet ./internal/graph/... → clean; go test ./internal/graph/... -v → 34 tests + 4 golden-fixture subtests, all PASS; go test ./internal/graph/ -run=^$ -fuzz=FuzzLayoutNeverPanicsAndIsDeterministic -fuzztime=60s → “15846963 execs, 280 interesting (total 287), PASS”, 0 failures; make test (whole repo: go vet ./... + go test ./...) → every package ok or [no test files], including the new internal/graph; make vuln → “No vulnerabilities found.”; make docs → “gendocs: wrote 8 files”, git status --porcelain before and after shows no change to any tracked file, only the new untracked internal/graph/; python3 tools/check-plan.py → “PLAN.html is coherent: 99 tasks, 78 checked, 63 decisions” both before and after this row was added.
2026-08-27P7-6 Independent review response — two blocking findings fixed, two more done, one design note adopted. Reviewer hand-derived the golden fixtures independently, mutation-tested a scratch copy, and confirmed the fuzz target is load-bearing (deleting the one sort in Layout passed the unit suite but failed the fuzz target in under 10s). Verdict landed “not ready to tick”; the row above this one is left as written, per §8 rule 6 — this row corrects and extends it rather than editing it.
Correction: the prior row’s test count was wrong. It said “34 tests + 4 golden subtests”; the actual count at that commit was 36 Test* functions, of which three run subtests rather than one: TestCornerGlyphAllFourBends (4), TestGoldenTerminalLayout (4) and TestGoldenLayoutIsStableAcrossRuns (4) — the 4 golden fixtures are 8 subtests, not 4, because two different tests each run all 4 — plus one fuzz target with 7 seed subtests, for 19 subtests total, not 4. “Green is a number” only holds if the number is the one that was actually true.
Finding 1 (blocking) — the terminal renderer could draw an edge that does not exist and hide one that does. route bends at a grid point that can coincide with another node’s own cell, and Terminal stamps node glyphs over everything drawn so far, so a corner landing on a third node’s cell vanishes under its glyph. Reviewer proved a star (a↔b, a↔c, no b-c edge) and a chain (a↔b, b↔c, no a-c edge) render byte-identical Cells — the committed testdata/golden/star_with_resources.txt has exactly this defect on its worker row, a star reading as a chain, the precise “isolation that isn’t” case this task exists to make visible. Fixed with the reviewer’s cheaper option: Canvas gains an authoritative Edges []TerminalEdge field, a straight, order-preserving projection of the Placement’s own RoutedEdges, unaffected by anything Cells drew or failed to draw — the same relationship Legend already has to node identity, extended to edges. Cells is now documented as a sketch, never a source of truth, with the collision explained in Canvas’s own doc comment and flagged inline on the golden fixture that demonstrates it. TestDrawSegmentNeverOverwritesAGlyph’s comment, which had claimed this exact collision “never” happens, is corrected to say precisely what the function does and does not guard. New test TestCanvasEdgesAreAuthoritativeEvenWhenCellsCollide reproduces the reviewer’s star/chain pair, asserts the Cells collision is real (fails loudly if a future routing change stops reproducing it, rather than passing silently), and asserts Canvas.Edges names the right edges for each topology and no others.
Finding 2 (blocking) — determinism was only asserted on rendered Terminal strings, never on Placement or SVG. Reviewer mutated Layout’s return to rebuild Nodes via unordered map iteration (identical positions, nondeterministic slice order): all 36 tests, all 4 golden fixtures and 2.8M fuzz execs still passed, because every check went through Terminal(l).String(), which draws by position and does not care about slice order — while SVG maps l.Nodes/l.Edges index-for-index, so that mutation would have made P7-8’s exported report emit SVG elements in a different document order every run. Fixed: reflect.DeepEqual now compares the Placement structs directly (not just their rendered strings) in the fuzz target, TestGoldenLayoutIsStableAcrossRuns and TestLayoutIsDeterministicAcrossInputOrder; the fuzz target additionally compares SVG(l1, DefaultSVGOptions()) against SVG(l2, …), and upgrades its Closure comparison from string formatting to reflect.DeepEqual on Agents/Hops too, for the same reason.
Finding 3 (worth fixing) — a map iteration in reach.go was exposed to an output order, contradicting the package doc comment’s own claim. for key, ws := range writers made the order adjacency entries were appended in nondeterministic; harmless today because BFS distance is order-invariant, but real, and would have bitten the first caller that wanted to name which resource explained a reach path. Fixed: the same computation now walks the already-sorted n.resources instead of ranging the map, which (with n.access’s own sort) makes every per-resource writer/reader list arrive already ordered with nothing further to sort. The package doc comment’s claim is corrected to say precisely what is actually true: every map this package builds is sorted, or walked in an already-sorted order, before a result is read out of it — and the fixed bug is named inline as the reason that sentence is now exact rather than aspirational.
Finding 4 (worth fixing) — Terminal could panic on a hand-built Placement. Zero Width/Height caused makeslice: len out of range; a node position outside the declared Width/Height caused an index-out-of-range panic. No Placement Layout itself returns can trigger either (its Width/Height always agree with its Nodes), but both fields are exported and cornerGlyph’s own comment already anticipated a hand-built one. Fixed: Terminal now derives the canvas size from the actual maximum node and edge-path position in l, never from l.Width/l.Height directly; two new tests construct exactly the two panicking shapes and assert the node still renders at its real position.
Finding 5 (cheap, done) — a caller obligation the package doc comment left unstated. Per internal/team/store.go (“No rule mentions this key, so it belongs to the whole team.”), a StoreKey with no matching rule is open to the whole team by default; an Input built only from declared [[team.store.key]] rules would miss that any-to-any reach entirely and understate what the team can touch — exactly the failure direction the package doc comment already says must never happen. A paragraph now states the caller’s obligation to synthesize an Access for every agent on every such key, and Access’s own doc comment points to it.
Design note, adopted rather than deferred — Closure.SharedResources(a, b). Reviewer flagged that excluding Domain/Secret from Hops is right (neither is a host-mediated hop this package can bound), but that two agents sharing one often have a real out-of-band channel this product cannot see (a shared domain is a plausible dead drop; a shared secret is credential inheritance) that a reach matrix would otherwise read as silence. Implemented as a separate signal, not folded into Hops: SharedResources returns every resource — any Kind, read or write — both agents have an Access record for, sorted, symmetric, empty for a == b or an unknown agent. The package doc comment’s wording is softened from “do not thereby gain a path to each other” (true only of this product’s own machinery) to naming SharedResources as what the exclusion does not have to mean silence.
Not this task’s obligation, flagged so it is not lost: reviewer noted no fuzz seed corpus is committed under testdata/fuzz/ (seeds are in-code f.Add only, and nothing has ever failed to create one) and that Phase 7 acceptance criterion 9 wants a committed corpus for every fuzz target the phase touched — but assessed that as P7-13’s exit-checkpoint obligation rather than a gap in this task. Recorded here rather than assumed remembered.
Checkbox still left unchecked — orchestrator routes final sign-off.		 commands run inside limactl shell kelyfos-dev, same worktree/branch: gofmt -l ./internal/graph/ → clean; go vet ./internal/graph/... → clean; go test ./internal/graph/... -v → 41 Test* functions total, 3 of which run subtests (TestCornerGlyphAllFourBends 4, TestGoldenTerminalLayout 4, TestGoldenLayoutIsStableAcrossRuns 4, the last two both over the same 4 golden fixtures) plus 1 fuzz target with 7 seed subtests — 19 subtests altogether, 60 PASS lines counted total (grep -c '^--- PASS: Test' → 41, grep -c '^ --- PASS:' → 19), 0 failures; existing golden fixtures required no regeneration — Terminal’s size fix computes the identical canvas dimensions for any Layout-produced Placement, only a hand-built one differs; go test ./internal/graph/ -run=^$ -fuzz=FuzzLayoutNeverPanicsAndIsDeterministic -fuzztime=90s (second run, stacked on the prior session’s corpus) → “20793507 execs, 65 new interesting (total 363), PASS”, 0 failures — combined with the prior session’s 60s run, ~36.6M executions, 0 failures across both blocking-fix commits; find internal/graph/testdata -type f → only the 4 golden files, no fuzz failure corpus; make test (whole repo) → every package ok or [no test files]; make vuln → “No vulnerabilities found.”; make docs → “gendocs: wrote 8 files”, git status --porcelain shows no change to any file outside internal/graph/; python3 tools/check-plan.py → “PLAN.html is coherent: 99 tasks, 78 checked, 63 decisions” both before and after this row was added.

2026-08-27P7-4 Correction to the incidental FuzzScopeCovers finding in the row above: the finding is real and reproduces, but its prose description had the two fuzz arguments swapped. The coordinator could not reproduce “covers(prefix="/repos/", target="/repos//") approves” against current source (commit f5305d5) and asked for a clean re-verification rather than either side guessing — correctly: that exact ordering does not fail, and neither did a fresh, unseeded go test -fuzz=FuzzScopeCovers -fuzztime=15s rediscover any failure in the window given (expected: the earlier regression seed was deleted rather than committed, so a fresh run has to blindly rediscover the same needle rather than replaying it). The error: the original Progress Log row named the fuzz function's two string arguments backwards. FuzzScopeCovers's corpus format lists them in declaration order — f.Fuzz(func(t *testing.T, prefix, target string), and scope := Scope{Path: prefix} — so the corpus file's first line is prefix (the credential's bound scope, Scope.Path) and its second is target (the incoming request path being checked against it). The original corpus file, read verbatim before it was deleted, was: string("/repos//") then string("/repos/") — prefix = "/repos//" (double trailing slash), target = "/repos/" (single trailing slash). Converting that into prose, the two got transposed: the row said covers("/repos/", "/repos//"), i.e. prefix and target swapped. Re-verified deterministically, not just re-argued: a corpus file holding the two possible orderings was written by hand into internal/egress/testdata/fuzz/FuzzScopeCovers/ and each run against commit f5305d5 in isolation. prefix="/repos//", target="/repos/" (the actual original corpus) — FAIL, the identical message originally logged, word for word. prefix="/repos/", target="/repos//" (what the row's prose said) — PASS, exactly matching the coordinator's own trace (literalAndNormal correctly rejects that ordering as path_not_literal before covers() ever reaches the prefix check). Corrected description of the finding: a secret scope bound to path /repos// (as --secret NAME@host/repos// would write it) is judged by Scope.covers to cover a request whose path is /repos/ — but the Tomcat/Jetty matrix-parameter-strip simulation the fuzz property already checks resolves that same escaped path to /repos, which is not beneath the literal prefix /repos//; a real origin server applying that normalisation could route the request outside the bound scope while this package still believed it was covered. Same risk family as the path-scoping fix already in CHANGELOG.md's Unreleased section, this time a double-trailing-slash case that fix did not cover. Still not committed (unrelated to P7-4, and dev/fuzz.sh's own convention commits a failing input only with its fix); the hand-written reproduction files were removed after confirming both orderings, so internal/egress is clean again. Nothing else in the review-fixes commit (D66, the EffectivePorts copy fix, the schema.go/docs/networking.md wording fixes) is affected by this correction — it touches only the wording of the incidental finding. all run inside limactl shell kelyfos-dev, repo at f5305d5, clean tree before and after:

2026-08-27P7-1 One fold, not three. internal/digest (new package): Digest.Absorb folds one event into per-agent counters, per-pair message counts, per-domain egress, store activity, the session header and usage receipts, and an annotated Timeline entry — in exactly one place. Walk runs it once over a whole chain (internal/report's use); kelyfos watch calls Absorb live, once per event, off its own tail. Both host/watch.go's absorb() and internal/report's three loops (the main switch and buildLanes' two passes) are rewritten onto it and lose their own copies: buildLanes now takes a *digest.Digest instead of re-walking raw events, and watch.go dropped its own counter bookkeeping and a second base64 decode of command output. Two divergences the old duplication had introduced without anyone noticing are now explicit instead of forked a third time: internal/report's Summary.TeamRefused counts a refused message or spawn only; kelyfos watch's status line has always added a denied store access on top. Digest.TeamRefused() and Digest.AllRefusals() are that difference, named and tested, rather than left to re-diverge unnoticed a third time. A real regression caught before it landed: retaining Timeline unconditionally would have reintroduced exactly the unbounded per-session memory growth host/watch.go's own bound() helper exists to refuse — a long-running watch's command.start entries kept forever, each still accumulating its command's whole output, for a view that never even reads Timeline back. Fixed with Digest.KeepTimeline (false by default, which is what a zero-value Digest — what watchModel embeds by value — gets for free; New/Walk opt in for internal/report, which needs the whole record) and openCommands (freed the moment a command's own exit is absorbed, so it stays bounded by commands still running rather than by how many ever ran). go vet ./... clean; go test ./... → 18 packages ok (rest [no test files]), including host (17.9s, real KVM boots), internal/report, internal/digest

2026-08-27P7-1 Adversarial review of P7-1 found three blocking regressions the fold's own tests never reached, and one non-blocking one; all four fixed, plus two hygiene findings, in this pass. The review built both the pre-P7-1 and post-P7-1 kelyfos binaries and diffed 115 real session chains through both report renderers — 31 differed, every difference traced to one of these. Finding 1 (blocking): the flat and lane views had come to gate a command's whole exit line, error message included, on en.Code != nil — but a command can exit with no numeric code at all (a supervisor crash mid-exec; host/servemcptools.go's exec-failure path writes exactly this shape), and the old code always showed the line, defaulting the code to -1, regardless. Fixed with a new Entry.Exited flag, set once a command's exit is absorbed, independent of whether that exit carried a code — the flat and lane views now gate on Exited and default code := -1 inside it, exactly as before P7-1. Finding 2 (blocking): Entry.Output accumulated every command.output chunk onto its command's entry regardless of KeepTimeline, so a live kelyfos watch (KeepTimeline false, which never reads Output back) still grew that string without bound for any still-running, still-printing command — the same defect class KeepTimeline was added to close, left half-open in the one place that accumulates by volume rather than by event count. Fixed: the accumulation itself is now gated on KeepTimeline. Finding 3 (blocking): Domains, Store and Pairs mint one map entry per distinct host, store key or message pair and never free one — every key at least partly a guest's choice, every attempt recorded whether allowed or refused. A hostile session naming an unbounded number of distinct hosts or keys grew a long-running watcher's own heap linearly and forever. Fixed with MaxDistinctKeys (4096): past the cap a key already tracked keeps accumulating, a genuinely new one sets a *Truncated flag instead of minting another entry — bounded, and saying so, per the RENDER checklist's own words. Finding 4 (non-blocking): shownImage asserted “per agent” for any chain with a blank Image, including one that never carried a session.start at all. Fixed with Digest.SawSessionStart, set only by an agentless session.start; the fallback text now requires it. Two hygiene findings also fixed: digest_test.go's allEventTypes was a hand-kept slice whose comment claimed it read internal/recorder's own type list — it now actually does, via recorder.Types(), with a test pinning that it stays in sync; and FuzzAbsorbNeverPanics never generated a nil Code (finding 1's exact shape), so 6.5M prior fuzz execs could not have caught it — a codeIsNil fuzz parameter and a dedicated seed close that gap. An evidence nit in the row above is also reconciled here rather than edited there: that row's “8 events” was captured mid-teardown, before session.end/resource.summary were appended; a fresh export of the same kind of session after full teardown reads 10, matching what the review found on disk. every fix has a direct, non-fuzz regression test naming the finding it closes: TestExitWithNoCodeStillMarksExitedAndKeepsItsError and (rendered, both views) TestExitWithNoCodeStillShowsItsErrorEverywhere; TestOutputDoesNotAccumulateWithoutKeepTimeline (5000 chunks, 1 KiB each, Output stays 0 bytes unkept vs. accumulates correctly when kept); TestDistinctDomainsAreBoundedAndSayWhenTruncated and TestDistinctStoreKeysAreBoundedAndSayWhenTruncated (MaxDistinctKeys+100 distinct keys → capped at MaxDistinctKeys, *Truncated set, an already-tracked key still accumulates past the cap); TestSawSessionStartIsFalseWithoutOne and TestNoSessionStartMeansNoImageClaim; TestAllEventTypesTracksTheRecorderPackage
go vet ./... clean; go test ./... → 18 packages ok (internal/digest now 30 tests + FuzzAbsorbNeverPanics, internal/report now 17); every test from the prior row's pass still passes unmodified
go test -run='^$' -fuzz=FuzzAbsorbNeverPanics -fuzztime=15s → ~2.0M execs, 0 failures (now exercising the nil-Code path every iteration codeIsNil fuzzes true); the four internal/report fuzz targets re-run at 15s each, clean; make vuln → no vulnerabilities found; make docs → still no diff

2026-08-27P7-1 Re-review (189 real chains this time, up from 115) confirmed the three blocking fixes hold byte-identical, and found one sibling collection the cap pass missed. PeerOnly — the peer of a message or spawn that never generated an event of its own, minted for a grid view's benefit — sat one line above the now-capped Pairs in the same case block and did not get MaxDistinctKeys: peer() de-duplicated with a linear scan of d.PeerOnly before an unbounded append, so it was quadratic on top of being unbounded. Reachable with a guest-chosen key exactly like its four siblings: internal/team/broker.go writes a team.refused carrying whatever recipient name a guest sent, verbatim, for any name outside the team, so team_send in a loop to an invented name mints one PeerOnly entry per call, forever, on a collection host/watch.go never even reads back (grepped: the only reader is internal/report/report.go's buildLanes). Fixed the same way as its siblings — capped at MaxDistinctKeys with a PeerOnlyTruncated flag — and the de-duplication switched from the scan to a peerSeen set at the same time, closing the quadratic cost as well as the unboundedness; the promotion path in agent() (a peer that later acts becomes a real agent) now keeps peerSeen in sync when it splices PeerOnly. TestDistinctPeersAreBoundedAndSayWhenTruncated added, in the shape of the three tests already covering Domains/Store: MaxDistinctKeys+75 distinct team.refused peers → PeerOnly capped at MaxDistinctKeys, PeerOnlyTruncated set, a peer already tracked is not re-added or duplicated past the cap
quadratic-to-linear confirmed directly, mirroring the review's own methodology (fresh Digest, n distinct team.refused peers, wall-clock per n): n=10000 → 3.97 ms, n=20000 → 6.11 ms, n=40000 → 11.71 ms, n=80000 → 20.89 ms — each doubling of n roughly doubles the time (linear), against the review's measured quadratic growth on the pre-fix scan (91 ms / 233 ms / 946 ms at n=10000/20000/40000); past the cap the remaining insertions are O(1) rejections, so the absolute times are lower again on top of no longer being quadratic

2026-08-28P7-3 Review-fix round on the independent review of P7-3. Verdict was “not ready to tick”: the core work (all four fields, positions 20–23, clipping, the constructor, wiring, Group’s content-hash semantics) was confirmed correct by mutation testing and three real boots — including one the reviewer ran and this lane did not, two different fork templates producing two different Group values — but two structural gaps and three doc-accuracy items had to close first. F2 (structural, fixed) — nothing asserted team.topology gets written at all: one recorder.NewTeamTopology call in the whole tree (host/team.go’s raiseTeam) and zero tests referenced it; the reviewer deleted the line and the whole suite stayed green. New host/topology_wiring_test.go, team.topology’s counterpart to TestEverySessionStartSiteHasAMatchingSessionPolicy: TestEveryRaiseTeamCallerReachesTeamTopology asserts every function that calls raiseTeam (found by scanning the source, not a hardcoded list — today teamUp and toolTeamUp) transitively reaches recorder.NewTeamTopology; TestRaiseTeamWritesTeamTopologyExactlyOnce counts the literal call sites inside raiseTeam’s own body and requires exactly one, so a duplicate hidden in the per-agent loop or a spawn closure would fail even though it would still be “reachable.” reachesSessionPolicy generalised to reachesFunction (a target parameter) so both tests reuse the one transitive-call-graph walk rather than a second copy of it — the same structural instinct as F1’s reflection guard and this task’s own typeConstants refactor, applied a fourth time.
F3 (structural, fixed) — nested struct field order (inside EvAgent, EvStoreKey, and pre-existing for EvError/EvSecret) was completely unpinned: TestTheEventFieldOrderIsFrozen’s keysInOrder decodes every object-valued field as an opaque json.RawMessage and discards it, so no test ever looked inside one. The reviewer proved it by swapping EvAgent.Name and EvAgent.Sandbox and watching the whole suite stay green, despite that changing the hash digest of every team.topology event ever written. Fixed by extending TestTheEventFieldOrderIsFrozen with a second pass: a literal, hand-written nestedFieldOrder map (deliberately not derived from reflect.TypeOf(EvAgent{}) or similar — a reflection-derived expectation would reorder itself in lockstep with a reordered struct and could never catch the reordering it exists to catch, the same reasoning the top-level want slice already rests on) checked against each nested object or object-array field's own key order via keysInOrder itself, which turned out to already be fully generic over any JSON object and needed no change.
F4–F6 (doc accuracy, fixed) — docs/events.md’s agent prose named only session.start/session.end as the exceptions; team.topology added as the third. docs/teams.md §8’s “what the record contains” table gained a team.topology row — the one event that actually carries the roster, sandbox ids, expanded edges and store ACLs, previously omitted. internal/recorder/schema.go’s cpu_quota_percent doc string said “absent when the team has no shared cgroup”; wrong condition, proven by host/teamplan.go’s needsSlice() (a shared cgroup exists whenever any of the team, a per-agent or a per-spawn cpu_quota is set, not only the team-wide one) — corrected to “absent when [team.resources] cpu_quota is not set,” with the same fix mirrored into docs/events.md’s own team.topology row so the two do not disagree. docs/policy-record.md §6 carries the identical imprecision and is left as-is: it is P7-0’s original spec, framed by its own text as a changelog of what was decided rather than a live reference, and the review named schema.go specifically as what ships the wording into the generated, user-facing reference.
F7 (log correction) — the P7-3 row above describes an earlier FuzzAppendFieldValues failure as “an external signal mid-run with no crash recorded — an environment blip, not a code fault.” That explanation was wrong and is corrected here rather than edited there. The reviewer investigated and reproduced a real, OOM kill of the fuzz worker process three times — twice on this branch, once independently on main with the same shared corpus — always around 12s into baseline coverage gathering, with syslog showing Out of memory: Killed process ... recorder.test. It does not reproduce on a fresh fuzz corpus and is not caused by anything in the P7-3 diff (it reproduces identically on main); it is a real, reproducible problem with FuzzAppendFieldValues’s memory profile once the corpus this session grew is large (267 entries at the time of the original failure). Independently re-confirmed here: go clean -fuzzcache then go test -fuzz=FuzzAppendFieldValues -fuzztime=30s ./internal/recorder/ → PASS, 77547 execs, 0 crashers, corpus regrown to 84 entries; a second run at -fuzztime=45s against that same regrown-but-still-small corpus → PASS, 102164 execs, 0 crashers, corpus at 132 entries — both clean, corroborating “fresh/small corpus passes clean” directly rather than only citing the reviewer’s own numbers. The original row’s positive result (0 crashers) was and remains real; only its explanation of the earlier failure was wrong. The reviewer suggested this OOM issue is worth its own tracked task or decision row of its own (this branch has not been rebased onto main, where a comparable precedent for tracking a found-mid-phase defect this way already exists) — the orchestrator will consider opening one at merge time; not opened here. 2026-08-28P7-7 The terminal views. kelyfos team graph (new subcommand, host/teamgraph.go): loads kelyfos.toml via the same loadPolicyAt/ planTeam/checkTeamScratch path kelyfos team up runs before it boots anything — including checkTeamFileScope, so a file combining [team] with [[plugin]] or [[forward]] is refused here, by name and line, with nothing booted. kelyfos team ps --graph (new flag on teamPS) draws the identical picture for a running team, sourced from that team's own recorded team.topology and session.policy events rather than from the file or run/team.json (D59's own reasoning). section 10

Decision log

IDDateDecisionRationale
D12026-08-21 Build the guest image + single-host CLI; explicitly not a kernel, VMM, control plane, or hosted service. The only under-served niche in a crowded market; solo-sized; portable across runtimes.
D22026-08-21 Buildroot for v0.x; revisit a Nix flake in phase 4. Fastest path to first boot; reproducible-build story can come later with signing/SBOM.
D32026-08-21 Go for supervisor and CLI. Single static binaries, trivial cross-compilation, fast iteration. Revisit Rust only if binary size or startup cost becomes a measured problem.
D42026-08-21 "agos" is a working title. Collision check is a launch-gate task (P3-7), not a today problem.
D52026-08-22 License: Apache-2.0, with DCO sign-off required on all contributions from day one. Decided by John, 2026-08-22. KelyfOS is positioned as the portable guest image other runtimes adopt — AGPL would block commercial runtimes from embedding it and fight the distribution strategy. The EU-compliance thread sells services and signed artifacts, not code exclusivity, so it survives under Apache (the Talos / Red Hat model). Matches the ecosystem: Firecracker itself is Apache-2.0. DCO keeps a future relicensing move possible. Executed in P0-7.
D62026-08-22 TLS strategy: option (a), a TLS-terminating proxy with a per-install KelyfOS CA in the guest trust store — applied selectively. A domain is terminated only when a secret is bound to it with --secret NAME@domain; every other allowlisted domain gets a plain CONNECT tunnel with no MITM. Default posture is therefore "tunnel and log", and termination is opt-in per domain, chosen by the person who binds the credential. Decided by Claude Code at the P2-0 gate after prototyping; implemented in P2-5/P2-6 and documented as a trade-off in P3-5. Option (b) cannot deliver the product's headline guarantee. Section 1 and P2-6 both promise that the secret value never exists inside the guest. For the guest to authenticate over a TLS session it terminates itself, it must hold a bearer credential — and a short-lived token is still a secret inside the guest for its whole validity window, reachable by exactly the prompt-injected code the threat model exists to contain. Option (b) also needs tool cooperation (a git credential helper and friends), which is no help at all against arbitrary agent-written code, and arbitrary agent code is the entire point. Option (a) works here for a reason that would not hold on a general-purpose machine: KelyfOS owns the whole guest — the image, the trust store, and the default environment, which docs/protocol.md §5.2 already defines as replacing rather than merging. That makes SSL_CERT_FILE, CURL_CA_BUNDLE, REQUESTS_CA_BUNDLE, NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS and GIT_SSL_CAINFO settable authoritatively, so the usual defeater — Python's certifi and Node's bundled roots ignoring the system store — is solvable rather than fatal. Terminating selectively keeps the blast radius honest: the proxy only ever sees plaintext for domains the user deliberately handed a credential to, and connection-level logging of everything else is exactly what P2-4 and P3-8 ask for (egress attempts, not HTTP transcripts). Accepted costs, to be stated plainly in docs/threat-model.md: certificate pinning breaks for terminated domains, and the proxy becomes a trust boundary holding plaintext for them. Binding implementation requirements, added by John on 2026-08-22 when endorsing this decision: (1) the KelyfOS CA is generated fresh per run, lives only in host memory or tmpfs, is never persisted, and nothing but the trust anchor ever crosses into the guest — a restart means a new CA, so a leaked anchor is worthless past the session that made it; (2) the flight recorder must record terminated vs tunnelled per egress event, so a user can always prove exactly which traffic the proxy was able to read, and the certificate-pinning limitation for secret-bound domains is documented in docs/networking.md. Both are conditions on P2-5/P2-6, not separate decisions.
D72026-08-21 Monitoring = readers of the flight-recorder JSONL only: HTML session export (P3-8) and terminal TUI (P3-9, P4-6). No web dashboard — no server with auth, persistence, or a fleet view. A localhost read-only viewer is the absolute ceiling, and only on demonstrated community demand. A dashboard is a control plane — non-goal territory where funded competitors live. Litmus test for any monitoring feature: does it read the JSONL, or does it manage sandboxes? Readers are fine; managers are scope creep.
D82026-08-21 Daily-driver and trust features adopted: workspace block-device sync (P3-10), kelyfos doctor (P3-11), kelyfos.toml policy-as-code (P3-12), hash-chained audit (P2-4) with signed exports (P4-3), CI benchmark (P3-6). Phase 3 re-estimated to 5–7 weekends. Approved by John, 2026-08-21. Differentiators no competitor ships as a set; all stay inside the non-goals (single-host, readers not managers, policy in files not servers).
D92026-08-22 Dev machine = MacBook Pro M5 Max / 64 GB: macOS 15+ → Lima (vz, nested virtualization) → Linux → Firecracker. aarch64 is the primary architecture; x86_64 is cross-built and gated from Phase 1 (P1-8) and is the CI reference. Host support model = the Docker Desktop pattern (P4-7): one CLI everywhere, transparently managed Linux layer on macOS/Windows; Firecracker itself always runs on Linux/KVM. Approved by John, 2026-08-22. Fastest compile loop for the Buildroot/kernel-heavy phases; 64 GB enables the many-fork demo; aarch64-first covers Graviton and cheap EU ARM hosting; the early x86 gate prevents drift. Native macOS backend (libkrun) stays a backlog experiment, not v0.x scope.
D102026-08-22 Project named KelyfOS — κέλυφος (kélyfos), "shell". CLI binary kelyfos, config kelyfos.toml, TAP prefix kelyfos<id>. Supersedes the "agos" working title (D4). Chosen over KratOS: "kratos" is heavily occupied in adjacent open source (a major Go microservices framework whose CLI binary is literally kratos, the Ory Kratos identity server) plus a listed defense contractor — bad findability and a PATH collision for a Go CLI. KelyfOS has clean airspace and the metaphor is literal: a shell wrapped around the agent. Final availability sweep stays gated at launch (P3-7).
D112026-08-22 Version policy fixed at P0-6: Buildroot 2026.02.3 (the yearly .02 long-term-supported line, not the newer 2026.05.x), guest kernel 6.18.45 (newest longterm line on kernel.org, pinned to an exact patch), Firecracker v1.16.1, Go 1.27.0. Every pin carries its sha256 in versions.mk. Go is installed from the official tarball by dev/install-go.sh instead of the golang apt package, and the quickstart in section 7 is updated to match. Buildroot's LTS line is the right default for a project whose selling point is reproducible images: a year of fixes without feature churn. The kernel is pinned to a patch release because "latest 6.18" is a floating version by another name, which is exactly what this task exists to prevent; buildroot.org ships only a PGP-signed manifest, so the digest is transcribed into versions.mk where the build can check it. Go from apt is 1.22 on Ubuntu 24.04 — old enough that a go.mod toolchain directive fails outright, so the distribution package cannot be the pin. Overrides stay possible for bisecting upstream regressions: every value uses ?=.
D122026-08-22 Guest kernel configuration strategy for P1-2: the base is Firecracker's own reference guest config (resources/guest_configs/microvm-kernel-ci-<arch>-6.1.config @ v1.16.1), vendored into image/buildroot/kernel/ with attribution, and the KelyfOS delta rides on top as Buildroot kernel config fragments. Two supporting decisions: (1) the kernel tarball is checksum-verified by KelyfOS itself before Buildroot ever sees it, because Buildroot documents that it "checks hashes of all packages downloaded, except those for which a custom version is used" — and 6.18.45 is a custom version to Buildroot 2026.02.3, whose hash file lists only 6.19.14 and 6.18.33; (2) kernel headers are pinned to the kernel version (BR2_KERNEL_HEADERS_AS_KERNEL) instead of Buildroot's default 6.19.14. Starting from an arch defconfig would mean switching off two thousand drivers and a module system by hand and hoping none crept back; starting from Firecracker's config means starting where the VMM's own CI already proved a virtio-only, module-free kernel boots. It already carries ext4, tmpfs, overlayfs, devtmpfs + auto-mount, virtio blk/net/mmio, vsock, and # CONFIG_MODULES is not set. The 6.1 → 6.18 gap is absorbed by olddefconfig and every guarantee we care about is then re-asserted by our own fragment and verified in the resulting .config, so nothing rests on trust. Un-verified downloads would have made the versions.mk kernel pin decorative — a checksum that nothing checks. Pinning headers to the kernel keeps userland from being compiled against a kernel interface newer than the one it will run on.
D132026-08-22 AF_VSOCK in Go is wrapped by hand in internal/vsock — a small net.Conn and net.Listener over golang.org/x/sys/unix and os.File — rather than taking a vsock library such as mdlayher/vsock. The Go module is single and repo-wide, with the wire format in internal/proto shared by guest and host. Discovered by failing: net.FileListener and net.FileConn reject an AF_VSOCK descriptor outright ("address family not supported by protocol") because Go's net package only parses AF_INET, AF_INET6 and AF_UNIX addresses. Something has to bridge the gap. The whole bridge is about eighty lines, because os.NewFile on a non-blocking descriptor registers it with the runtime poller — so real deadlines and goroutine-friendly blocking come for free and only the two address methods and a poller-aware Accept are left to write. Vsock is the single interface this entire product is built on and it lands in PID 1 at P2-1; owning it outright is worth more than the eighty lines saved, and it keeps the guest's init free of third-party runtime code. One module for both sides means the host and guest cannot disagree about the wire format, because there is only one copy of it.
D142026-08-22 The guest kernel is trimmed well past Firecracker's reference config, and the cuts are a security posture as much as a boot-time one. Removed: all SCSI/iSCSI, XFS, HID and the entire input stack (with CONFIG_VT, since the console is a serial port), the extra I/O schedulers, the virtio devices KelyfOS never attaches (PCI transport, balloon, mem, pmem, console), loop devices, filesystem encryption, module-signature machinery, kernel audit, all network filesystems (NFS/SUNRPC/FUSE), all of netfilter, eBPF, magic-sysrq and profiling. CONFIG_EXPERT=y is enabled because several of these have no Kconfig prompt without it. Kept deliberately: IPv6, cgroups, namespaces, virtio-rng, the PL031 RTC, and DEBUG_KERNEL/SLUB_DEBUG. Every line removes code that this machine cannot reach: there is one virtio-blk disk, one virtio-net NIC, one vsock device and one 16550A UART, and a kernel that cannot load modules will never grow another. Netfilter is the pointed one — KelyfOS enforces egress policy on the host TAP (P2-5) precisely so the guest cannot participate in its own containment, and shipping the guest a firewall it might reconfigure would be arguing with the design. eBPF goes for the same reason a sandbox does not ship a debugger: it is large, privileged and historically exploitable, and seccomp filters (P4-2) are classic BPF and do not need it. DEBUG_KERNEL and SLUB_DEBUG stay because they are menu gates, and turning them off would take CONFIG_DEBUG_LIST — list-corruption detection, a hardening feature — with them. IPv6 stays until P2-5 can actually test egress with it gone; removing it on a guess would be the kind of change that fails three phases later.
D152026-08-22 Boot-time numbers measured under nested virtualisation are informational only. The Lima / vz dev machine does not define pass or fail for any timing claim. The binding measurements — the ≤ 500 ms Phase 1 target and the ≤ 300 ms cold-boot claim in the definition of done — are defined on the P3-6 reference environment: a bare-KVM x86_64 CI runner, measured by make bench the same way P1-7 measures locally (host timestamp to the first frame on the guest-initiated ready channel). P1-7's local median of 899 ms therefore records the dev-machine baseline and does not put the phase target in question. Further local boot optimisation stops here: IPv6 removal and init-path trimming are deferred and will be revisited only if the bare-KVM number demands it. Extended by John on 2026-08-22 to Phase 3's ≤ 100 ms restore target, which binds on the same bare-KVM reference environment and is measured the same way: make bench with n ≥ 10, median and p95 published in the CI job summary alongside cold boot. Local restore numbers are informational for the same reason local boot numbers are. Approved by John, 2026-08-22, on the P1-7 evidence: guest user-mode CPU is at parity with its host and syscalls are free, but virtio block I/O runs at ~77 MB/s and the kernel's 465 ms to userspace is spread across ~100 initcalls with no hotspot — the cost is in the virtualised device and MMIO path, where every L2 exit is serviced by Lima's hypervisor. Optimising against that number would be tuning for an artefact of the development environment, and risks trading real guest capability (IPv6, a readable init path) for milliseconds that do not exist on the hardware the claim is actually about. This is the risk table's own position — "Mac-local timings are informational; Linux CI publishes the reference numbers" — promoted from a note to a rule.
D162026-08-22 The guest gets no DNS at all. P2-5 as written says the nftables chain permits "only the host-side proxy port and DNS"; KelyfOS permits only the proxy port. There is no DNS responder on the TAP address, no nameserver in the guest's /etc/resolv.conf, and UDP/53 is dropped like everything else. Name resolution happens in the egress proxy, on the host, as part of deciding whether a connection is allowed. It falls out of the proxy design rather than being an extra restriction: a guest configured with HTTPS_PROXY does not resolve anything itself — it sends CONNECT github.com:443 to the proxy and the proxy resolves. So DNS in the guest is not load-bearing for any traffic KelyfOS intends to allow, and every rule that exists but is never needed is surface without benefit. Removing it also closes the oldest exfiltration channel there is: DNS tunnelling defeats a domain allowlist completely, because the data leaves inside the query names to a resolver that was explicitly permitted, and an allowlist checking hostnames cannot see it. A prompt-injected agent with UDP/53 to anywhere has a working covert channel no matter how careful the HTTP policy is. The cost is honest and worth stating in the threat model: anything that resolves before connecting — ping, raw sockets, a library that ignores proxy environment variables — fails rather than being silently allowed, which is the correct failure for a deny-all sandbox.
D172026-08-22 P3-3 (the dev flavor) is pulled forward to run before P2-6's verification and P2-7. It is a prerequisite for finishing Phase 2 as written, not a phase-3 convenience. The base flavor stays exactly as minimal as it is; nothing is added to it. Phase 2's acceptance test will be run against --image dev, and the plan's step 1 wording (--image base) is read as "a sandbox" rather than as a requirement that base carry tools it deliberately does not have. P3-3 stays in the Phase 3 list and will be ticked there; only its execution moves. Discovered by trying to run the acceptance test. base is BusyBox with CONFIG_TLS, CONFIG_SSL_CLIENT and CONFIG_FEATURE_WGET_HTTPS all unset — it has no HTTPS client of any kind. But Phase 2's acceptance test requires git clone https://github.com/… and an authenticated API call, and P2-7 requires cloning a repository, editing it and running its tests. Those are git, curl and python3, which the plan itself assigns to the dev flavor at P3-3. So the phase cannot be closed honestly without it: the alternative is either adding language runtimes to the flavor whose entire purpose is minimalism, or declaring an acceptance test passed on evidence that does not exist. Pulling one task forward is the smaller change, and the dependency is real rather than a matter of taste — an agent sandbox with egress and no TLS client cannot use the egress.
D182026-08-22 P2-7 is completed in two stages, both required: (1) protocol interop — the official MCP Python SDK 2.0.0 drives a full clone → edit → test workflow; (2) product dogfood — real Claude Code, attached through the .mcp.json in the README, does the same. Stage 1 is recorded; stage 2 is appended to the progress log as supplementary evidence. The checkbox stays ticked throughout — this is completing the proof, not reopening the task. Ruled by John, 2026-08-22. The SDK was the right instinct and is stronger interop evidence than validating my own client against my own server would have been — but the two prove different things. A strict SDK tolerates what a real agent product might fumble, and a real product exercises paths a conformance-minded SDK never touches: how it lists and describes tools to a model, how it handles a tool that returns isError, what it does with structuredContent, whether it copes with a server that appears only when a sandbox happens to be running. The plan named Claude Code deliberately, and "an MCP client worked" is not the same claim as "the product this exists to serve worked".
D192026-08-22 The P3-1 resync design is endorsed as written and must not be simplified later: mix host-supplied entropy into /dev/urandom first, attempt RNDADDENTROPY credit second and ignore its failure, set the wall clock with clock_settime(CLOCK_REALTIME). Restore is measured through the resync round trip rather than stopping at resume(). Endorsed by John, 2026-08-22. The ordering is load-bearing rather than stylistic. The plain write is what makes N forks of one snapshot diverge: without it every fork resumes with an identical random pool and generates the same session ids, nonces and temporary filenames — a correctness failure that looks like nothing until two forks collide. The RNDADDENTROPY credit is second and optional because it solves a different problem — a restored guest can otherwise block in getrandom(2) waiting for the kernel's entropy estimate to recover, which is how a 100 ms restore becomes a two-second one — and the guaranteed part must not depend on the optional part. Measuring through resync matters for the same reason: stopping at resume() reported 3 ms locally where the honest number, with the guest proven to be answering, is 130 ms. The flattering number was available and is not the one that gets published.
D202026-08-22 The README quickstart installs a prebuilt image from the GitHub release; building from source is the documented alternative, not the default path. The Phase 3 acceptance test requires a person following only the README to reach a working kelyfos exec in ≤ 5 minutes. The from-source path cannot satisfy that under any optimisation: make image compiles a cross toolchain, a kernel and a userland, and takes ~35 minutes on CI hardware. Either the criterion is dropped or the image ships prebuilt, and dropping it would mean the first thing a visitor does with KelyfOS is wait half an hour, which is the opposite of the product's whole claim. So the v0.3 release publishes vmlinux/Image + rootfs.ext4 per arch with a SHA256SUMS file, and make fetch-image (script dev/fetch-image.sh) downloads and verifies them into $(IMAGE_DIR). Artifacts ship gzipped: 141 MB of kernel + rootfs becomes 32 MB, which is most of the quickstart's wall clock. gzip rather than zstd (which would reach 21 MB) because gunzip is on every machine already and the quickstart must not require installing a decompressor. The sums cover the compressed files — that is what gets downloaded — and decompression happens only after they verify, into a temp dir, so a bad download cannot damage an image already in the cache. This is a distribution change, not an architecture change: the artifacts are byte-identical to what make image produces, the build path stays first-class and is what CI exercises on every commit, and the checksums are published so the download is verifiable. It also does not cross a non-goal — the artifacts are static files on a GitHub release, not a hosted service or a registry KelyfOS operates. Signed images and reproducible builds are P4-3; until then the checksum is integrity, not provenance, and dev/fetch-image.sh says so.
D212026-08-22 Every built or fetched image carries an image.json manifest, and the sandbox refuses to boot when the requested flavor does not match it. Found while preparing the v0.3 release: ImageDir() resolves to out/<arch> with no flavor component, so --image was a label that selected nothing. Build base, run --image dev, and the sandbox boots base while the flight recorder writes image: dev into session.start. That is not a cosmetic bug: the entire value proposition of the recorder is that it is the one account of the session the guest cannot influence, and here the host was writing a field it had never checked. An audit record that is confidently wrong is worse than one that is absent. Rejected the alternative of making the path flavor-scoped (out/<arch>/<flavor>/): it is the tidier model, but it invalidates every existing cache and snapshot workspace_path, and it still would not stop a hand-placed rootfs from being mislabelled. The manifest fixes the actual defect — the host asserting an unverified fact — and it earns its keep twice more: it is the provenance record the release artifacts needed anyway (arch, flavor, kernel and rootfs digests, pinned Buildroot/Linux versions, build date), and it lets fetch-image tell you what you just downloaded. make image and dev/fetch-image.sh both write it; a missing manifest is a hard error naming the fix rather than a silent fallback, because a silent fallback is how the original bug survived this long.
D222026-08-22 kelyfos snapshot restore re-creates the egress path and re-pairs the guest's NIC to a fresh TAP via Firecracker's network_overrides. Forks stay vsock-only exactly as P3-2 specifies. The Phase 3 acceptance test surfaced this: step 1 prepares a sandbox by cloning a repo inside it, which requires a NIC, and step 2 restores that snapshot. Restore had only ever been exercised on vsock-only machines, so it failed — and failed badly. With the parent still running, Firecracker reported Resource busy on the original TAP; with the parent gone, Operation not permitted, because the TAP no longer existed and nothing re-created it. Either way the user had already paid for a 512 MB snapshot that could never be loaded, and the error they saw was a raw Firecracker fault string about ifreq structures. P3-2 deliberately scopes forks to vsock-only, and that stands: N forks would need N distinct guest network identities, and the guest's address and default route are baked into the memory image, so genuinely separating them needs a network namespace per fork — which is P4-1 jailer territory. But P3-1 never scoped restore that way, and restore is the tractable case precisely because there is only one machine: it can re-use the original addressing. The mechanism was already available and simply unused — Firecracker's PUT /snapshot/load takes network_overrides (host device re-pairing per interface) alongside the vsock_override KelyfOS already passes. Restore now builds the egress path in the same order run does (TAP, proxy, firewall, then the machine), loads with the override, and re-installs the trust anchor over the control channel — necessary because D6 mints a fresh CA per run, so a restored guest is otherwise carrying an anchor for a CA that no longer exists. fork now refuses a networked snapshot with a message that says so, instead of emitting the same Firecracker fault four times.
D232026-08-22 kelyfos run [flags] -- <command> runs that command on the host for the lifetime of the sandbox, exports KELYFOS_SANDBOX to it, and exits with its status. Section 1's definition of done names this exact invocation — kelyfos run --image dev --workspace . --allow github.com --secret GITHUB_TOKEN@github.com -- <agent cmd> — and it did not exist: run took no positional arguments at all, so the only way to use a sandbox was run & followed by exec. Found while checking §1 line by line rather than assuming it was covered, since the PLAN-FEATURES activation gate depends on §1 being met. The bullet also settles what the command is: it says the agent “operates only through MCP tools on your project files”. An agent running inside the guest would not use MCP tools, it would just use the filesystem. So the trailing command runs on the host, reaching the sandbox over the MCP bridge — which is also the shape that makes the secret guarantee meaningful, since the value stays on the host side of the proxy and never enters the guest either way. sandbox.Load now falls back to $KELYFOS_SANDBOX when no --sandbox is given, so the child's kelyfos mcp and kelyfos exec attach to the right machine even when other sandboxes are running — previously an empty id meant “the only running one” and simply failed when there were several. Teardown, workspace sync-back and the flight recorder's session.end all run on the child's exit, so the sandbox's lifetime is exactly the command's.
D242026-08-23 CI build economics, settled as one change: a documentation-only change skips the build and boot jobs; Buildroot gains a compiler cache (BR2_CCACHE) persisted between runs and keyed on versions.mk, next to the download and per-arch tree caches that were already there; and a weekly scheduled run restores no cache at all. A from-source Buildroot run is tens of minutes per architecture, and this project’s own protocol asks for a commit per completed task — most of which touch nothing but PLAN.html, PLAN-FEATURES.html and STATUS.md. Paying an hour of runners to rebuild a kernel because a progress row was appended is waste, and slow feedback is the mechanism by which people stop reading CI. Three specifics worth recording because the obvious alternative is wrong in each case. The filter is a job-level if, not paths-ignore: a workflow skipped by paths-ignore never reports a status at all, which leaves a required check pending forever, whereas a job skipped by a conditional reports as skipped and satisfies it. checks stays unconditional regardless, so a documentation-only commit is still audited by tools/check-plan.py — the plan files are exactly the documentation most able to break. And the filter is four lines of git diff rather than a marketplace action: a third-party action in the path of every build is a supply-chain dependency bought for nothing. The ccache key has no restore-keys: Buildroot’s own help text makes purging the cache the user’s responsibility when the compiler changes, and versions.mk is what changes it, so a bump starts a clean cache instead of inheriting objects built by a toolchain that no longer exists. The download cache keeps its loose restore key, because those tarballs are checksum-verified on every use and a stale one is inert. The weekly run exists to make the caches falsifiable: a build that only succeeds because of what a cache is holding — a vanished upstream tarball, a package that no longer configures from scratch — is a build that works until the first person clones the repository. Seven days is the longest that can now stay hidden. cmake joins dev/install-build-deps.sh for ccache’s sake alone: without a host cmake ≥ 3.18 Buildroot quietly builds its own, spending ten minutes to save a few.
D252026-08-23 Parked, not taken: the move to Bubble Tea v2 and Lip Gloss v2. KelyfOS stays on bubbletea v1.3.10 and lipgloss v1.1.0 — the newest releases of the v1 lines — while bubbletea/v2 v2.0.9 and lipgloss/v2 v2.0.6 exist and are current. Revisit at E1-7, which is the next task to open host/watch.go. Recorded because a version that exists and is not taken should be a decision rather than an oversight. The two libraries are used by exactly one file, host/watch.go, and a major version is an API rewrite: taking it means rewriting the TUI, and rewriting the TUI on a dependency refresh — a change whose entire purpose is to be uneventful — mixes a mechanical upgrade with a real one. The v1 lines are not abandoned; both are the current release of their line and neither carries an advisory. E1-7 adds a resources lane to kelyfos watch and E2-8 rebuilds it as a multi-lane team view, so the file is going to be rewritten on purpose soon enough; that is when the v2 API should be adopted, if it is adopted at all, and the work will be paid for once instead of twice.
D262026-08-23 D25 is discharged: the Bubble Tea v2 / Lip Gloss v2 move is declined, not deferred again. KelyfOS stays on bubbletea v1.3.10 and lipgloss v1.1.0. The full reasoning is F-D23 in PLAN-FEATURES.html; this row exists so a reader of this file does not find D25’s condition still open and pointing at a task that has already happened. Two corrections of fact to D25 while it is being answered. The v1 pins are the newest release of their line (go list -m -u all offers no update for either), and govulncheck ./... is clean. And the v2 modules are not at the path D25 gives them: their go.mod files say charm.land/bubbletea/v2 and charm.land/lipgloss/v2, not github.com/charmbracelet/…/v2. D25 named its own revisit point — “E1-7… and E2-8 rebuilds it as a multi-lane team view; that is when the v2 API should be adopted, if it is adopted at all.” E1-7 passed without the question being asked, and E2-8 has now rebuilt the file. Leaving it open a third time would turn a decision into a habit, and log rows are immutable, so the only way to discharge a stale condition is to append the answer. The answer is that E2-8 needed nothing v2 has: the multi-lane view is fixed-width columns, a bounded ticker and a status line, and the one library call that carries weight — measuring a styled string in terminal columns — exists in both. Adopting a major version whose only argument is that it exists would be paying an API rewrite for nothing, in the middle of the task with the most reason to be about something else. That is D25’s own argument, applied to the moment D25 pointed at. The module-path correction matters more than it looks: taking v2 would mean depending on a vanity domain this project has never resolved, which is a supply-chain fact neither D25 nor F-D23 could weigh because both had it wrong. F-D23’s condition for revisiting stands and is better than a date: a v2 API this project actually needs, an advisory on a v1 pin, or the v1 line ceasing to receive releases.
